## GENERAL INTRODUCTION

In recent years the importance of combining agricultural productivity and [SOURCE: Page_6]

sustainability has risen to become one of the most important issues in agriculture. [SOURCE: Page_6]

Meanwhile, plant diseases continue to represent a major limiting factor in agricultural [SOURCE: Page_6]

production (Dordas, 2008).. The control of plant diseases using classical pesticides has raised [SOURCE: Page_6]

serious concerns about food safety, environmental quality and pesticide resistance, which [SOURCE: Page_6]

have dictated the need for alternative pest management techniques and their combined use in [SOURCE: Page_6]

the framework of integrated protection (Atkinson and McKinlay, 1997). One such alternative [SOURCE: Page_6]

could be a rational use of fertilization to reduce the susceptibility of a crop to its pathogens or [SOURCE: Page_6]

to improve the efficacy of different control methods. Mineral elements are routinely applied [SOURCE: Page_6]

to boost crop yields and improve over all plant health and quality. They also have marked [SOURCE: Page_6]

effects on numerous plant diseases, and in certain situations, their rational use can reduce [SOURCE: Page_6]

disease to an acceptable level, or at least to a level at which further control by other cultural [SOURCE: Page_6]

practices or conventional organic biocides is more successful and less expensive (Huber and [SOURCE: Page_6]

Watson, 1974) [SOURCE: Page_6]

Among all elements, nitrogen (N) is by far the most extensively reported mineral [SOURCE: Page_6]

nutrient affecting plant disease, it is also the nutrient element applied in the largest quantity [SOURCE: Page_6]

and the most frequently deficient in cultivated soils (Datnoff et al 2007). A commonly [SOURCE: Page_6]

accepted generality is that high N fertilization tends to increase disease (Huber and [SOURCE: Page_6]

Thompson, 2007); however, this generality fails to take into account the effects of the rate of [SOURCE: Page_6]

application, time of application, form of N, soil conditions and interaction with other [SOURCE: Page_6]

elements. [SOURCE: Page_6]

Botrytis cinerea, the causal agent of grey mould can infect more than 200 plant species [SOURCE: Page_6]

including tomato and lettuce which are economically important crops (Jarvis, 1992; [SOURCE: Page_6]

5 [SOURCE: Page_6]

Williamson et al., 2007). Due to its broad host range this pathogen can cause huge losses, [SOURCE: Page_7]

although the specific costs of damage are difficult to estimate (Dean et al., 2012). The fungus [SOURCE: Page_7]

is considered as a typical necrotroph, which co-opts programmed cell death pathways in the [SOURCE: Page_7]

host to achieve infection (van Baarlen et al., 2007) [SOURCE: Page_7]

Greenhouse tomatoes are susceptible to a wide variety of diseases and pests. Among [SOURCE: Page_7]

all diseases, grey mould is one of the most important (Bardin et al., 2008; Nicot and Baille, [SOURCE: Page_7]

1996). Infections of the pruning wounds by B. cinerea result in stem cankers that can rapidly [SOURCE: Page_7]

kill the plants (Decognet et al., 2010). On lettuce, a crop that is often grown in rotation with [SOURCE: Page_7]

tomato in unheated greenhouses, grey mould is also a common diseasewhich can reduce both [SOURCE: Page_7]

yield and quality Affected leaves are inedible and need to be removed at harvest. The worst [SOURCE: Page_7]

case is when the lettuce is attacked at the base and slimy, yellowish red-brown rot forms, [SOURCE: Page_7]

which can the kill the entire plant (Jarvis, 1992; Williamson et al., 2007). [SOURCE: Page_7]

On both crops, fungicides are widely used to control grey mould but due to limited [SOURCE: Page_7]

number of fungicides available and the possible emergence of resistant strains complicating [SOURCE: Page_7]

their use (Elad et al., 2004; Leroux, 2004), much research is dedicated to developing [SOURCE: Page_7]

alternative control methods. A possible alternative could be the use of biocontrol agents, . [SOURCE: Page_7]

Another possible alternative to reduce disease severity might be through the manipulation of [SOURCE: Page_7]

plant fertilization. Availability of nutrients is known to affect plant disease development [SOURCE: Page_7]

(Dordas, 2008; Walters and Bingham, 2007). Among common nutrients, nitrogen appeared to [SOURCE: Page_7]

have the greatest effect on plant susceptibility to plant pathogens (Huber and Thompson, [SOURCE: Page_7]

2007). In case of B. cinerea N fertilization of the host plant influences the susceptibility of [SOURCE: Page_7]

several crops against this pathogen (Dik and Wubben, 2004b; Yermiyahu et al., 2006). [SOURCE: Page_7]

Moreover, sporulation is very important for the reproduction and spread of B. cinerea, [SOURCE: Page_7]

as it plays a key role in the development of epidemics, as this pathogen can produce millions [SOURCE: Page_7]

of spores on diseases tissues within one week after inoculation especially in favorable [SOURCE: Page_7]

6 [SOURCE: Page_7]

conditions, that are easily disseminated to large areas and thus play a key role in epidemics of [SOURCE: Page_8]

grey mould (Nicot et al., 1996).        Suppression of B. cinerea sporulation is a valuable [SOURCE: Page_8]

component in Botrytis blight management in commercial greenhouses, because reduction in [SOURCE: Page_8]

sporulation may lead to retardation of an epidemic (Morandi et al., 2003). So there is need to [SOURCE: Page_8]

find a control measures how to reduce the sporulation of this fungus and N fertilization may [SOURCE: Page_8]

be used to overcome this problem. Several studies have shown that high N fertilization [SOURCE: Page_8]

increased the sporulation of B. cinerea on sweet basil (Yermiyahu et al., 2006). But little is [SOURCE: Page_8]

known of possible effect of N fertilization on sporulation of the B. cinerea on tomato diseases [SOURCE: Page_8]

tissues and on the pathogenicity of resulting secondary inoculums. [SOURCE: Page_8]

Biological control has gained increasing interest, but a lower and less efficacy as [SOURCE: Page_8]

compared to pesticides, is often cited as one of the disadvantage of biocontrol agents and their [SOURCE: Page_8]

wide use by growers (Nicot 2011). In order to enhance the biocontrol efficacy against fungal [SOURCE: Page_8]

pathogens certain strategies such as adding various compounds like calcium salts, [SOURCE: Page_8]

carbohydrates and amino acids were used to enhance the efficacy of biocontrol agents (El- [SOURCE: Page_8]

Ghaouth et al., 2002; Janisiewicz et al., 1992). In addition, In vitro conditions studies have [SOURCE: Page_8]

shown that the antagonistic activity of Trichoderma harzianum was stimulated against [SOURCE: Page_8]

Sclerotium rolfsii in the presence of three nitrogen sources (Khattabi et al., 2004). According [SOURCE: Page_8]

to our knowledge there are no studies on the possible role of N fertilization on the efficacy of [SOURCE: Page_8]

biocontrol agents against B. cinerea. [SOURCE: Page_8]

Objectives of the thesis work: [SOURCE: Page_8]

The different objectives of the present work are the following: [SOURCE: Page_8]

1: The first objective was to evaluate the effect of different levels of N fertilization on the [SOURCE: Page_8]

susceptibility of tomato plants to six strains of B. cinerea widely different in their [SOURCE: Page_8]

7 [SOURCE: Page_8]

aggressiveness and to test the influence of initial inoculum density and fungal strain on the [SOURCE: Page_9]

growth response of the fungus, both in vitro and in planta. [SOURCE: Page_9]

2: The 2nd objective was to evaluate the effect of different regimes of N fertilization on the [SOURCE: Page_9]

susceptibility of lettuce to the same six strains of B. cinerea which were tested before on the [SOURCE: Page_9]

tomato plants and to test whether the N fertilization effect on severity of disease was linked to [SOURCE: Page_9]

the plant N status or was related to the main plant sugars. What is the link between the [SOURCE: Page_9]

concentration of primary plant components at the time of infection and the severity of [SOURCE: Page_9]

disease? [SOURCE: Page_9]

3: The 3rd objective was focused on the sporulation of B. cinerea on diseased plants and the [SOURCE: Page_9]

aggressiveness of the secondary inoculum. The specific objectives were (1) to assess the [SOURCE: Page_9]

effects of N nutrition of tomato on sporulation of B. cinerea on diseased tissues and on the [SOURCE: Page_9]

pathogenicity of the resulting secondary inoculum, and (2) to correlate these effects with [SOURCE: Page_9]

modifications in plant tissue composition. [SOURCE: Page_9]

4: The 4th objective of the study was to investigate a possible effect of N fertilization on the [SOURCE: Page_9]

efficacy of two biocontrol fungi, Microdochium dimerum and Trichoderma atroviride, shown [SOURCE: Page_9]

in previous work to provide protection of tomato pruning wounds against B. cinerea. To [SOURCE: Page_9]

understand the mechanisms at cellular and histological level the final objective of this study [SOURCE: Page_9]

was (1) is there effect of N fertilization on the integrity of B. cinera mycelim? (2) N [SOURCE: Page_9]

fertilization has any effect on the mycelial structure of two BCA and ultimately on the [SOURCE: Page_9]

efficacy of biological control agents T. atroviride and M. dimerum through cyto-histological [SOURCE: Page_9]

techniques by means of light microscopy?. [SOURCE: Page_9]

### 1. Botrytis cinerea: causal agent of grey mould:

B. cinerea is one of the most important plant pathogenic fungi, being the responsible [SOURCE: Page_11]

of major crop losses on more than 200 plant species in temperate and subtropical regions [SOURCE: Page_11]

worldwide. Based on its scientific and economic importance B. cinerea the causal agent of [SOURCE: Page_11]

grey mould has been recently ranked as number 2 among the top 10 fungal pathogens in [SOURCE: Page_11]

molecular plant pathology (Dean et al., 2012). It is a ubiquitous fungus which causes grey [SOURCE: Page_11]

mould on many economically important crops including vegetables, ornamentals and fruits [SOURCE: Page_11]

(Jarvis, 1992; Williamson et al., 2007). It can attack many organs including leaves, stems and [SOURCE: Page_11]

fruits as a nectrotroph, often with heavy losses after harvest (Elad, 1997b). B. cinerea infects [SOURCE: Page_11]

stem wounds of greenhouse tomatoes and can cause serious economic losses (Eden et al., [SOURCE: Page_11]

1996),infections of the pruning wounds result in stem cankers that can rapidly kill the plants [SOURCE: Page_11]

(Decognet et al., 2010). In a study of 15 greenhouses in the south of France, (Nicot and [SOURCE: Page_11]

Baille, 1996) showed that the incidence of B. cinerea between May and June 1991 ranged [SOURCE: Page_11]

from 32-100% and plant mortality reached 46% in some greenhouses. In a larger survey [SOURCE: Page_11]

carried out in the same area in late April 1993 (Terrentroy, 1994) showed that B. cinerea [SOURCE: Page_11]

attacks were found in 58 of 73 tomato greenhouses studied and partial mortality of plants by [SOURCE: Page_11]

the fungus was recorded in 31 of these greenhouses. [SOURCE: Page_11]

This pathogen can cause the partial or total destruction of the host plant and in some [SOURCE: Page_11]

cases the whole crop. Economically, this fungus is considered such a major pest problem in [SOURCE: Page_11]

viticulture worldwide (Martinez et al., 2005). Estimated global losses due to B. cinerea on [SOURCE: Page_11]

alone grapevine to $ 2 billion per year (Elmer and Michailides, 2004a). In addition, the rapid [SOURCE: Page_11]

and insidious B. cinerea produces the annual destruction of crops on hundreds of hectares of [SOURCE: Page_11]

vines (Bolay and Pezet, 1987). Estimated losses for vineyards in France amount to 15-40% of [SOURCE: Page_11]

crops depending on weather conditions. In Champagne, infection rates can reach 15 to 25% [SOURCE: Page_11]

10 [SOURCE: Page_11]

depending on the year (Cilindre et al., 2007). In protected corps, for example, tomatoes, [SOURCE: Page_12]

peppers, lettuce and strawberries the risk of attack by this fungus is always standing on [SOURCE: Page_12]

(Jarvis, 1992). [SOURCE: Page_12]

1.1 Taxonomic position: [SOURCE: Page_12]

Botrytis has been recognized as a genra by Micheli in 1729 where he was listed in his book " [SOURCE: Page_12]

Nova Plantarum Genera." At the beginning it was sometimes confused with Sclerotinia spp. [SOURCE: Page_12]

but details were made by (Smith, 1900) and the confusion was resolved by (Whetzel, 1945) in [SOURCE: Page_12]

### 1945. The genra, redefined by (Hennebert, 1973) and includes 22 species, most of which

have a restricted host range, such as B. tulipae attacking on tulips, B. fabae on beans or B. [SOURCE: Page_12]

squamosa on onion (Hennebert, 1973). In contrast, B. cinerea is ubiquitous and there are [SOURCE: Page_12]

many species of plants on which it can cause serious damage before and after harvest. [SOURCE: Page_12]

Botrytis cinerea name was given in 1801 by Persoon a pathogen of the vine. This fungus like [SOURCE: Page_12]

many others experiencing double classification: [SOURCE: Page_12]

•   Perfect form (teleomorph) Botryotinia fuckeliana (Barry) Wetzel. This is an [SOURCE: Page_12]

Ascomycete, class of Discomycetes, the order of Leotiales and family of [SOURCE: Page_12]

Sclerotiniaceae. [SOURCE: Page_12]

•   An imperfect (anamorph), Botrytis cinerea Pers. This is a Deuteromycete the class of [SOURCE: Page_12]

Hyphomycetes, order of Moniliales and family of Moniliaceae. [SOURCE: Page_12]

It is de Bary (1866) which established a genetic relationship between Botrytis cinerea Pers., [SOURCE: Page_12]

asexual organism and Botryotinia fuckeliana originally called Peziza fuckeliana, sexual [SOURCE: Page_12]

organism. Drayton and Groves (1939) observed for the first time, in vitro, the formation of [SOURCE: Page_12]

apothecia by B. cinerea confirming the systematic relationship between the two forms of the [SOURCE: Page_12]

fungus. However, it is the name of B. cinerea, widely known by mycologists and plant [SOURCE: Page_12]

11 [SOURCE: Page_12]

pathologists was retained while generally the scientific name of a fungus is given by its sexual [SOURCE: Page_13]

form. [SOURCE: Page_13]

### 1.2. Life cycle of the pathogen:

During its life cycle, B. cinerea can produce mycelium, asexual spores (conidia), sexual [SOURCE: Page_13]

spores and sclerotia. The mycelium of B. cinerea includes articulated filaments, grayish or [SOURCE: Page_13]

olive-colored, cylindrical, sometimes vesicular at the central partition, whose diameter varies [SOURCE: Page_13]

considerably depending on the conditions for the development of hyphae (Faretra and [SOURCE: Page_13]

Antonacci, 1987; Faretra et al., 1988). When the mycelium is at the stage of fruit, it produces [SOURCE: Page_13]

clusters of gray conidiophores. Sometimes this method of multiplication may disappear and [SOURCE: Page_13]

give way to a white mycelial growth which corresponds to the elongation of hyphae slender, [SOURCE: Page_13]

hyaline that spread in the form of web (Beever and Weeds, 2004).. The mycelium can be [SOURCE: Page_13]

stored in plant debris from the previous crop. When conditions become favorable B. cinerea [SOURCE: Page_13]

grows to give conidia. The development of conidia is characterized by the production of [SOURCE: Page_13]

conidiophores erect in tufts that often extended, forming an intense gray chain. Their release [SOURCE: Page_13]

is favored by a humid climate, and then they are transported by wind, rain and insects (Holz et [SOURCE: Page_13]

al., 2004).   When conditions become unfavorable to the development of mycelium and [SOURCE: Page_13]

conidia sclerotia are formed. They consist of aggregated white mycelium (Coley-Smith et al., [SOURCE: Page_13]

1980).. As young get older, they become hard and black. They are composed of a thick [SOURCE: Page_13]

cortex of cells forming thin barrier pseudo-parenchymal cells and a large central medulla [SOURCE: Page_13]

composed of filamentous hyphae. In spring, the sclerotia germinate and produce mycelium or [SOURCE: Page_13]

conidia (Beever and Weeds, 2004; Delcan and Melgarejo, 2002; Faretra et al., 1988) [SOURCE: Page_13]

The fungus overwinters as sclerotia or as mycelium in plant debris and may be [SOURCE: Page_13]

seedborne as spores or mycelium in a few crops. Other crops may also serve as sources of the [SOURCE: Page_13]

pathogen and are likely to cross-infect. Conidia are airborne and may also be carried on the [SOURCE: Page_13]

surface of splashing rain drops (Williamson et al., 2007). In the field, spores landing on [SOURCE: Page_13]

12 [SOURCE: Page_13]

tomato plants germinate and produce an infection when free water from rain, dew, fog, or [SOURCE: Page_14]

irrigation occurs on the plant surface (Fig.1). Dying flowers are a favorable site for infection, [SOURCE: Page_14]

but infections can also result from direct contact with moist infested soil or plant debris. In [SOURCE: Page_14]

the greenhouse, stem lesions develop either by direct colonization of wounds or through [SOURCE: Page_14]

infected leaves. The presence of external nutrients, such as pollen grains in the infection [SOURCE: Page_14]

droplet, can markedly increase infection (Elad et al., 2004). [SOURCE: Page_14]

Figure^1: Life cycle of Botrytis cinerea on different crops (Agrios, 2005). [SOURCE: Page_14]

### 1.3. Pathogenicity and host range:

B. cinerea produces a range of cell-wall-degrading enzymes, toxins and other low-molecular- [SOURCE: Page_14]

weight compounds such as oxalic acid. This fungus secrete a variety of compounds among [SOURCE: Page_14]

which oxalic acid, peptidases, and a pool of toxic metabolites(Williamson et al., 2007). These [SOURCE: Page_14]

compounds allow the pathogen to modify the host redox status, perturb the host defence, alter [SOURCE: Page_14]

13 [SOURCE: Page_14]

the cell integrity and macerate the plant tissues (Alghisi and Favaron, 1995; Godoy et al., [SOURCE: Page_15]

1990; Riou et al., 1991). [SOURCE: Page_15]

B. cinerea has the vide host range of host plants, over 200 mainly dicotyledonous plant [SOURCE: Page_15]

species and can cause grey mould on different plant organs, including flowers, fruits, leaves, [SOURCE: Page_15]

shoots and soil storage organs (i.e. carrot, sweet potato), Vegetables (i.e. cabbage, lettuce, [SOURCE: Page_15]

broccoli, beans) and small fruit crops (grape, strawberry, raspberry, blackberry) are most [SOURCE: Page_15]

severely affected (Jarvis, 1992; Williamson et al., 2007). Culture of plants out-of-season in [SOURCE: Page_15]

heated or unheated greenhouses and under plastic tunnels used increasingly to supply fruits, [SOURCE: Page_15]

vegetables, herbs and flowers in northern latitudes greatly increases the risk of infection, [SOURCE: Page_15]

especially in tomato, lettuce, cucumber and sweet pepper (Jarvis, 1989). [SOURCE: Page_15]

### 1.4. Symptoms:

B. cinerea is responsible for a very wide range of symptoms.          The most characteristic [SOURCE: Page_15]

symptom is a grey-brown furry mould, which are masses of spores of the grey mould fungus, [SOURCE: Page_15]

covering the infected area. When shaken, clouds of spores are released from these infected [SOURCE: Page_15]

areas. On tomato, the infected areas can expand rapidly covering whole stems, leaves or [SOURCE: Page_15]

petals. Stem infections can girdle the whole stem and cause wilting of the plants above the [SOURCE: Page_15]

infected area (Fig. 2). On lettuce a quick-spreading grey mould appears on lettuce leaves, [SOURCE: Page_15]

which renders them inedible. If the fungus strikes at the base of the plant, it turns yellowish- [SOURCE: Page_15]

brown and becomes a slimy rot (Williamson et al., 2007). [SOURCE: Page_15]

14 [SOURCE: Page_15]

### 1.5. Factors affect the development of the pathogen:

There are so many factors that greatly influence the development of the B. cinerea during the [SOURCE: Page_16]

early stages of infection, the disease development and sporulation of the fungus (Elad and [SOURCE: Page_16]

Yunis, 1993; Yunis et al., 1990) For example, nutrient requirements, light, climatic factors [SOURCE: Page_16]

etc. [SOURCE: Page_16]

1.5.1. Nutrient requirements: Among other factors, nutrient requirement of the pathogen play a key role in the [SOURCE: Page_16]

development of the pathogen. Because of the lack of endogenous energy reserves, the conidia [SOURCE: Page_16]

of B. cinerea, require an exogenous source of nutrients to germinate (Yoder and Whalen, [SOURCE: Page_16]

1975).    According to (Blakeman, 1975), the presence of both carbon and nitrogen is [SOURCE: Page_16]

necessary. The presence of nutrients such as glucose and fructose promotes germination and [SOURCE: Page_16]

elongation of the germ filament and allow the elderly to regain their conidia germination [SOURCE: Page_16]

15 [SOURCE: Page_16]

(Clarck and Lorbeer, 1977).      Thus the addition of sucrose, maltose, lactose, mannose, [SOURCE: Page_17]

galactose or xylose stimulates the germination of conidia of B. cinerea (Shiraishi et al., 1970). [SOURCE: Page_17]

Spraying onion leaves with conidia of B. cinerea suspended in the water does not result in [SOURCE: Page_17]

injuries but the addition of a source of nutrients (sugars and minerals) leads to the germination [SOURCE: Page_17]

of spores and leads to the formation of lesions (Clarck and Lorbeer, 1977). The molecular [SOURCE: Page_17]

mechanisms of induction of germination by carbon sources were studied in detail by [SOURCE: Page_17]

(Doehlemann et al., 2005; Doehlemann et al., 2006) [SOURCE: Page_17]

Nutrients are also required for the infection process of the pathogen and can be [SOURCE: Page_17]

provided in the form of glucose, leaf extract of cabbage, PDA medium) or injury to the point [SOURCE: Page_17]

of inoculation. The concentration of nutrients influences the ability of B. cinerea to cause [SOURCE: Page_17]

rotting of tissues (Yoder and Whalen, 1975). Nutrients also play a key role in the sporulation [SOURCE: Page_17]

of the Botrytis. For example, (Maas and Powelson, 1972) demonstrated that B. convoluta [SOURCE: Page_17]

sporulate abundantly on glucose, sucrose, and fructose media as compared to maltose, [SOURCE: Page_17]

galactose and starch media, sporulation was also increased by the casein hydrolysate, [SOURCE: Page_17]

asparagine, ammonium tartrate, and ammonium sulfate, less profuse with glutamine and [SOURCE: Page_17]

potassium nitrate, and absent with glycine, urea and no nitrogen. [SOURCE: Page_17]

### 1.5.2. Effect of light:

Light is an important factor to consider for the control of B. cinerea in greenhouse [SOURCE: Page_17]

crop protection. Germination of conidia of B. cinerea occurs better in light as compared to [SOURCE: Page_17]

darkness, on the condition there is water and nutrients provided in sufficient quantity [SOURCE: Page_17]

(Blakeman, 1980). (Nicot et al., 1996) showed that the germination of spores of B. cinerea on [SOURCE: Page_17]

PDA medium has no difference when the spores were placed under a selective ultraviolet [SOURCE: Page_17]

filter film (UV) or when placed in a film that does not absorb UV. Sporulation of B. cinerea [SOURCE: Page_17]

is dependent on the quality of the light received and especially UV (Elad, 1997a; Nicot et al., [SOURCE: Page_17]

16 [SOURCE: Page_17]

1996; West et al., 2000). Spore production in a selective filtering ultraviolet film in a petri [SOURCE: Page_18]

dish represents 0.05% of the spores under a non-control film for several weeks after [SOURCE: Page_18]

inoculation. This demonstrates that the absence of UV inhibits sporulation rather than delay it [SOURCE: Page_18]

(Nicot et al., 1996). Finally, it appears that some isolates are able to sporulate in the dark and [SOURCE: Page_18]

are not affected by the quality of light (Dik and Wubben, 2004b). (Nicot et al., 1996) [SOURCE: Page_18]

estimate that more than 15 million spores are produced in 7 days on a tomato stem segment of [SOURCE: Page_18]

2 cm and that reduces sporulation in a radical way to wait only 6 to 18,000 spores in a [SOURCE: Page_18]

selective UV-filtering film. In greenhouse crops, the light filtering using a polyethylene film [SOURCE: Page_18]

of green or pink and can inhibit sporulation of B. cinerea from 35 to 75%, respectively (Elad, [SOURCE: Page_18]

1997a). The spread of symptoms associated with this pathogen could be effectively limited [SOURCE: Page_18]

by the effective management of light in protected crops, due to the reduction of spore [SOURCE: Page_18]

production (Sutton, 1983; Sutton and Peng, 1993). The survival of conidia in the air is also [SOURCE: Page_18]

influenced by the quality of light. [SOURCE: Page_18]

1.5.3 Climatic factors: [SOURCE: Page_18]

Environmental conditions, especially relative humidity and temperature play a key role [SOURCE: Page_18]

for plant infection by B. cinerea and development of the disease. B. cinerea can thrive under [SOURCE: Page_18]

a range of temperatures between 2 and 30oC (Elad and Yunis, 1993). The optimum [SOURCE: Page_18]

temperatures for the different growth phases are range from 12–30 oC. B. cinerea will [SOURCE: Page_18]

therefore always be a potential threat in greenhouse crops. Other studies have also evaluated [SOURCE: Page_18]

the temperature requirements for the spores of B. cinerea germination. (Jarvis, 1977) reported [SOURCE: Page_18]

that conidia of B. cinerea germinate to 100% at 20 °C, 15 oC and 5 oC with 100% humidity. [SOURCE: Page_18]

A 95% relative humidity, only 80% of conidia of B. cinerea germinate at 15 oC and 5 o C, however^100% of the spores germinate at 20 oC. At 90% relative humidity, 85% of [SOURCE: Page_18]

conidia germinated at 20 oC, and germination is stopped when the relative humidity and [SOURCE: Page_18]

17 [SOURCE: Page_18]

temperature are lower (Jarvis, 1977). Under conditions of relative humidity of about 80 to [SOURCE: Page_19]

100%, the incidence of the disease is more severe on cucumber in dry condition (Yunis et al., [SOURCE: Page_19]

1990). The aerial mycelium and sporulation develop a more rapid at 21 oC, 94% relative [SOURCE: Page_19]

humidity (Thomas and Marois, 1986). An area of open water or high relative humidity (95%) [SOURCE: Page_19]

even seems necessary for conidial germination and penetration of germinal filaments, and the [SOURCE: Page_19]

success of the infection (Williamson et al., 1995). According to (O'Neill et al., 1997), the [SOURCE: Page_19]

sporulation phase is favored by high relative humidity and the interruption of these conditions [SOURCE: Page_19]

causes a delay in sporulation. [SOURCE: Page_19]

### 2.1. Cultural control

Cultural methods that ensure ventilation and drying of plant canopy after rain, whilst [SOURCE: Page_19]

maintaining adequate water supply to the roots, are the most effective means developed so far [SOURCE: Page_19]

for prevention of Botrytis epidemics (Elad and Shtienberg, 1995).             Increasing public [SOURCE: Page_19]

awareness of some potential drawbacks of chemical fungicides was addressed by the [SOURCE: Page_19]

development of alternative control measures making use of microbial antagonists that are [SOURCE: Page_19]

capable of disease suppression (Dubos, 1992). Cultural measures can be a powerful means to [SOURCE: Page_19]

suppress plant diseases in greenhouses where the value of crops is high and the farmers make [SOURCE: Page_19]

considerable efforts during long cropping seasons. Such measures are usually aimed at [SOURCE: Page_19]

altering the microclimate in the canopy and around susceptible plant organs, prevention of [SOURCE: Page_19]

inoculums entrance into the greenhouse and its build up and rendering the host plants less [SOURCE: Page_19]

susceptible to diseases.     Calcium loading of plant tissues and alteration of nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_19]

fertilization reduce susceptibility to Botrytis (Elad and Shtienberg, 1995a). Cultivars resistant [SOURCE: Page_19]

to B. cinerea are not available. In straw berry only the combination of a one-row-system, leaf [SOURCE: Page_19]

18 [SOURCE: Page_19]

sanitation and fruit sanitation decreased almost half gray mould damage in the first crop year [SOURCE: Page_20]

compared to a two-row-system without leaf and fruit sanitation. B. cinerea damage correlated [SOURCE: Page_20]

significantly and positively with the biomass of plants (Schmid et al., 2005). [SOURCE: Page_20]

### 2.2. Chemical control

The most common means for disease management is by application of chemical [SOURCE: Page_20]

fungicides. Both spraying of fungicides and application of fungicides directly to sporulating [SOURCE: Page_20]

wounds is practiced. However, high activity of several fungicides is being lost, at least in [SOURCE: Page_20]

part, due to the development of resistance. As fungicides still remain an important tool for [SOURCE: Page_20]

control of epidemics caused by B. cinerea, it is important to monitor populations of the [SOURCE: Page_20]

pathogen for their resistance towards potential fungicides (Elad and Shtienberg, 1995a) [SOURCE: Page_20]

Chemical control remains the main way to reduce the incidence of grey mould and other [SOURCE: Page_20]

Botrytis diseases on major crops. The use of botryticides is an efficient way to protect crops [SOURCE: Page_20]

against Botrytis spp. The most common interventions consist of spraying aerial parts of plants [SOURCE: Page_20]

with fungicides. The applied doses vary from 2000-3000 g/ha (e.g. maneb, thiram, [SOURCE: Page_20]

dichlofluanid) to 400-500 g/ha (e.g. carbendazim, fludioxonil, pyrimethanil). The number of [SOURCE: Page_20]

treatments during a season ranges from one or two, to more than twenty. [SOURCE: Page_20]

### 2.3. Biological control

Biocontrol of Botrytis-incited diseases has been extensively investigated over the last [SOURCE: Page_20]

50 years. Biocontrol of Botrytis-incited diseases with filamentous fungi, bacteria and yeasts [SOURCE: Page_20]

has been intensively studied over the last two decades (Blakeman, 1993; Elad and Shtienberg, [SOURCE: Page_20]

1995b; Tronsmo, 1991). Biocontrol offers an attractive alternative or supplement to the use [SOURCE: Page_20]

of conventional methods for disease control since microbial biocontrol agents (BCAs) are [SOURCE: Page_20]

perceived to be less demanding to the environment and their generally complex mode of [SOURCE: Page_20]

action reduces the risk of resistance development. For example Ulocladium atrum showed a [SOURCE: Page_20]

19 [SOURCE: Page_20]

real potential to protect stem wounds from B. cinerea. The level of protection provided by the [SOURCE: Page_21]

BCA was as good as that provided by the fungicide both in heated glasshouse and in unheated [SOURCE: Page_21]

plastic tunnels (Fruit and Nicot, 1999). In 16 greenhouse trials conducted in southern France, [SOURCE: Page_21]

an antagonistic strain of Fusarium sp. significantly reduced the incidence of stem lesions [SOURCE: Page_21]

caused by B. cinerea on tomatoes (Decognet et al., 1999). [SOURCE: Page_21]

The efficacy of combined biological control against B. cinerea, it is concluded that M. [SOURCE: Page_21]

dimerum, L. lecanii and R. sachalinensis extract are compatible for application on tomatoes [SOURCE: Page_21]

(Bardin et al., 2004). Yeast isolates consistently reduced incidence of disease and sporulation [SOURCE: Page_21]

of B. cinerea in tomato.      Several isolates reduced disease by more than 75% in all [SOURCE: Page_21]

experiments (Dik et al., 1999) U. atrum was most effective in suppressing Botrytis spp. [SOURCE: Page_21]

Colonization fruit rot of strawberries was significantly reduced by weekly applications of U. [SOURCE: Page_21]

atrum in 3 out of 4 field experiments. The antagonist was as effective as fungicide treatments [SOURCE: Page_21]

(Kohl and Fokkema, 1998). U. atrum is a strong competitor on necrotic above-ground plant [SOURCE: Page_21]

tissues. In onion leaf spot the potential of the antagonist to reduce colonisation of necrotic [SOURCE: Page_21]

leaf tissue by Botrytis spp. and subsequent sporulation was studied in two field experiments. [SOURCE: Page_21]

U. atrum colonised necrotic tissues and consistently reduced the sporulation of fungal [SOURCE: Page_21]

competitors.(Kohl et al., 2003) [SOURCE: Page_21]

The effectiveness of Trichoderma harzianum in suppression of tomato stem rot caused [SOURCE: Page_21]

by B. cinerea was examined on tomato stem pieces and on whole plants, ten days after [SOURCE: Page_21]

inoculation with B. cinerea and T. harzianum, the incidence of infected stem pieces was [SOURCE: Page_21]

reduced by 62–84%, the severity of infection by 68–71% and the intensity of sporulation by [SOURCE: Page_21]

87%. Seventeen days after inoculation of wounds on whole plants, the incidence of stem rot [SOURCE: Page_21]

was reduced by 50 and 33% at 15 and 26 °C, respectively, and the incidence of rot at leaf scar [SOURCE: Page_21]

sites on the main stem was reduced by 60 and 50%, respectively. Simultaneous inoculation [SOURCE: Page_21]

and pre-inoculation with T. harzianum gave good control of B. cinerea (50 and 90%) disease [SOURCE: Page_21]

20 [SOURCE: Page_21]

reduction, 10 days after inoculation (O'Neill et al., 1996). Trichosporon sp. controlled grey [SOURCE: Page_22]

mould and blue mould of apple fruits more effectively than C. albidus. Apple fruits treated [SOURCE: Page_22]

with Trichosporon sp. and C. albidus had a lower incidence of grey mould rot than blue [SOURCE: Page_22]

mould rot in the same storage conditions. Biocontrol efficacy of the yeasts for controlling [SOURCE: Page_22]

gray mould and blue mould was better in apples than in pears (Tian et al., 2002a). [SOURCE: Page_22]

### 3. Effect of nutrition on the susceptibility of plants to Pathogens

Fungal pathogens are developing strategies to parasite their hosts to collect various [SOURCE: Page_23]

nutritional resources needed for their growth. A traditionally distinction is biotrophic fungi, [SOURCE: Page_23]

which thrive in the host plant without causing cell death through structures called haustoria, [SOURCE: Page_23]

and the necrotrophic fungi, they secret the enzymes and toxins designed primarily to cause [SOURCE: Page_23]

necrosis and collect resources from dead cells. The availability of minerals and mineral [SOURCE: Page_23]

nutrition of the plant resulting act at three levels in the host-pathogen relationship: [SOURCE: Page_23]

1      The nutrients have an effect on growth and development of the plant, and thus how it [SOURCE: Page_23]

interacts with its environment and that the pathogen; [SOURCE: Page_23]

2      The plant nutrition affects the distribution, quantitative and qualitative, molecules that [SOURCE: Page_23]

can be used by the pathogen in planta; [SOURCE: Page_23]

3      The mineral nutrition of the plant modulates its ability to implement defence [SOURCE: Page_23]

mechanisms, whether constitutive or induced [SOURCE: Page_23]

Pathogenic fungi are implementing a wide variety of mechanisms for infecting their hosts. It [SOURCE: Page_23]

follows from the complexity of these interactions that influence the nutrition of a host on the [SOURCE: Page_23]

development of the parasite that attacks is very variable. Thus, increasing the nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_23]

content of tomato causes increased severity of infection by Oidium lycopersicum, but has no [SOURCE: Page_23]

effect on Fusarium wilt caused by Fusarium oxysporum, while it decreases susceptibility to B. [SOURCE: Page_23]

cinerea (Hoffland et al., 1999b; Hoffland et al., 2000a).          However, general trends are [SOURCE: Page_23]

suggested in the literature. For example, assume that the susceptibility of plants to rusts of [SOURCE: Page_23]

cereals, mildews, and many other foliar pathogens tends to increase with the increase in the [SOURCE: Page_23]

level of nitrogen nutrition (Huber and Thompson, 2007; Walters and Bingham, 2007). It is [SOURCE: Page_23]

also considered that, generally a good availability of potassium; calcium and magnesium [SOURCE: Page_23]

22 [SOURCE: Page_23]

limits the severity of epidemics. But understanding the exact role of nutrition in the host- [SOURCE: Page_24]

pathogen interaction and therefore the use of nutrition as a way of limiting parasite pressure [SOURCE: Page_24]

must be done by considering each specific pathosystem. We propose here a review of [SOURCE: Page_24]

literature of major nutrition influences to plant known in host-pathogen relationships and [SOURCE: Page_24]

focusing the study on nitrogen. In a second step, we examine more specifically the sensitivity [SOURCE: Page_24]

of B. cinerea to the nutritional status of various plants that may infect. [SOURCE: Page_24]

3.1    Effect of nitrogen nutrition on the host pathogen relationship [SOURCE: Page_24]

Nitrogen is an essential element for plant growth and a key element of agricultural [SOURCE: Page_24]

inputs. Rapid increases of crop yields became possible when synthetic N fertilizer became [SOURCE: Page_24]

available after the discovery of the Haber–Bosch process in the early 20th century (Smil, [SOURCE: Page_24]

1999). However, increased use of N fertilizers has also led to increased N losses from agro [SOURCE: Page_24]

ecosystems, especially since the 1950s. Distribution of fertilizer N across the globe is very [SOURCE: Page_24]

uneven. In some areas N is used excessively and leads to N pollution, causing a lot of [SOURCE: Page_24]

problems for human and ecological health. Other parts of the world suffer from reduced soil [SOURCE: Page_24]

fertility, diminished crop production, and other consequences of inadequate N supply (Mosier [SOURCE: Page_24]

et al., 2004). We can consider the effect of plant nutrition on host-pathogen relationships, the [SOURCE: Page_24]

pathogen life cycle and interactions that promote infection, disease development in the host, [SOURCE: Page_24]

and secondary spread of the pathogen and their interaction that facilitate infection. [SOURCE: Page_24]

3.2    Relationship between the form of nitrogen and host susceptibility [SOURCE: Page_24]

A wide range of plant hosts and their pathogens are influenced by N, but the form of N [SOURCE: Page_24]

available to the host plant or the pathogen may greatly influence the disease reaction, [SOURCE: Page_24]

independently of the rate of N available, for example disease susceptibility in strawberry was [SOURCE: Page_24]

affected by both N-concentration and N-source. B. cinerea lesions were largest in ammonium [SOURCE: Page_24]

nitrate > ammonium sulphate > calcium nitrate. This data suggest that calcium nitrate may be [SOURCE: Page_24]

a suitable source of nitrogen, helping growers to reduce disease risk (Walter et al., 2008a). In [SOURCE: Page_24]

23 [SOURCE: Page_24]

wheat crop when compared with the nil N treatment, ammonium nitrogen fertilizers, either as [SOURCE: Page_25]

ammonium sulphate or ammonium chloride drilled with the seed, lowered the severity of [SOURCE: Page_25]

take-all of wheat (Brennan, 1992).        Availability or failure to act, on both growth and [SOURCE: Page_25]

organogenesis nitrogen is the main growth factor element in the plants. An increased nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_25]

supply has a strong impact on leaf area and foliage density. Ammonia may have fungicidal [SOURCE: Page_25]

effects on V. dahliae and some Fusarium (Davis et al., 1976; Tenuta and Lazarovits, 2002). [SOURCE: Page_25]

The use of organic nitrogen base may also improve the competition between certain fungi and [SOURCE: Page_25]

micro-organisms antagonistic to pathogens in favor of the latter, and thus limit disease [SOURCE: Page_25]

outbreaks (Van Bruggen, 1995). In cases where ammonium has an unfavorable effect on the [SOURCE: Page_25]

development of a disease, the use of nitrification inhibitors may be a way to limit the risk of [SOURCE: Page_25]

infection while continuing to provide the host plant with sufficient nitrogen (Huber and [SOURCE: Page_25]

Thompson, 2007). [SOURCE: Page_25]

Many authors have suggested that further development of the canopy could induce a [SOURCE: Page_25]

favorable microclimate in infectious process (Champeil et al., 2004; Danial and Parlevliet, [SOURCE: Page_25]

1995; Leitch and Jenkins, 1995; Oerke and Schonbeck, 1990). This hypothesis is supported [SOURCE: Page_25]

by the fact that a target size of leaves or fruit may limit the deleterious effect of nitrogen on an [SOURCE: Page_25]

outbreak of B. cinerea (R'Houma et al., 1998b). Proposed contradictions about the effect of [SOURCE: Page_25]

nitrogen on the development of certain diseases can be overcome by considering the nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_25]

supply in the form of ammonia or nitrate (Huber and Watson, 1974; Huber and Thompson, [SOURCE: Page_25]

2007). The germination and penetration into the host can be promoted or inhibited by the [SOURCE: Page_25]

presence of leaf or root exudates from the host. For example, root exudates promote the [SOURCE: Page_25]

germination of Fusarium solani f. chlamidospores sp. phaseoli, and the quantity and chemical [SOURCE: Page_25]

composition of these exudates may be influenced by the type of nitrogen nutrition (Huber and [SOURCE: Page_25]

Watson, 1974). [SOURCE: Page_25]

3.3    Biological interactions between nutrition and soil microorganisms. [SOURCE: Page_26]

A nitrogen supply often leads to a significant limitation of the severity of epidemics. [SOURCE: Page_26]

Diseases of the cortex and roots, caused by Fusarium, Rhizoctonia, Aphanomyces, [SOURCE: Page_26]

Cercospora, and Armillaria are limited by the nitrate (Huber and Watson, 1974). As in most [SOURCE: Page_26]

agricultural soils, because of active nitrification by soil bacteria, ammonia is almost [SOURCE: Page_26]

completely converted to nitrate; nitrogen fertilization reduces the risk of developing infection. [SOURCE: Page_26]

On the other hand, when a pathogen attacks early on root systems, better supply of nitrogen in [SOURCE: Page_26]

the plant allows it to stimulate his organogenesis to create alternative structures and continue [SOURCE: Page_26]

to keep its hydro power mineral while activating jointly metabolic defence mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_26]

(Walters and Ayres, 1980; Walters and Ayres, 1981). Generally, it has often been reported [SOURCE: Page_26]

that the transition from one level of sub-optimal nitrogen sufficient has limited outbreaks in [SOURCE: Page_26]

cereals, for example Pyrenophora tritici ,Gaeumannomyces graminis, or Septoria, nodorum [SOURCE: Page_26]

caused by Stagonosphora.       Similarly in tomato, reduced availability of nitrogen promotes [SOURCE: Page_26]

blight caused by Alternaria solani and damping-off caused by Pythium spp infections [SOURCE: Page_26]

(Snoeijers et al., 2000b). Often, the kinds of nutritional effects of above reported are only [SOURCE: Page_26]

partial, and can not seems to cause the severe epidemics, For example, the previously reported [SOURCE: Page_26]

25 [SOURCE: Page_26]

(Elmer and Ferrandino, 1994). [SOURCE: Page_27]

3.4     Trophic host-pathogen interactions [SOURCE: Page_27]

Nitrogen is found in many forms in the plant, in elementary oxidized forms (NO$_3$-) or [SOURCE: Page_27]

reduced (NO$_2$-, NH$_4$ +) or incorporated after reduction with carbon skeletons to form amino [SOURCE: Page_27]

acids or amides or protein.       The distribution of different forms of nitrogen is not [SOURCE: Page_27]

homogeneous in the plant and pathogens find different sources of nitrogen, according to their [SOURCE: Page_27]

characteristics (surface or underground, biotrophic or necrotrophic).(Walters and Bingham, [SOURCE: Page_27]

2007)    Many correlations between susceptibility or resistance to diseases and various [SOURCE: Page_27]

indicators of nitrogen status of the plant (amino acids, nucleic acids, phenols, total N, protein [SOURCE: Page_27]

content, C / N, etc) have been reported (Huber and Thompson, 2007). [SOURCE: Page_27]

The successful colonization of a pathogen is related to its ability to draw resources [SOURCE: Page_27]

from the plant. Several authors support the hypothesis that biotrophic fungi, which have [SOURCE: Page_27]

limited access to nitrogen resources, are more sensitive to nitrogen nutrition than [SOURCE: Page_27]

necrotrophic, who have access to the entire cellular content of the plant.            But some [SOURCE: Page_27]

necrotrophic, fungus-like non obligatory fungus Magnaporthe grisea, the pathogen of rice [SOURCE: Page_27]

blast, can be stimulated by excess nitrogen (Kurschner et al., 1992; Long et al., 2000). The [SOURCE: Page_27]

example of M. grisea in the same way refuses the hypothesis supported by (Dordas, 2008), [SOURCE: Page_27]

that the obligatory parasites are favoured by excess nitrogen, while facultative parasites have [SOURCE: Page_27]

increased severity at low level N. In fact, it seems that one can generalize the rule that [SOURCE: Page_27]

biotrophic are favoured by nitrate and inhibited by ammonium, while in contrast the [SOURCE: Page_27]

necrotrophic are favoured by nitrate and inhibited by ammonium(Solomon et al., 2003). The [SOURCE: Page_27]

means employed by the pathogen for appropriate resources of the host is subject to questions [SOURCE: Page_27]

and controversies (Solomon et al., 2003). [SOURCE: Page_27]

26 [SOURCE: Page_27]

The fact that the expression of genes essential for pathogenicity is induced in vitro by [SOURCE: Page_28]

reduced concentrations of N and this expression is controlled by regulatory genes of nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_28]

metabolism inducing ability to acquire nitrogen from secondary sources, has led some authors [SOURCE: Page_28]

to the hypothesis that fungi in planta, find limited nitrogen concentrations(Talbot et al., [SOURCE: Page_28]

1997b; Thomma et al., 2006).       In addition, the expression of many genes involved in [SOURCE: Page_28]

pathogenicity is modified by the nitrogen status of the host (Snoeijers et al., 2000b). [SOURCE: Page_28]

However, the cellular concentrations of nitrate and amino acids in the host are well above [SOURCE: Page_28]

those required for proper growth of pathogenic fungi in vitro (Solomon et al., 2003). Studies [SOURCE: Page_28]

of the tomato apoplast during infection by Cladosporium fulvum showed the presence of [SOURCE: Page_28]

many amino acids, and proteins at high nitrate concentration (Solomon and Oliver, 2001)). [SOURCE: Page_28]

The contradiction between the studies could be explained by the fact that some [SOURCE: Page_28]

biotrophic in the early phase of infection find poor-nitrogen environments and are forced to [SOURCE: Page_28]

activate the secondary nitrogen metabolic pathways (Divon and Fluhr, 2007). A significant [SOURCE: Page_28]

portion of nitrogen resources of C. fulvum could derive amino acids from the host apoplast [SOURCE: Page_28]

(Solomon et al., 2003). This is the fact that fungal genes encoding transporters of amino acids [SOURCE: Page_28]

are activated during infection (Mendgen et al., 2000; Solomon et al., 2003) Some non-protein [SOURCE: Page_28]

amino acids like GABA or ornithine are involved in defence mechanisms of the plant and [SOURCE: Page_28]

accumulate in infected tissues. Pathogenic fungi would therefore use these host responses to [SOURCE: Page_28]

these molecules affect their own defensive metabolism (Divon and Fluhr, 2007). Mutants of [SOURCE: Page_28]

non-users nitrate have a full pathogenicity (Lau and Hamer, 1996) which reinforces the [SOURCE: Page_28]

hypothesis that amino acids are the main source of nitrogen supply of pathogenic fungi. [SOURCE: Page_28]

However, some mutant auxotrophic for methionine or arginine show a reduced or no [SOURCE: Page_28]

pathogenicity (Divon and Fluhr, 2007) (Solomon et al., 2003), which proves that the synthesis [SOURCE: Page_28]

of certain amino acids by the pathogen is necessary for infection The type of plant nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_28]

nutrition, nitrate or ammonia, has an influence on its amino acid content. Increase of the [SOURCE: Page_28]

27 [SOURCE: Page_28]

nitrate content of the plant increase the proline content and promote Fusarium in tomato and [SOURCE: Page_29]

ammonium increased concentration of asparagine, which promotes the development of [SOURCE: Page_29]

Rhizoctonia solani in beet (Huber and Watson, 1974) The increased severity of epidemics of [SOURCE: Page_29]

rust and mildew on cereals where abundant nitrogen supply is explained by the higher content [SOURCE: Page_29]

of amino acids into leaf tissue (Huber and Thompson, 2007). [SOURCE: Page_29]

3.5     Effect of nitrogen nutrition on the capacity of host defence [SOURCE: Page_29]

The plant is implementing four major types of defence following infection by a [SOURCE: Page_29]

pathogen: [SOURCE: Page_29]

(I)     a hypersensitive reaction leading to an oxidative burst, characterized by the synthesis [SOURCE: Page_29]

of compounds such as H2O$_2$ near sites of infection that cause cell death; [SOURCE: Page_29]

(II)    The establishment of structural barriers in the cell walls, particularly through the [SOURCE: Page_29]

production of callose and lignin, preventing the fungus to grow in the plant; [SOURCE: Page_29]

(III)   The synthesis of secondary metabolites with antifungal properties. These compounds [SOURCE: Page_29]

may be constitutive (Phytoanticipins), or synthesized following new infection (Phytoalexins) [SOURCE: Page_29]

IV)     The synthesis of proteins to detoxify fungal toxins or alter the metabolism of the [SOURCE: Page_29]

pathogen PR proteins (Pathogenesis Related Proteins). The availability of nitrogen strongly [SOURCE: Page_29]

affects the expression of defence mechanisms, both constitutive and induced. [SOURCE: Page_29]

The availability of nitrogen strongly affects the expression of defence mechanisms, [SOURCE: Page_29]

both constitutive and induced. Theories are meant to predict the relative importance of types [SOURCE: Page_29]

of secondary compounds for defence according to the environment of the plant (Bryant et al., [SOURCE: Page_29]

1983; Ruohomaki et al., 1996). An environment that promotes photosynthesis and limiting [SOURCE: Page_29]

nutrients promote the acquisition of defensive compounds based on carbon, such as terpenes [SOURCE: Page_29]

or phenols. An environment poor in light and rich in nutrients favour the contrary, the [SOURCE: Page_29]

28 [SOURCE: Page_29]

accumulation of nitrogen compounds such as alkaloids. This assumption contributes to a [SOURCE: Page_30]

more general theory, called the growth-differentiation balance (Herms and Mattson, 1992)), [SOURCE: Page_30]

suggesting a negative correlation between tissue growth and synthesis of secondary [SOURCE: Page_30]

compounds. [SOURCE: Page_30]

In environments rich in resources, they are a priority to growth and secondary [SOURCE: Page_30]

metabolism is limited, however, when stress limits the growth more than photosynthesis, the [SOURCE: Page_30]

theory predicts a larger allocation of resources for secondary metabolism. It was suggested; [SOURCE: Page_30]

however (Hoffland et al., 1999b; Walters and Bingham, 2007) that the low concentration of [SOURCE: Page_30]

defensive compounds and their accumulation located in the body makes them dependent on [SOURCE: Page_30]

the overall balance of carbon and nitrogen resources of the plant. A compound as-α tomatine, [SOURCE: Page_30]

and saponin fungi-toxic is produced in relatively large quantities in tomato, seems its [SOURCE: Page_30]

concentration positively correlated with C / N of the plant, then it is considered a nitrogen- [SOURCE: Page_30]

based molecule (Hoffland et al., 1999b). [SOURCE: Page_30]

The literature reports conflicting results regarding the effect of nitrogen on the [SOURCE: Page_30]

expression of defence molecules. a negative correlation has been found, for example, in [SOURCE: Page_30]

grapevine, between the amount of resveratrol a phytoalexin phenolic of stilbene family and [SOURCE: Page_30]

nitrogen fertilization (van Baarlen et al., 2004). Stout et al. (1998) showed that the total [SOURCE: Page_30]

concentration of phenolic compounds was lower with high nitrogen nutrition. These findings [SOURCE: Page_30]

seem to illustrate a general situation. But for the terpenes and alkaloids, relationship with N [SOURCE: Page_30]

nutrition are more variable (Dietrich et al., 2004) While a deficient nitrogen supply limits the [SOURCE: Page_30]

overall content of the plant protein, a variation of the nitrogen in tomato plants did not alter [SOURCE: Page_30]

the induction of proteinase inhibitor activity following a leaf stress (Stout et al., 1998) This [SOURCE: Page_30]

contradicts the hypothesis that favoured the induction of nitrogen based defence molecules at [SOURCE: Page_30]

high nitrogen nutrition. However, another study (Dietrich et al., 2004) showed that the [SOURCE: Page_30]

availability of nitrogen in the plant modulates both the constitutive and induced levels of [SOURCE: Page_30]

29 [SOURCE: Page_30]

certain defensive proteins, and their speed of induction in case of stimulation of a chemical [SOURCE: Page_31]

elicitor. But all the PR proteins do not have their increased synthesis by a higher nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_31]

content of the plant, which undermines the general theories to predict the importance of [SOURCE: Page_31]

defensive reaction to the level of nutrition. [SOURCE: Page_31]

3.6     Effect of nitrogen on the development of plant disease [SOURCE: Page_31]

Nitrogen plays an important role in development of plant diseases. The effect of different [SOURCE: Page_31]

nitrogen levels on disease severity of genotypes of wheat varying in quantitative resistance to [SOURCE: Page_31]

yellow rust caused by Puccinia striiformis, in both years the disease severity increased [SOURCE: Page_31]

strongly with increased N levels. The infection types also increased with increased N levels [SOURCE: Page_31]

(Danial and Parlevliet, 1995). In the field excessive amounts of N fertilizer had a marked [SOURCE: Page_31]

tendency to increase the size of the late blight lesions on the potato leaves. In the greenhouse [SOURCE: Page_31]

excessive use of N fertilizer did not increase the late blight lesions on the potato [SOURCE: Page_31]

leaves.(Awan and Struchtemeyer, 1957). By increasing the amended N, there was a linear [SOURCE: Page_31]

increase of 34.8% for AUIPC (Area under incidence progress curve) and 34.3% for AUSPC [SOURCE: Page_31]

(Area under severity progress curve) of Phoma spot of coffee. The dry matter of coffee [SOURCE: Page_31]

seedlings increased linearly with the increase in the amended N. By increasing the amended [SOURCE: Page_31]

N, a corresponding increase in shoot N content was observed (De Lima et al., 2010). [SOURCE: Page_31]

Table II. Effect of N level on disease severity of several diseases. [SOURCE: Page_31]

Pathogen/disease                 Low N          High N     References Puccinia graminis                decrease       increase   Howard et al 1994 Erysiphe graminis                decrease       increase   Büschbell and Hoffmann 1992 Oidium lycopersicum              decrease       increase   Hoffland et al. 2000 Plasmodiophora brassicae         decrease       increase   Kiraly 1976 Tobacco mosaic virus             decrease       increase   Singh 1970 Pseudomonas syringae             decrease       increase   Hoffland et al 2000 Xanthomonas vesicatoria          increase       decrease   Chase 1989 Alternaria solani                increase       decrease   Blachinski et al. 1996 Fusarium oxysporum               increase       decrease   Woltz and Engelhar 1973 Botrytis cinerea                 increase       decrease   Lecompte et al 2010 [SOURCE: Page_31]

### 4. Relationship between nutritional status of the host plant and

susceptibility to Botrytis cinerea. [SOURCE: Page_32]

4.1    Nitrogen nutrition of the host and Botrytis [SOURCE: Page_32]

The effect of nitrogen nutrition of plants on their susceptibility to the fungus is [SOURCE: Page_32]

controversial (Dik and Wubben, 2004a; Yermiyahu et al., 2006). Some of the inconsistencies [SOURCE: Page_32]

in literature is certainly what the different phases of the cycle of the pathogen were examined, [SOURCE: Page_32]

and the response to nitrogen nutrition for the host is multifactor. There remains, however [SOURCE: Page_32]

some inconsistencies. (Huber and Watson, 1974), refer to the bean infection 2.5 times greater [SOURCE: Page_32]

with ammonium than with the nitrate nutrition. The authors explain that the addition of [SOURCE: Page_32]

ammonium increased quantity and quality of leaf exudates (the richest in sugars and amino [SOURCE: Page_32]

acids) and cell permeability of the host is increased with NH$_4$. These two factors contribute to [SOURCE: Page_32]

a better germination of spores and increased infection of the host. Nitrogen fertilization [SOURCE: Page_32]

promotes both leaf growth of vines and the development of clusters, making them more [SOURCE: Page_32]

susceptible to infection by B. cinerea (Elmer and Michailides, 2004a; R'Houma et al., 1998b), [SOURCE: Page_32]

but some authors also report no effect of nitrogen on the development of the pathogen (Elmer [SOURCE: Page_32]

and Michailides, 2004a; R'Houma et al., 1998b), showed that the frequency of infection and [SOURCE: Page_32]

the severity of the disease increased with intake of ammonium nitrate from 200 kg / ha to 700 [SOURCE: Page_32]

kg / ha, but the size of some of the leaves and clusters allowed removing the increased [SOURCE: Page_32]

susceptibility with nitrogen. It seems that at least part of the susceptibility of the vine is the [SOURCE: Page_32]

result of the abundant fertilization with establishment a favourable microclimate for infection. [SOURCE: Page_32]

However, a negative correlation was found between nitrogen fertilization and resveratrol [SOURCE: Page_32]

content of the plant (van Baarlen et al., 2004). This compound is active against Botrytis in [SOURCE: Page_32]

planta; it is also possible that the increased susceptibility is related to a lower production of [SOURCE: Page_32]

defence compounds in plants well supplied with N.           Increased susceptibility of several [SOURCE: Page_32]

31 [SOURCE: Page_32]

legumes to Botrytis (B. cinerea or B. fabae) with increasing doses of nitrogen has also been [SOURCE: Page_33]

reported (Davidson et al., 2004).. [SOURCE: Page_33]

The potential role of the microclimate in the infection is not known. The effect of [SOURCE: Page_33]

nitrogen nutrition of the host on the severity of infections after artificial inoculation of the [SOURCE: Page_33]

pathogen is mixed. A logarithmic relationship between the nitrogen content in the nutrient [SOURCE: Page_33]

solution (between 1.8 and 28 mM NH4NO$_3$) and the incidence of sporulation on stem [SOURCE: Page_33]

segments of basil has been reported by (Yermiyahu et al., 2006). But in this study neither the [SOURCE: Page_33]

incidence nor the growth of cankers were significantly affected by nitrogen. (Pitchay et al., [SOURCE: Page_33]

2007) have reported a much higher incidence of lesions in the begonia for fertilization [SOURCE: Page_33]

plethoric 42 mM N. For lower doses (between 1 and 28 .7 mM), a hump-shaped relationship [SOURCE: Page_33]

between nitrogen content and frequency of cankers was shown, with a maximum severity at 7 [SOURCE: Page_33]

mM. A quadratic susceptibility response of the fungus to the nitrogen nutrition has been [SOURCE: Page_33]

reported in chrysanthemum (Hobbs and Waters, 1964).           But in the tomato, it has been [SOURCE: Page_33]

consistently reported a negative correlation between nitrogen nutrition and susceptibility to B. [SOURCE: Page_33]

cinerea (Hoffland et al., 1999b; Lecompte et al., 2010; Verhoeff, 1968b). (Hoffland et al., [SOURCE: Page_33]

1999a) showed a strong positive correlation between the C / N of the plant and its [SOURCE: Page_33]

susceptibility, the latter being estimated by the number of lesions on inoculated leaves after [SOURCE: Page_33]

sampling. A proposed correlation was more questionable between soluble carbohydrates [SOURCE: Page_33]

content and susceptibility. These authors also showed that tomatine content was correlated to [SOURCE: Page_33]

the susceptibility of the plant, and assumed that the nitrogen nutrition of the host was not [SOURCE: Page_33]

likely to affect other defence compounds (phytoalexins and PR proteins) active on Botrytis. [SOURCE: Page_33]

In contradiction with these results, it has been shown, in source-sink manipulations on tomato, [SOURCE: Page_33]

as cankers on shaded plants (with a content of soluble carbon likely lower than the un shaded [SOURCE: Page_33]

control) grew faster than control, while the rate of pathogen development was slowed when [SOURCE: Page_33]

32 [SOURCE: Page_33]

the fruits were removed from the plant (probably leaving a carbon content in the stems higher [SOURCE: Page_34]

than controls (Shtienberg et al., 1998). [SOURCE: Page_34]

4.2    Effect of form of nitrogen on severity of B. cinerea [SOURCE: Page_34]

A wide range of pathogens and their hosts are influenced by N, but the form of of N [SOURCE: Page_34]

available to the pathogen or the host may influence the disease reaction, independently of the [SOURCE: Page_34]

rate of N available (Huber and Watson, 1974). The influence of the different forms of N on [SOURCE: Page_34]

the severity of B. cinerea is well documented. For example in in vitro conditions when 8 [SOURCE: Page_34]

different nitrogen sources were tested, the best development of B. cinerea was observed on L- [SOURCE: Page_34]

leucine aminoacide and DL-asparagine, monohydrate amide substrates. The least favorable [SOURCE: Page_34]

nitrogen sources were DL-norvaline and L-asparagine, substrates on which the test fungus [SOURCE: Page_34]

grew and sporulated very weakly.           Among 13 carbon sources tested on Czapek culture [SOURCE: Page_34]

medium supplemented with yeast extract 0.2%, the most metabolized were the [SOURCE: Page_34]

polysaccharides cellulose and inulin, and the least was sorbose (Stefan, 2001). The disease [SOURCE: Page_34]

susceptibility in strawberry was affected by both N-concentration and N-source. B. cinerea [SOURCE: Page_34]

lesions were largest in ammonium nitrate > ammonium sulphate > calcium nitrate. These [SOURCE: Page_34]

data suggest that calcium nitrate may be a suitable source of nitrogen, helping growers to [SOURCE: Page_34]

reduce disease risk caused by B. cinerea (Walter et al., 2008b). [SOURCE: Page_34]

4.3    Nitrogen increases the susceptibility of crops to B. cinerea [SOURCE: Page_34]

Nitrogen is by far the most extensively reported element affecting plant disease caused [SOURCE: Page_34]

by B. cinerea by increasing the susceptibility of plants. Several studies demonstrated that [SOURCE: Page_34]

high N increased the susceptibility of plants to B. cinerea. For example, the susceptibility of [SOURCE: Page_34]

winter rape infection by B. cinerea increased with increasing N rate, while infection by S. [SOURCE: Page_34]

sclerotiorum and shedding of flower buds and flowers due to water and heat stress decreased [SOURCE: Page_34]

as N rate increased (Jankowski and Budzynski, 1997). In Rape crop differences in N fertilizer [SOURCE: Page_34]

application did not affect the presence of S. sclerotiorum, but infection with B. cinerea [SOURCE: Page_34]

33 [SOURCE: Page_34]

increased with increasing amounts of N (Lemanczyk et al., 1997). Elevated levels of N [SOURCE: Page_35]

supply from 7.15 to 57.1 mM also increased the susceptibility of begonia to B. cinerea disease [SOURCE: Page_35]

by 10-80% in stems and 3-14% in leaves, the increase in susceptibility also corresponded with [SOURCE: Page_35]

increased tissue energy content and altered leaf orientation (Pitchay et al., 2007).          In [SOURCE: Page_35]

grapevines high N predisposed grapevines to infection by B. cinerea bunch rot had increased [SOURCE: Page_35]

disease severity, latent infection of cups and berries, as well as visible infection of clusters [SOURCE: Page_35]

increased as the rate of ammonium nitrate amendment increased (R'Houma et al., 1998a). [SOURCE: Page_35]

The high N nutrition also increased the susceptibility of sweet basil to B. cinerea , disease [SOURCE: Page_35]

incidence, lesion size and rate of disease progression were erratic and rarely significant [SOURCE: Page_35]

(Yermiyahu et al., 2006). [SOURCE: Page_35]

(Lacroux et al., 2008) reported that high N fertilization also enhances aroma [SOURCE: Page_35]

expression, but it also increases vine vigour and susceptibility to grey mould, grapes may be [SOURCE: Page_35]

apparently healthy when packed, but bunches from vigorous vines grown with excess N could [SOURCE: Page_35]

develop high levels of B. cinerea rot during and after storage (Chambers et al., 1993). In [SOURCE: Page_35]

other study high N increased vegetative growth but not improve the quality & yield of grapes [SOURCE: Page_35]

there was no visible effect of B. cinerea (Chambers et al., 1995). High N causing increased [SOURCE: Page_35]

disease severity and canopy density, which in turn causes a microclimate more conducive to [SOURCE: Page_35]

the development of botrytis bunch rot in grapes (Mundy and Beresford, 2007). Further more [SOURCE: Page_35]

in strawberries high N increased severity of B. cinerea, optimising cultural methods may, [SOURCE: Page_35]

however be of greater importance in susceptible strawberry cultivars with their greater [SOURCE: Page_35]

potential losses due to Botrytis (Daugaard et al., 2003). In straw berry elevated nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_35]

concentrations in the fertilizer solution increased disease severity of Botrytis rot (Nam et al., [SOURCE: Page_35]

2006). High N increased Botrytis store rot in Kiwi fruit and fruits were more susceptible in [SOURCE: Page_35]

cold storage (Pertot and Perin, 1999). High N also increased the susceptibility of maize to [SOURCE: Page_35]

grey leaf spot, AUDPC was higher with higher nitrogen (Caldwell et al., 2002). [SOURCE: Page_35]

34 [SOURCE: Page_35]

4.4    Reduction of plant susceptibility to Botrytis cinerea with nitrogen fertilization [SOURCE: Page_36]

The N nutritional status of the plant is known to decrease the susceptibility to B. [SOURCE: Page_36]

cinerea in different host plants. In a very careful study conducted by Lecompte et al (2010) [SOURCE: Page_36]

on the effect of different levels of N on the susceptibility of tomato to six isolates of B. [SOURCE: Page_36]

cinerea the results showed that the overall disease severity was lower for all isolates on plants [SOURCE: Page_36]

with higher N fertilization regimes, regardless of inoculum concentration. Disease onset was [SOURCE: Page_36]

delayed on all plants with higher nitrogen inputs, but the response was greater for isolates [SOURCE: Page_36]

with lower aggressiveness on tomato. The daily rate of stem lesion expansion decreased with [SOURCE: Page_36]

increasing nitrogen fertilization levels for the more aggressive isolates, while it increased for [SOURCE: Page_36]

the less aggressive isolate (Lecompte et al., 2010). Furthermore earlier studies also confirmed [SOURCE: Page_36]

this phenomenon, for example, when leaves of tomato plants grown at low N availability were [SOURCE: Page_36]

about 2.5 times more susceptible to primary lesion formation by B. cinerea compared to plant [SOURCE: Page_36]

grown at high nitrogen availability (Hoffland et al., 1999a; Verhoeff, 1968a). [SOURCE: Page_36]

In other study on pepper plants the results showed that increasing nitrate supply was [SOURCE: Page_36]

positive to suppress B. cinerea on stems. With regard to nitrogen analysis, the leaves had [SOURCE: Page_36]

more nitrogen content than stems and roots. The total nitrogen concentrations in plants [SOURCE: Page_36]

increased with increasing calcium and nitrate supply, results of this study indicate that the [SOURCE: Page_36]

increasing calcium and nitrate in nutrient solution positively affected vegetative growth and [SOURCE: Page_36]

also effective against B. cinerea of sweet pepper plants (Yoon et al., 2010).            High N [SOURCE: Page_36]

fertilization rates or late timing application increased the resistance of pepper plants (Carballo [SOURCE: Page_36]

et al., 1994a). In raspberry plants high N decreased B.cinerea, N + mixtures of fungicides [SOURCE: Page_36]

combine give better results (Cruz A, 1993). In some cases the application of 80 and 240 kg [SOURCE: Page_36]

N/ha and a mixture of benomyl + captan on B. cinerea development on raspberry reduced [SOURCE: Page_36]

conidial number in ripe and unripe berries and percentage of infected berries (Cruz A, 1993). [SOURCE: Page_36]

35 [SOURCE: Page_36]

4.5    High N nutrition increase the susceptibility of crops during storage [SOURCE: Page_37]

The high N nutrition also have the effect on Botrytis rots during storage for example in grapes [SOURCE: Page_37]

during the storage excess N could develop high level of Botrytis rot because due to high N [SOURCE: Page_37]

grapes becomes healthy and vigorous and they were more susceptible to rot (Chambers et al., [SOURCE: Page_37]

1993). Preliminary investigations have shown that excessive N-fertilization significantly [SOURCE: Page_37]

increases the incidence of rot in kiwifruit in cold storage, since the incidence of rot varies [SOURCE: Page_37]

from year to year in fruit from plants not receiving high rates of N (Pertot and Perin, 1999). [SOURCE: Page_37]

In other study in vine yards showed that during infection the quantity of nitrogen used did not [SOURCE: Page_37]

have a visible effect on the infection rates of B. cinerea in the vineyard, but after harvest there [SOURCE: Page_37]

was a greater presence of the fungus in grapes from vines treated with high N (Chambers et [SOURCE: Page_37]

al., 1995). [SOURCE: Page_37]
