### 5.     Effect of nitrogen nutrition on fungal sporulation

The most important aspect in the epidemiology caused by fungi is the time of onset of [SOURCE: Page_38]

sporulation and also it is a major factor for determining rates of disease progress because [SOURCE: Page_38]

spore is the major vehicle for the development and the maintenance of epidemic within a crop [SOURCE: Page_38]

(Dahlberg and Van Etten, 1982a; De Vallavieille-Pope et al., 2000; Meredith, 1973). Fungal [SOURCE: Page_38]

sporulation is of great interest to workers in many scientific disciplines. Sporulation is [SOURCE: Page_38]

important to plant pathologists and mycologists since spores are the major vehicle for [SOURCE: Page_38]

dissemination of fungal diseases. Nitrogen nutrition of the plant has the effect on sporulation [SOURCE: Page_38]

of many fungi. The severity of an epidemic in cultural conditions also depends on the ability [SOURCE: Page_38]

of the fungus to form and spread a secondary inoculum.             Studies linking the level of [SOURCE: Page_38]

sporulation of the fungus to the nitrogen nutrition of the host are few, but they usually lead to [SOURCE: Page_38]

the conclusion that high nitrogen content in the host promotes sporulation of the pathogen [SOURCE: Page_38]

(Hoffland et al., 2000a; Jensen and Munk, 1997; Robert et al., 2002). [SOURCE: Page_38]

Robert et al. (2002) showed that despite the nitrogen and carbon contents of wheat [SOURCE: Page_38]

seedlings were very different, spores of P. triticina was relatively constant. In contrast, a [SOURCE: Page_38]

comparable density of spores, the number of spores per unit area was significantly weakened [SOURCE: Page_38]

in the nitrogen deficient host. If sporulation can be induced in vitro by depletion of the [SOURCE: Page_38]

medium carbon and nitrogen analysis of transcription during sporulation in in planta showed [SOURCE: Page_38]

that transcripts are similar to those found during sporulation in vitro in rich media. It does not [SOURCE: Page_38]

seem to be any mention in the literature of variability in aggressiveness of spores from a [SOURCE: Page_38]

primary infection on plants with access to different food resources. The canopy structure, [SOURCE: Page_38]

which depends on nitrogen nutrition, may affect the dispersal of secondary inoculum. Lovell [SOURCE: Page_38]

et al. (1997) showed that the severity of infection by S. tritici on wheat crop was dependent on [SOURCE: Page_38]

both the ability of spores to grow vertically into the canopy and the latency time between [SOURCE: Page_38]

cycles of infection. Despite a canopy structure more conducive to limiting the spread of [SOURCE: Page_38]

37 [SOURCE: Page_38]

inoculum to the upper leaf canopy in the case of good nitrogen availability, a reduced duration [SOURCE: Page_39]

of the infectious cycle in plants well supplied with N have led to a more severe infection of [SOURCE: Page_39]

wheat by S. tritici. [SOURCE: Page_39]

The composition of medium in vitro and also type and quantity of nutrients available [SOURCE: Page_39]

for the fungi play an important role in the spore production of different fungi but nutritional [SOURCE: Page_39]

requirements for fungal growth and sporulation vary among different fungi (Dong and Yao, [SOURCE: Page_39]

2005; Schisler et al., 1991a; Steyaert et al., 2010). For any fungal biocontrol agent to be [SOURCE: Page_39]

commercially viable, artificial substrates with different composition are very important for [SOURCE: Page_39]

their mass production. In this regard several studies have been conducted to investigate the [SOURCE: Page_39]

effect of medium nutrients on mass production of spores of several biocontrol fungi for [SOURCE: Page_39]

example Trichoderma viride; Paecilomyces lilacinus (Gao et al., 2007); weed biocontrol [SOURCE: Page_39]

agent Colletotrichum truncate (Schisler et al., 1991a); the insect pathogenic fungus [SOURCE: Page_39]

Metarhizium anisoplia (Shah et al., 2005); for red yeast fungus Monascus. purpureus a [SOURCE: Page_39]

combination of glucose and sucrose greatly enhanced sporulation and cell mass production [SOURCE: Page_39]

(Ajdari et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_39]

For plant pathogens like Botrytis sp according to (Maas and Powelson, 1972) B. [SOURCE: Page_39]

convoluta sporulate abundantly on media containing glucose, sucrose, and fructose media as [SOURCE: Page_39]

compared to maltose, galactose, and starch media and absent with glycine, urea and no [SOURCE: Page_39]

nitrogen. As for as B. cinerea is concerned generally this pathogen infects healthy plant [SOURCE: Page_39]

tissues but mycelial growth and production of spores occurs in necrotic tissues after infection . [SOURCE: Page_39]

B. cinerea is a polycyclic pathogen and produced epidemics in the field usually are [SOURCE: Page_39]

polycyclic, and involve cycles of infection and sporulation. The timing and abundance of [SOURCE: Page_39]

sporulation by B. cinerea on dead plant tissues contributes to the development and the [SOURCE: Page_39]

maintenance of epidemic within a crop (Hausbeck and Moorman, 1996). For understanding [SOURCE: Page_39]

the development of epidemics and predicting yield losses the description of spore production [SOURCE: Page_39]

38 [SOURCE: Page_39]

and the factors affecting the rate and pattern of pathogen sporulation is necessary (Robert et [SOURCE: Page_40]

al., 2004);(Bastiaans, 1991). Therefore the reduction in sporulation of B. cinerea may result [SOURCE: Page_40]

in a retardation of the polycyclic epidemics and rate of disease development (Yermiyahu et [SOURCE: Page_40]

al., 2006). In addition the severity of epidemic condition in crops also depends on the ability [SOURCE: Page_40]

of the fungus to spread and form a secondary inoculum. In absence of efficient control, B. [SOURCE: Page_40]

cinerea have the ability to produce massive amount of spores on diseased plants. In favorable [SOURCE: Page_40]

conditions it can produce millions of spores per gram of fresh tissues of tomatoes within a [SOURCE: Page_40]

week after inoculation (Nicot et al., 1996). [SOURCE: Page_40]

Regarding the influence of N fertilization of the host plant on the sporulation of B. [SOURCE: Page_40]

cinerea, the results showed that the nitrogen concentrations in the standard irrigation solution [SOURCE: Page_40]

(7.2 and 1.3 mM, respectively) were varied in a series of experiments conducted in pots and [SOURCE: Page_40]

large containers. Effect of the N nutrition on grey mould incidence of sweet basil, lesion size [SOURCE: Page_40]

and rate of disease progression were erratic and rarely significant, but decreasing the [SOURCE: Page_40]

concentration of N and increasing the concentration of Ca in the irrigation solution decreased [SOURCE: Page_40]

sporulation of B. cinerea (Yermiyahu et al., 2006). [SOURCE: Page_40]

5.1    Effect of type of substrates on the sporulation of different fungi [SOURCE: Page_40]

For plant pathogens, plant substrates can also play an important role in the production [SOURCE: Page_40]

of spores but surprisingly little or no information on effect of plant fertilization, although it [SOURCE: Page_40]

must modify the content of nutrients available to plant pathogens. The effect of substrate [SOURCE: Page_40]

composition on spore production by fungi, including B. cinerea, has been fairly well [SOURCE: Page_40]

documented (Dahlberg and Van Etten, 1982b; Dallemole-Giaretta et al., 2011; Gao and Liu, [SOURCE: Page_40]

2010; Maas and Powelson, 1972; Nicot et al., 1996; Winder, 1999). It has been shown that [SOURCE: Page_40]

sporulation is generally favored in the substrates with nutritional conditions that restrict [SOURCE: Page_40]

growth. Some fungi have specific carbon and nitrogen requirements for sporulation, others [SOURCE: Page_40]

sporulate only upon starvation or nutritional depletion (Dahlberg and Van Etten, 1982a). [SOURCE: Page_40]

39 [SOURCE: Page_40]

5.2      Effect of nutrient substrate on aggressiveness of fungi [SOURCE: Page_41]

Composition and type of substrate has been shown to affect the aggressiveness of [SOURCE: Page_41]

different fungi. A few in vitro studies have shown that aggressiveness of fungi can be [SOURCE: Page_41]

affected by the composition of the spore production medium.            For plant pathogens for [SOURCE: Page_41]

example, aggressiveness of B. cinerea spores to rose flowers was shown to increase with [SOURCE: Page_41]

increasing concentrations of glucose in the inoculum production medium (Phillips et al., [SOURCE: Page_41]

1987). Other effects of substrate have also been reported on the aggressiveness of other plant [SOURCE: Page_41]

pathogens such as Fusarium avenaceum and Phytophthora infestans (Winder, 1999; [SOURCE: Page_41]

Zarzycka, 1995) and the entomophagous fungus Beauveria bassiana (Bena-Molaei et al., [SOURCE: Page_41]

2011). [SOURCE: Page_41]

Some results indicate that incubation of conidia under conditions of nutrient stress can [SOURCE: Page_41]

attenuate the aggressiveness and germinability of B. sorokiniana conidia (Arora et al., 1985). [SOURCE: Page_41]

The cultural substrates types also affect the production of spores and virulence of the fungi [SOURCE: Page_41]

(Schisler et al., 1991b). Earlier studies shows that when inoculum of Fusarium avenaceum [SOURCE: Page_41]

was produced on puffed wheat was twice as virulent as that produced on marsh reed grass [SOURCE: Page_41]

straw amended with malt extract, while straw cultures generally produced a higher proportion [SOURCE: Page_41]

of macro conidia (Winder, 1999). When fungus has enter into the plant tissues the available [SOURCE: Page_41]

nitrogen source also depend on the infected tissues of the plant being colonized like leaves, [SOURCE: Page_41]

stems and roots of the same plant has different amount of nitrogen sources (Snoeijers et al., [SOURCE: Page_41]

2000a). [SOURCE: Page_41]

5.3      Effect of nitrogen supply on sugars, acids carotinoids and phenolic compounds. [SOURCE: Page_41]

Nitrogen supply has a wide range effects on the plants primary and secondary [SOURCE: Page_41]

metabolism. The levels of amino acids, tricarboxylic acid cycle intermediates, sugars, sugar [SOURCE: Page_41]

alcohols, and representative compounds of secondary metabolism revealed substantial [SOURCE: Page_41]

changes under the various growth regimes applied in tomato. The nitrate nutrition has wide- [SOURCE: Page_41]

40 [SOURCE: Page_41]

ranging effects on plant leaf metabolism with nitrate deficiency resulting in decreases in many [SOURCE: Page_42]

amino and organic acids and increases in the level of several carbohydrates and [SOURCE: Page_42]

phosphoresces as well as a handful of secondary metabolites (Urbanczyk-Wochniak and [SOURCE: Page_42]

Fernie, 2005). Lowering nitrogen supply had a low impact on tomato fruit commercial yield [SOURCE: Page_42]

but it reduced plant vegetative growth and increased fruit dry matter content, improving [SOURCE: Page_42]

consequently fruit quality. Fruit quality was improved due to lower acid (10-16%) and [SOURCE: Page_42]

increased soluble sugar content (5-17%). The content of some phenolic compounds (rutin, a [SOURCE: Page_42]

caffeic acid glycoside, and a caffeic acid derivate) and total ascorbic acid tended to be higher [SOURCE: Page_42]

in fruit with the lowest nitrogen supply, but differences were significant in only a few cases. [SOURCE: Page_42]

With regard to carotenoids, data did not show significant and univocal differences [SOURCE: Page_42]

related to different levels of nitrogen supply (Benard et al., 2009). In apple lower phenolic [SOURCE: Page_42]

compounds were found at higher N rates and also high N increased the susceptibility of apple [SOURCE: Page_42]

to scab (Leser and Treutter, 2005). Potato plants supplied with high N showed significantly [SOURCE: Page_42]

increased biomass as compared to plants without additional N fertilisation, C/N ratio was [SOURCE: Page_42]

lower and protein content was higher in leaves of the high N variant. The concentration of [SOURCE: Page_42]

chlorogenic acids and flavonols was significantly lower in leaves from the high N variant. [SOURCE: Page_42]

Whereas resistance to Alternaria solani increased when plants were supplied with additional [SOURCE: Page_42]

nitrogen, these plants were more susceptible to Phytophthora infestans (Mittelstrass et al., [SOURCE: Page_42]

2006). [SOURCE: Page_42]

But in case of Barley higher phenol content was found at higher N levels and high N [SOURCE: Page_42]

and K levels were positively correlated with a high level of resistance against barley leaf spot [SOURCE: Page_42]

(Jalal and Sarhan, 1988).       In basil higher phenolic contents observed when nutrient [SOURCE: Page_42]

availability was limited at the lowest (0.1 mM) applied nitrogen treatment. Similarly, basil [SOURCE: Page_42]

treated at the lowest nitrogen fertilization level generally contained significantly higher [SOURCE: Page_42]

rosmarinic and caffeic acid concentrations than basil treated at other nitrogen levels. Nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_42]

41 [SOURCE: Page_42]

fertilization also affected antioxidant activity with basil treated at the highest applied nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_43]

level, 5.0 mM, exhibiting lower antioxidant activity than all other nitrogen treatments [SOURCE: Page_43]

(Nguyen and Niemeyer, 2008). In oil seed rape the oil content was negatively correlated with N [SOURCE: Page_43]

fertilizer rate and the increase in the N fertilizer rate resulted in the increase in infection by [SOURCE: Page_43]

Peronospora parasitica. Protein content also increased with the increase in N fertilizer rate [SOURCE: Page_43]

(Sochting and Verreet, 2004). [SOURCE: Page_43]

5.4       Effect of nitrogen nutrition on growth, transpiration and nutrient uptake of the [SOURCE: Page_43]

plants. [SOURCE: Page_43]

The optimum N concentrations for pepper for maximum stem and leaf dry matter [SOURCE: Page_43]

production were in the range of 8.0 to 9.2 mM. The optimum N-NO$_3$: N-NH$_4$ ratio for [SOURCE: Page_43]

maximal stem DM production was 3.5.(Bar-Tal et al., 2001) The optimum value of N [SOURCE: Page_43]

concentration for total fruit DM production was 9.4 mM. The N concentration, but not N [SOURCE: Page_43]

source, affected leaf chlorophyll content. Shorter plants with more compacted canopies were [SOURCE: Page_43]

obtained as the N-NO$_3$: N-NH$_4$ ratio decreased.              The effect of N concentration on [SOURCE: Page_43]

transpiration was related to its effect on leaf weight and area. Nitrogen uptake increased as [SOURCE: Page_43]

the N concentration in the solution increased (Bar-Tal et al., 2001). [SOURCE: Page_43]

Levels of NO$_3$- and Ca$_2$+ exceeding normal concentration resulted in decreased seedling [SOURCE: Page_43]

growth, shorter internodes, and lower shoot/root relations. These effects are presumably due [SOURCE: Page_43]

to the increase in solution salinity and the greater osmotic potential in the growing medium [SOURCE: Page_43]

(Guzman and Sanchez, 2003). An adequate balance between growth rates and transplant [SOURCE: Page_43]

quality parameters was obtained with 8 meq. L-1. NO$_3$- and 5 meq. L-1 Ca$_2$+ in nursery [SOURCE: Page_43]

fertigation solution. When different cultivars of transplanted pepper (Capsicum annuum L) [SOURCE: Page_43]

were subjected to very high concentrations of NO$_3$- (32 meq.L-1) and low concentrations of [SOURCE: Page_43]

Ca$_2$+ (5 meq.L-1) in nutrient solution, the vegetative growth rate, leaf area expansion [SOURCE: Page_43]

42 [SOURCE: Page_43]

parameters and leaf biomass production were positively affected (Guzman and Sanchez, [SOURCE: Page_44]

2003). [SOURCE: Page_44]

43 [SOURCE: Page_44]

6      Effect of nutrition on the enhancement of biological control [SOURCE: Page_45]

Biological control has advanced greatly during the last few years and microbial [SOURCE: Page_45]

antagonists have been reported to control several diseases (Janisiewicz and Marchi, 1992). [SOURCE: Page_45]

Reliability and cost are two major factors that will determine the feasibility of any biocontrol [SOURCE: Page_45]

system (Janisiewicz et al., 1992). In order to be a successful competitor at the wound site, an [SOURCE: Page_45]

antagonist has to be better adapted than the pathogen to various environmental and nutritional [SOURCE: Page_45]

conditions, to grow rapidly at the wound site, to use nutrients effectively at low [SOURCE: Page_45]

concentrations, and survive and grow at the infection site at range of temperatures, pH and [SOURCE: Page_45]

osmotic conditions. Enhancers are chemicals that serve as a food base for antagonistic [SOURCE: Page_45]

microbes or are selectively toxic or fungicidal to pathogens (Spurr, 1994). Therefore, [SOURCE: Page_45]

enhancers can be used to manipulate the antagonist populations on fruit and can greatly [SOURCE: Page_45]

improve biocontrol levels. [SOURCE: Page_45]

In order to replace recommended synthetic fungicides with biocontrol agents it will be [SOURCE: Page_45]

necessary to develop other methods to enhance the efficacy of biocontrol (Droby, 2001; Droby et al., [SOURCE: Page_45]

1997; Wisniewski et al., 1995). For this to enhance biocontrol activity of antagonists against [SOURCE: Page_45]

fungal pathogens, certain strategies, such as adding calcium salts, carbohydrates, amino acids [SOURCE: Page_45]

and other nitrogen compounds to biocontrl treatments are proposed (Janisiewicz et al., 1992) [SOURCE: Page_45]

Some exogenous substances, such as chitosan, amino acids, antibiotic, calcium salts and [SOURCE: Page_45]

carbohydrates have been studied to enhance biocontrol capabilityof antagonists against fungal [SOURCE: Page_45]

pathogens (El-Ghaouth et al., 2002). Nutritional manipulation has been shown to enhance [SOURCE: Page_45]

biocontrol activity of several antagonists against several pathogens for example biocontrol [SOURCE: Page_45]

with yeast against B. cinerea rot in apple was enhanced when Trichosporon sp. even at low [SOURCE: Page_45]

concentration of 10(5) was applied in the presence of 1-2% CaCl$_2$ in an aqueous suspension [SOURCE: Page_45]

(Tian et al., 2001). Biocontrol activity of yeasts against Rhizopus in peach is also improved [SOURCE: Page_45]

(Tian et al., 2002b). Another study reported that Candida saitoana plus glycol chitosan [SOURCE: Page_45]

44 [SOURCE: Page_45]

enhanced the control of postharvest decay on apples and citrus (El-Ghaouth et al., 2000; El- [SOURCE: Page_46]

Ghaouth et al., 2002) Sodium bicarbonate also increased the effectiveness of post harvest [SOURCE: Page_46]

decay of apple control by each antagonist alone or in combination (Conway et al., 2004). [SOURCE: Page_46]

(Janisiewicz et al., 1992) demonstrated that, when using the antagonist Pseudomonas [SOURCE: Page_46]

syringae, the addition of specific amino acids (L- asparagine and L-proline) enhanced [SOURCE: Page_46]

biocontrol activity against the blue mould of apple caused by Penicillium expansum. It was [SOURCE: Page_46]

suggested that the enhancement of P. syringae activity was due to the ability of the bacterium [SOURCE: Page_46]

to outcompete the fungal pathogen for a specific nitrogen source, leading to enhanced growth [SOURCE: Page_46]

of the bacterium in the wound site. [SOURCE: Page_46]

Previous in-vitro screening of the nutritional requirements of the antagonist and the [SOURCE: Page_46]

pathogen identified several amino acids that were selectively utilized by the antagonist and [SOURCE: Page_46]

not by the pathogen. (McLaughlin et al., 1990) reported that calcium salts improved the [SOURCE: Page_46]

efficacy of yeast biocontrol agents against Botrytis and Penicillium rots of apples. [SOURCE: Page_46]

Suspensions of yeast cells at concentrations as low as 106 to 107 CFU/ml effectively inhibited [SOURCE: Page_46]

disease development in the presence of different calcium salt solutions. In the absence of the [SOURCE: Page_46]

salts, a yeast cell concentration of at least 10 and usually 100 times greater was required to [SOURCE: Page_46]

achieve the same level of protection.       They further demonstrated that the effect was [SOURCE: Page_46]

specifically due to the calcium cations rather than other cations or anions. Salt solutions [SOURCE: Page_46]

applied to apple wounds without the antagonist cells failed to reduce the incidence of the rot. [SOURCE: Page_46]

The authors suggested that the effect of calcium on the biocontrol activity of the yeast [SOURCE: Page_46]

antagonists was due to some interaction with the yeast or its metabolic products at the wound [SOURCE: Page_46]

site rather than a direct effect of calcium on the pathogen or the fruit tissue. In contrast, [SOURCE: Page_46]

(Wisniewski et al., 1995) have indicated that calcium chloride reduced germination and germ [SOURCE: Page_46]

tube elongation of B. cinerea and Penicillium expansum in vitro. The inhibition was again [SOURCE: Page_46]

demonstrated to be due to the presence of the calcium cation rather than the chloride anion [SOURCE: Page_46]

45 [SOURCE: Page_46]

(e.g., MgCl$_2$ had no effect). The application of CaCl$_2$ with the yeast antagonist Candida [SOURCE: Page_47]

oleophila enhanced the biocontrol activity of the yeast against both pathogens on apple. In [SOURCE: Page_47]

other study the effect of foliar fertilizers containing various macro and microelements on [SOURCE: Page_47]

mycelial growth, spore germination and antagonism of Trichoderma isolates. It was found [SOURCE: Page_47]

that foliar fertilizers cause changes in the development and antagonism of Trichoderma spp. [SOURCE: Page_47]

Foliar fertilizer cause change in mycelial growth, spore germination and antagonism rate of T. [SOURCE: Page_47]

harzianum isolate. It was found that potassium Alkalin and Wapnovit fertilizers significantly [SOURCE: Page_47]

increased the growth rate of T. harzianum mycelium. Potassium alkalin is a fertilizer which [SOURCE: Page_47]

favourably affects the antagonism of Trichoderma isolates towards B. cinerea and R. solani [SOURCE: Page_47]

(Duzniewska, 2008). [SOURCE: Page_47]

46 [SOURCE: Page_47]

7      Possible mechanisms involved in disease suppression by nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_48]

7.1    Physiology of the plant [SOURCE: Page_48]

Nitrogen is one of the main factors which are involved in the plant’s physiological [SOURCE: Page_48]

functions for the growth and also for the disease resistance. Nitrogen influences many plant [SOURCE: Page_48]

constituents like amino acids, proteins, total N, phenolics, nucleic acids and ratio of [SOURCE: Page_48]

carbohydrates to N and these plant components are directly correlated to susceptibility or [SOURCE: Page_48]

resistance to disease (Huber, 1980).     The plant diseases are influenced by the nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_48]

nutrition in several ways. Nitrogen influence physiology of the plants by causing changes in [SOURCE: Page_48]

their structures, it also affect the growth and virulence of the pathogen and also cause changes [SOURCE: Page_48]

in the biotic and abiotic environment of the plants. In addition to this N nutrition affects the [SOURCE: Page_48]

vegetative growth of the plants and also on the chemical composition of the plants for [SOURCE: Page_48]

example, in tomato low nitrogen increased soluble content and also the content of some [SOURCE: Page_48]

phenolic compounds (rutin, a caffeic acid glycoside, and a caffeic acid derivate) and total [SOURCE: Page_48]

ascorbic acid tended to be higher in fruit with the lowest nitrogen supply. It was reported that [SOURCE: Page_48]

primary and secondary metabolites could be affected as a result of a specific response to low [SOURCE: Page_48]

nitrogen, combined with a lower degree of vegetative development, increasing fruit [SOURCE: Page_48]

irradiance, and therefore modifying fruit composition (Benard et al., 2009). In sweet basil at [SOURCE: Page_48]

high N phenolic compounds are decreased and also higher N fertilization lowers the [SOURCE: Page_48]

antioxidants (Nguyen and Niemeyer, 2008). [SOURCE: Page_48]

Passive and active mechanisms of disease control are activated through nutrient [SOURCE: Page_48]

management. Mineral nutrients are the components of plants and regulate metabolic activity [SOURCE: Page_48]

associated with resistance of a plant and virulence of a pathogen. Adequate nutrition is [SOURCE: Page_48]

generally required to maintain a high level of disease resistance. Plants contain preformed [SOURCE: Page_48]

anti-microbial compounds and have active response mechanisms where inhibitory [SOURCE: Page_48]

47 [SOURCE: Page_48]

phytoalexins, phenols, flavonoids, and other defense compounds accumulate around infection [SOURCE: Page_49]

sites of resistant plants if the nutrients required for the synthesis or induction of those [SOURCE: Page_49]

compounds are adequate. [SOURCE: Page_49]

The interaction of fungal disease and fertilizers is of great practical interest and there [SOURCE: Page_49]

have been numerous studies into the interaction of (in particular) nitrogenous fertilizer and [SOURCE: Page_49]

disease. Application of nitrogen above the recommended rate has often been shown to [SOURCE: Page_49]

significantly increase disease incidence and lesion area. This applies to both necrotrophic [SOURCE: Page_49]

Magnaporthe grisea (Long et al., 2000) and biotrophic pathogens Blumeria graminis f. [SOURCE: Page_49]

sp.tritici; and tomato mildew Oidium lycopersicum (Hoffland et al., 2000b; Jensen and Munk, [SOURCE: Page_49]

1997). The nature of the nitrogen also has an effect, Huber and Watson’s (Huber and Watson, [SOURCE: Page_49]

1974) review noted that nitrate consistently increased and ammonium decreased the incidence [SOURCE: Page_49]

of hemi-biotrophs (mildew, Colletotrichum and rust) whereas nitrate decreased and [SOURCE: Page_49]

ammonium increased the incidence of the necrotrophs (Cochliobolus, Magnaporthe and [SOURCE: Page_49]

Botrytis). Sub-optimal nitrogen and also lead to more disease. In the grey mould pathogen B. [SOURCE: Page_49]

cinerea, the formation of primary lesion on tomatoes was more frequent with low nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_49]

levels (Hoffland et al., 1999a). These plants have a high physiological carbon/nitrogen ratio [SOURCE: Page_49]

and a large amount of soluble carbohydrates in tissue (Hoffland et al., 1999a), indicating that [SOURCE: Page_49]

the growth of the pathogen was dependent on high soluble carbohydrate levels.               The [SOURCE: Page_49]

hypotheses being tested in the above studies can be redefined as follows; [SOURCE: Page_49]

(A) An insufficiency of N fertilization leads to susceptibility by weakening a plant and [SOURCE: Page_49]

promoting invasion and disease. [SOURCE: Page_49]

(B) Superabundant N fertilization leads to hyper-susceptibility by either [SOURCE: Page_49]

1   providing excess nutrient that is directly available to support the growth of pathogens, [SOURCE: Page_49]

2   promoting the growth of ill-defended plant tissue, or [SOURCE: Page_49]

48 [SOURCE: Page_49]

3   Diverting metabolic resources into the assimilation of the nutrient. [SOURCE: Page_50]

Studies that might distinguish these possibilities have given conflicting results. Matsuyama [SOURCE: Page_50]

and Dimond (1973) showed that the application of nitrogenous fertilizers to rice led to a [SOURCE: Page_50]

reduction in the activities of enzymes in the phenyl proponed pathway and the production of [SOURCE: Page_50]

lignin, suggesting that fertilizers compromised the intensity of secondary plant defenses. This [SOURCE: Page_50]

is consistent with hypothesis B$_3$. The positive effect of ammonium on necrotrophs is [SOURCE: Page_50]

consistent with hypothesis B$_1$, whereas the positive effect of low nitrogen on Botrytis is [SOURCE: Page_50]

consistent with hypothesis A. Clearly more work is needed in this area and it is, of course, [SOURCE: Page_50]

likely those different fungi will respond in different ways to the application of N fertilization. [SOURCE: Page_50]

It is important that future studies combine measurements of fungal growth and symptom [SOURCE: Page_50]

production with plant biomass and in plant nutrient levels. [SOURCE: Page_50]

7.2       Pathogen growth and virulence [SOURCE: Page_50]

Plant pathogens germination, growth, and virulence may be influenced by the amount [SOURCE: Page_50]

and also the form of N. Obligate fungal pathogens mostly rely on the nutrients supplied by [SOURCE: Page_50]

the host plant, so that changes in the plant constituents can greatly affect the growth, [SOURCE: Page_50]

reproduction and virulence of the pathogens. For example, few studies have addressed the [SOURCE: Page_50]

effect of N fertilization on the infection of fungal pathogens. Work on powdery mildew of [SOURCE: Page_50]

barley showed that infection of Blumeria (syn. Erysiphe) graminis was enhanced with [SOURCE: Page_50]

increasing N fertilization (Bainbridge, 1974; Jensen and Munk, 1997). Similarly, disease [SOURCE: Page_50]

severity by Oidium neolycopersici on tomato leaves increased with increasing nitrogen supply [SOURCE: Page_50]

to the plants (Hoffland et al., 2000b) and leaf rust uredospore production per lesion was [SOURCE: Page_50]

higher in leaves with a high-nitrogen content (Robert et al., 2004). In case of the facultative [SOURCE: Page_50]

saprophytes their survival, growth, and survival may also be greatly influenced by N nutrition. [SOURCE: Page_50]

For example, the presence of both carbon and nitrogen is necessary for the germination of [SOURCE: Page_50]

spores of the B. cinerea (Blakeman, 1975), because of the lack of endogenous energy reserves [SOURCE: Page_50]

49 [SOURCE: Page_50]

present in the conidia of B. cinerea, the fungus requires an exogenous source of nutrients to [SOURCE: Page_51]

grow (Yoder and Whalen, 1975). Nitrogen may affect the virulence of the pathogen by [SOURCE: Page_51]

stimulation or inhibiting enzyme synthesis or activity required for pathogenesis. This type of [SOURCE: Page_51]

mechanism could be especially important in macerating diseases. [SOURCE: Page_51]

7.3    Modification of the biotic or a biotic environment [SOURCE: Page_51]

Nitrogen fertilization can affect the growth of plants growth, development, chlorophyll [SOURCE: Page_51]

content, leaf angle, nutrient uptake, and thus influence the microclimate conditions within the [SOURCE: Page_51]

canopy or vegetation that might then affect disease progress (Huber and Watson, 1974). For [SOURCE: Page_51]

example, high N predisposed grapevines to infection by B. cinerea and increased disease [SOURCE: Page_51]

severity. Latent infection of cups and berries, as well as visible infection of clusters increased [SOURCE: Page_51]

as the rate of ammonium nitrate amendment increased. Conversely, removal of leaves around [SOURCE: Page_51]

clusters, when practiced 2 or 3 times during the season, and thinning of berries, significantly [SOURCE: Page_51]

reduced disease development and resulted in less latent and visibly infected clusters and [SOURCE: Page_51]

berries. These green pruning practices also attenuated the beneficial effects of N fertilizers on [SOURCE: Page_51]

disease development (R'Houma et al., 1998c). [SOURCE: Page_51]

7.4    Effect of nitrogen on gene expression of fungal pathogens [SOURCE: Page_51]

For successful infection of plants by fungal pathogens requires efficient utilization of [SOURCE: Page_51]

nutrient resources available in host tissues. Several studies have been reported that fungal [SOURCE: Page_51]

genes are specifically induced during pathogenesis and under nitrogen-limiting conditions in [SOURCE: Page_51]

vitro. For example, avirulence gene Avr^9 of the biotrophic fungal pathogen Cladosporium [SOURCE: Page_51]

fulvum is both induced in planta and during nitrogen starvation in vitro (Perez-Garcia et al., [SOURCE: Page_51]

2001). In the entomopathogenic fungus Metarhizium anisopliae the products encoded by the [SOURCE: Page_51]

genes pr^1A and pr^2 show protease activity and their expression is subjected to both carbon [SOURCE: Page_51]

and nitrogen repression (Smithson et al., 1995). In addition mpg^1 of Magnaporthe grisea [SOURCE: Page_51]

50 [SOURCE: Page_51]

(Talbot et al., 1997a), pCgGS of Colletotrichum gloeosporioides (Stephenson et al., 1997) are [SOURCE: Page_52]

also induced during infection of the host and during nitrogen starvation in vitro. [SOURCE: Page_52]

Further studies are thus required to understand the mechanisms involved at the level of [SOURCE: Page_52]

gene expression. Expression of the sporulation-regulating brlA gene of Aspergillus nidulans [SOURCE: Page_52]

was reported to be induced by either glucose or N starvation, but with different kinetics and [SOURCE: Page_52]

with differing phenotypical outcomes (Skromne et al., 1995). Similar studies with B. cinerea [SOURCE: Page_52]

would be possible, as orthologs of genes known to function upstream of blrA (including fadA, [SOURCE: Page_52]

fluG, flbC and flbD) have been reported in the genome of this fungus (Amselem et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_52]

Trophic interactions related to primary metabolism are unlikely to be the only [SOURCE: Page_52]

mechanisms implicated. Other compounds related to plant defense mechanisms would merit [SOURCE: Page_52]

further attention as they could influence fungi indirectly through their effect on plant tissue [SOURCE: Page_52]

colonization by the pathogen. N nutrition has a large effect on the amino acid content (Le Bot [SOURCE: Page_52]

et al., 2001) and constitutive phenolics (Stout et al., 1998) involved in various stages of the [SOURCE: Page_52]

infection process in tomatoes. In other plant models, peroxidase and chitinase (two enzymes [SOURCE: Page_52]

implicated in plant defense) were lower when N was limiting (Dietrich et al., 2004). Further [SOURCE: Page_52]

work could also include the effect of N (and resulting differences in plant tissue constituents) [SOURCE: Page_52]

on the expression of pathogenicity-related genes of B. cinerea. Nitrogen-limiting conditions [SOURCE: Page_52]

can influence the expression of such genes for several plant pathogenic fungi (Snoeijers et al., [SOURCE: Page_52]

2000a; Solomon et al., 2000) and possibly for human pathogens such as Aspergillus fumigatus [SOURCE: Page_52]

(Panepinto et al., 2002). [SOURCE: Page_52]

51 [SOURCE: Page_52]

Chapter 2 [SOURCE: Page_53]

Publication 1 Lecompte, F, Abro, M. A, and Nicot, P. C. 2010. Contrasted responses of Botrytis cinerea isolates developing on tomato plants grown under different nitrogen nutrition regimes. Plant Pathology 59:891-899 [SOURCE: Page_53]

52 [SOURCE: Page_53]

Plant Pathology (2010)                                                                 Doi: 10.1111/j.1365-3059.2010.02320.x [SOURCE: Page_54]

Contrasted responses of Botrytis cinerea isolates developing on tomato plants grown under different nitrogen nutrition regimes [SOURCE: Page_54]

F. Lecomptea*, M. A. Abrob and P. C. Nicotb a INRA, UR 1115 Plantes et Systèmes de Cultures Horticoles, Domaine Saint Paul, Site Agroparc, F-84914, Avignon Cedex; and b INRA, UR407 Pathologie végétale, Domaine Saint Maurice, BP94, F-84143 Montfavet Cedex, France [SOURCE: Page_54]

The nutritional status of a plant is known to influence its susceptibility to pathogens. In the case of Botrytis cinerea, the role of nitrogen fertilization of various host plants on disease development appears to be variable. This study was carried out to characterize possible variability associated with isolates and inoculum density of B. cinerea in its ability to infect leaf-pruning wounds and to develop stem lesions on tomato plants, as affected by the nitrogen input. Six isolates differing in their aggres- siveness to tomato were compared. They all had similar reaction patterns in vitro in response to differential nitrogen levels. In tests on plants grown with contrasted regimes of nitrate fertilization, overall disease severity was lower for all isolates on plants with higher nitrogen inputs, regardless of inoculum concentration. However, differences among isolates were observed in the effect of plant nitrogen nutrition on infection and on lesion expansion. Disease onset was delayed on all plants with higher nitrogen inputs, but the response was greater for isolates with lower aggressiveness on tomato. The high est contrast among isolates was observed with the colonization of stems. The daily rate of stem lesion expansion decreased with increasing nitrogen fertilization levels for the more aggressive isolates, while it increased for the less aggressive isolate. Hypotheses to explain these results are discussed in light of the possible physiological effects of nitrogen fertilization on nutri- ent availability for the pathogen in the host tissue and of possible production of defence metabolites by the plant. [SOURCE: Page_54]

Botrytis cinerea, tomato and nitrogen                                                                  5 [SOURCE: Page_58]

the data compared to a linear regression (r^2 of 0Æ33 and 0Æ21,respectively). For slow growing isolates AUDPC was not significantly altered at low N nutrition rates, but decreased steeply between 5 and 20 mmol L)1 at higher nitrate nutrition. A quadratic model did not improve the regression compared to a linear model (r^2 of 0Æ19 in both cases). [SOURCE: Page_58]

Kinetics of stem infection There was an overall highly significant effect (P < 0Æ0001) of inoculum concentration and plant nutrition on the latency period before symptom appearance. Details for the different isolates are given in Table 2. For BC$_1$ and BC21, results were not different in experiments E$_1$ and E$_2$ (P = 0Æ22), and were pooled. For each isolate, at each inoculum concentration, there was an effect of N nutri tion on the delay before symptom appearance: high nitrate nutrition delayed symptoms by 0Æ5–2Æ5 days. This increase in latency period was more pronounced for less aggressive isolates (Table 2). An increase in inoculum concentration resulted in a shorter latency period, but the Figure 2 Area under disease progress curve (AUDPC) for stem                       effect was more pronounced at low N levels. Botrytis cinerea, tomato and nitrogen                                                    5 [SOURCE: Page_60]

of calcium on B. cinerea has also been shown to depend on the isolate, but a decrease in fungal growth at high cal cium concentration was always noted (Chardonnet et al.,2000). Here, an opposite effect of plant N on the two types of isolates used in this study was consistently observed in three independent experiments. It has been suggested that high nitrate nutrition increases host susceptibility to biotrophic fungi and decreases that of necrotrophic fungi. One explanation could be that biotrophic pathogens rely on the nutrient content of the apoplast, or on nutrient transfer from the host cells by a haustorium, while necrotrophic fungi that kill cells have better access to the host nutrients (Solomon et al., 2003; Divon & Fluhr, 2007). This paper shows that in vitro the isolates responded positively, and similarly, to the N content of the substrate, at least up to a concentration of 2g L)1 NH4NO3 (Fig. 1). As the plant N content increased with increasing N in the nutrient solution, it could be hypothesized that any repression of fungal growth at the higher host N content was the result of a better Figure 4 Daily rate of stem lesion expansion on tomato following the inoculation of leaf pruning wounds with spore suspensions of ability of the plant to defend itself against the path- ogen. Lesion size was greater in E1 for B. cinerea. Lesions caused by S. sclerotiorum appeared more severe in E2, whereas in that experiment lesions caused by B. cinerea were minimal. Necrotic lesions developed usually as an ovoid shape, being slightly wider towards the distal end of the leaf (Fig. 3b,c). A GLM analysis relating the size of lesions caused by B. cinerea to the NO3− fertilization and experiment effects, along with their interaction, yielded a highly significant model (r2 = 0.41, 1,349 df, F = 175, p < 0.0001, Fig. 4a). Response curves were best fitted by a logistic function in E1 and a power function in E2 and E3. Nitrogen fertilization of the host plant influences production and pathogenicity of Botrytis cinerea secondary inoculum [SOURCE: Page_81]

M. A. Abro^1, F. Lecompte^2, F. Bryone^1 and P. C. Nicot^1 1. INRA, UR407 Pathologie végétale, Domaine Saint Maurice, CS 60094, F-84143 Montfavet cedex, France. 2. INRA, UR 1115 Plantes et systèmes de cultures horticoles, Domaine Saint Paul, CS 40509, F-84914, Avignon cedex, France [SOURCE: Page_81]

## ABSTRACT

The influence of nitrogen (N) nutrition on a plant's susceptibility to Botrytis and other [SOURCE: Page_81]

pathogens is well documented However, little is known of possible effects on sporulation of [SOURCE: Page_81]

the pathogen on diseased tissue and on the pathogenicity of resulting secondary inoculum. To [SOURCE: Page_81]

address this question, sporulation by two strains of Botrytis cinerea was quantified on tomato [SOURCE: Page_81]

plants produced under different N irrigation regimes with inputs varying from 0.5 to 45 mmol [SOURCE: Page_81]

NO$_3$-L-1 (mM). Sporulation decreased significantly (P<0.05) with increasing N fertilization up [SOURCE: Page_81]

to 15-30 mM NO$_3$-.L-1. The secondary inoculum was collected and used to inoculate pruning [SOURCE: Page_81]

wounds on tomato plants produced under a standard fertilization regime. Pathogenicity of the [SOURCE: Page_81]

spores was significantly influenced by the nutritional status of their production substrate. [SOURCE: Page_81]

Disease severity was highest with spores produced on plants with very low or very high N [SOURCE: Page_81]

fertilization (0.5 or 30 mM NO$_3$- L -1). It was lowest for inoculum from plants with moderate [SOURCE: Page_81]

levels of N fertilization. These results suggest that it may be possible to find an optimum level [SOURCE: Page_81]

of nitrogen fertilization to reduce the production of secondary inoculum and its pathogenicity to [SOURCE: Page_81]

tomato. [SOURCE: Page_81]

Additional key words: Gray mold, sporulation, fertilization, Tomato, Solanum lycopersicum. [SOURCE: Page_81]

80 [SOURCE: Page_81]

## INTRODUCTION

Sporulation is essential for the reproduction and spread of many fungi. For those that [SOURCE: Page_82]

are airborne plant pathogens, the kinetics and abundance of spore production play a key role in [SOURCE: Page_82]

the development of epidemics (9,33). Fungal sporulation can be influenced by many factors, [SOURCE: Page_82]

including the availability of nutrients (7,15,34,60). Some fungi have specific carbon (C) and [SOURCE: Page_82]

nitrogen (N) requirements, while for others, sporulation is triggered by nutrient depletion [SOURCE: Page_82]

(8,12).     For most fungi, the specific substrate also influences spore production.         Much [SOURCE: Page_82]

information has been generated to optimize substrate composition for mass producing fungi of [SOURCE: Page_82]

industrial interest such as biocontrol agents against plant pathogens (17,50) and insects [SOURCE: Page_82]

(19,24,26,44). The effects of culture conditions and host substrate on initial spore production [SOURCE: Page_82]

have also been documented for certain plant pathogenic fungi (22,32,35,42). Surprisingly little [SOURCE: Page_82]

information is available on possible effects of plant fertilization on subsequent sporulation of [SOURCE: Page_82]

pathogens although it is known to modify the concentration of nutrients available to plant [SOURCE: Page_82]

pathogens and to affect plant susceptibility (23). [SOURCE: Page_82]

The composition and type of nutrient substrate has also been shown to affect the [SOURCE: Page_82]

pathogenicity of spores produced in vitro by plant pathogenic Colletotrichum truncate, [SOURCE: Page_82]

Fusarium avenaceum and Phytophthora infestans (43,54,58); and the entomopathogen [SOURCE: Page_82]

Beauveria bassiana (4). Aggressiveness may be attenuated when spores are produced under [SOURCE: Page_82]

conditions of nutrient stress, as exemplified by Bipolaris sorokiniana conidia (2), or increased [SOURCE: Page_82]

with increasing availability of key nutrients such as glucose, as exemplified by Botrytis cinerea [SOURCE: Page_82]

(38). However, to our knowledge no information is available on effects of plant fertilization on [SOURCE: Page_82]

the pathogenicity of fungal spores produced on diseased tissues. [SOURCE: Page_82]

Botrytis cinerea, the causal agent of gray mold, is responsible for severe losses in many [SOURCE: Page_82]

crops including tomato (36,45,53). In favorable conditions, this fungus can produce a large [SOURCE: Page_82]

81 [SOURCE: Page_82]

quantity of spores on diseased tissue (35) that are easily dispersed and have a key role in the [SOURCE: Page_83]

development of gray mold epidemics (10). The control of gray mold is highly dependent on [SOURCE: Page_83]

fungicide applications and much research is dedicated to developing alternative control [SOURCE: Page_83]

methods (13). A possible alternative could consist in reducing sporulation with the help of [SOURCE: Page_83]

biocontrol agents (27) or the use of uv-filtering films (35). Another possibility to reduce [SOURCE: Page_83]

sporulation might be through the manipulation of plant fertilization. Several studies have [SOURCE: Page_83]

shown that high N nutrition of the host plant promotes the sporulation of several fungi, [SOURCE: Page_83]

including B. cinerea on sweet basil (55).       Although no information is available for the [SOURCE: Page_83]

sporulation of B. cinerea on tomato, previous studies have shown that high N nutrition reduces [SOURCE: Page_83]

the susceptibility of the plant (21,31,52). Furthermore, we were unable to find information [SOURCE: Page_83]

regarding the effect of N fertilization of plants on the pathogenicity of resulting secondary [SOURCE: Page_83]

inoculum of B. cinerea. [SOURCE: Page_83]

The objectives of this study were (1) to assess the effects of N nutrition of tomato on [SOURCE: Page_83]

sporulation of B. cinerea on diseased tissue and on pathogenicity of the resulting secondary [SOURCE: Page_83]

inoculum, and (2) to correlate these effects with modifications in plant tissue composition. [SOURCE: Page_83]

82 [SOURCE: Page_83]

## MATERIALS AND METHODS

Production of plants under different N fertilization regimes. [SOURCE: Page_84]

Tomato plants (Solanum lycopersicum var. esculentum cv Swanson) were grown from [SOURCE: Page_84]

seeds in 1cm^3 rock wool cubes in a heated greenhouse and transferred ten days after sowing to [SOURCE: Page_84]

rock wool blocks as previously described (33). The plants were irrigated with a standard [SOURCE: Page_84]

commercial greenhouse nutrient solution the first month and then transplanted to 2-L pots filled [SOURCE: Page_84]

with a mixture (1:1 V/V) of vermiculite and pozzolana (inert crushed volcanic rock) to start the [SOURCE: Page_84]

different N fertilization treatments (40 plants per N level). Five levels of nitrate concentrations [SOURCE: Page_84]

(0.5, 5, 15, 30 and 45 mmol.L-1 – abbreviated as mM in the rest of this paper) were evaluated to [SOURCE: Page_84]

span a wide range from severe N deficiency to heavy excess. To minimize possible complex [SOURCE: Page_84]

nutritional effects on the physiology of the plants, two constraints were taken into account [SOURCE: Page_84]

when designing the chemical composition of the nutrient solutions: (i) keep other elements [SOURCE: Page_84]

(particularly P, K, Ca and Mg) at a constant concentration in the different N treatments, as they [SOURCE: Page_84]

may also influence plant susceptibility to pathogens (23) and (ii) keep the total electrical [SOURCE: Page_84]

conductivity similar in all treatments, as this is known to influence the physiology of tomato [SOURCE: Page_84]

(28). The composition of the 15 mM nitrate solution was adapted from nutrient solutions used [SOURCE: Page_84]

by growers in commercial tomato glasshouses.           It contained three types of nitrate salts [SOURCE: Page_84]

(KNO$_3$,10 mM; Ca(NO$_3$)2,1.75 mM; and NH4NO$_3$,1.5 mM). The total N level of that solution [SOURCE: Page_84]

thus amounted to 15 mM as nitrate plus 1.5 mM as ammonium. For solutions with lower N [SOURCE: Page_84]

concentrations, no ammonium was used and Cl- salts (a combination of KCl, CaCl$_2$ and NaCl) [SOURCE: Page_84]

were substituted to nitrate in order to maintain similar cation concentrations.         Designing [SOURCE: Page_84]

solutions with higher N levels within the two above-mentioned constraints was more complex. [SOURCE: Page_84]

To achieve higher N concentrations with minimal consequences for the electrical conductivity, [SOURCE: Page_84]

NH4NO$_3$ was added to the solutions, bringing the total N levels to 46.5 and 76.5 mM (including [SOURCE: Page_84]

16.5 mM and 31.5 mM as ammonium), respectively, for the solutions containing 30 mM and [SOURCE: Page_84]

83 [SOURCE: Page_84]

45 mM of nitrate. To maintain the electric conductivity at the same level as in other N [SOURCE: Page_85]

treatments, the addition of NH4NO$_3$ was fully compensated for by the removal of NaCl in the [SOURCE: Page_85]

solution with 30 mM nitrate. This was not sufficient for the highest N level. Thus, this last [SOURCE: Page_85]

solution had a higher electric charge (59.5 meq of anions and 61.5 meq of cations) than the four [SOURCE: Page_85]

others (44.5 meq anions and 46.5 meq of cations). Phosphorous (P, 2 mM), K (12 mM), Ca (5 [SOURCE: Page_85]

mM), Mg (4 mM) and minor-elements were kept constant in all solutions. [SOURCE: Page_85]

The plants were fertigated with a drip irrigation system (one dripper per pot with one [SOURCE: Page_85]

minute pulses) several times a day depending on the climatic demand. Three pots chosen at [SOURCE: Page_85]

random were weighted continuously to evaluate water loss as an indicator of climatic demand. [SOURCE: Page_85]

The pH was adjusted to 6 in each treatment by the addition of H2SO$_4$. Plants were grown on the [SOURCE: Page_85]

respective solutions for four weeks prior to use for experiments on spore production and [SOURCE: Page_85]

pathogenicity of secondary inoculum of B. cinerea. [SOURCE: Page_85]

Tissue content assessment. [SOURCE: Page_85]

Just before inoculation, five plants were randomly selected from each N regime. For [SOURCE: Page_85]

each plant, the stem and leaves were weighed separately and then dried 72h at 70°C. The tissue [SOURCE: Page_85]

content was then analysed for calcium, carbon, magnesium, nitrate, potassium, phosphorous [SOURCE: Page_85]

and total N; and expressed as mg per gram of dry matter as described previously (31). Soluble [SOURCE: Page_85]

sugars (glucose, fructose and sucrose) were determined with an enzymatic method in a [SOURCE: Page_85]

microplate reader as proposed by Gomez et al. (18). [SOURCE: Page_85]

Evaluation of spore production by Botrytis cinerea on differentially fertilized plants. [SOURCE: Page_85]

Strains and preparation of primary inoculum. [SOURCE: Page_85]

For the spore production assay, two strains of B. cinerea (BC$_1$ and BC21) were used. [SOURCE: Page_85]

They were shown in earlier work to differ in their level of aggressiveness on tomato (31). [SOURCE: Page_85]

84 [SOURCE: Page_85]

Primary inoculum for both strains was produced in Petri dishes on potato dextrose agar (PDA) [SOURCE: Page_86]

incubated at 21°C under cool white fluorescent light (14-hour photoperiod; 114 μmole m-2s-1). [SOURCE: Page_86]

After 3 days of culture, mycelial plugs were excised from the growing margin of the colony [SOURCE: Page_86]

with a cork borer and used to inoculate detached organs prepared from the plants grown under [SOURCE: Page_86]

the different N fertilization treatments. [SOURCE: Page_86]

Preparation and inoculation of detached plant organs. [SOURCE: Page_86]

The plants were severed at the base and 2 cm-long segments were excised from the [SOURCE: Page_86]

stems and petioles (3 each per plant). Leaf discs 2 cm in diameter, were excised from the [SOURCE: Page_86]

leaves (3 discs per plant, one from each of leaf number 3, 4 and 5 – starting from the collar [SOURCE: Page_86]

upward). Each sample was weighed (mg fresh weight) and then placed individually in the 3- [SOURCE: Page_86]

cm diameter cap of a screw-cap tube. Each stem and petiole segment was inoculated in the [SOURCE: Page_86]

center with a 5-mm diameter mycelial plug and each leaf disk with a 2-mm diameter plug, [SOURCE: Page_86]

ensuring that the mycelium was in contact with plant tissue.         The caps were placed in [SOURCE: Page_86]

transparent polystyrene boxes over moistened filter paper, covered with lids and placed in a [SOURCE: Page_86]

growth chamber under cool white fluorescent light (16-hour photoperiod; 162 μmole m-2s-1) at [SOURCE: Page_86]

20°C. For each strain of B. cinerea, 9 replicate samples of each type of plant organ were [SOURCE: Page_86]

inoculated (3 per plant x 3 plants) and the sporulation experiment was repeated twice. [SOURCE: Page_86]

Quantification of spore production on detached organs. [SOURCE: Page_86]

Ten days after inoculation, the samples were removed from the growth chamber and 5 [SOURCE: Page_86]

mL of an alcoholic solution (containing 20 % ethanol V/V and 100 µL of Tween 80 per liter) [SOURCE: Page_86]

were added to each cap. A centrifuge tube was then screwed onto each cap and the samples [SOURCE: Page_86]

were stored at 4°C until spore counting. Two liquid subsamples were taken from each tube and [SOURCE: Page_86]

spores were counted with a Malassez haemocytometer. Spore production on each detached [SOURCE: Page_86]

organ was then expressed as number of spores per gram fresh weight. [SOURCE: Page_86]

85 [SOURCE: Page_86]

Assessment of pathogenicity of secondary inoculum [SOURCE: Page_87]

Production of secondary inoculum. [SOURCE: Page_87]

Leaf disks and stem segments excised from plants produced under four different N [SOURCE: Page_87]

levels (0.5, 5, 15 and 30 mM nitrate) were inoculated and incubated as described above. Ten [SOURCE: Page_87]

days after inoculation, dry spores were collected from the different organs into Eppendorf tubes [SOURCE: Page_87]

using a microaspirator connected to a vacuum pump in order to recover spores without plant [SOURCE: Page_87]

debris or fluids from macerated tissue. The dry spores were stored at 4°C until use for [SOURCE: Page_87]

inoculation trials (4 days maximum). Immediately before plant inoculation, water suspensions [SOURCE: Page_87]

were prepared from these dry spores. All inocula were filtered through 30-µm mesh sterile [SOURCE: Page_87]

filters (to remove possible mycelial fragments) and adjusted to a concentration of 106 [SOURCE: Page_87]

spores.mL-1. Two such batches of secondary inoculum were produced independently to assess [SOURCE: Page_87]

their pathogenicity. Spores from 14-day old cultures of both strains of B. cinerea on PDA were [SOURCE: Page_87]

used as a reference (31). [SOURCE: Page_87]

Production of plants under a standard fertilization regime. [SOURCE: Page_87]

The pathogenicity of secondary inoculum was tested on two independent batches of [SOURCE: Page_87]

tomato plants (cv Monalbo) produced under a single N regime. The plants were grown in [SOURCE: Page_87]

9 x 9 x 8cm disposable pots containing 450 mL (120g) of a commercial potting mixture [SOURCE: Page_87]

(SP533, Klasmann-Deilmann, Germany) composed of blended white and black sphagnum peat [SOURCE: Page_87]

(containing 5.25g of 14-16-18 N-P-K fertilizer per kg).       The plants were watered daily [SOURCE: Page_87]

according to their needs with a solution containing 0.8% (V/V) of a standard nutrient solution [SOURCE: Page_87]

prepared by mixing 48g of 16-8-18 NPK fertilizer (EDDHA Optiplan, Duclos, France) per liter [SOURCE: Page_87]

of water. They were inoculated after 9 weeks of growth, when they had approximately 9 fully [SOURCE: Page_87]

expanded leaves. [SOURCE: Page_87]

86 [SOURCE: Page_87]

Inoculation with secondary inoculum. [SOURCE: Page_88]

Four plants were used for each type of inoculum and each plant was inoculated with [SOURCE: Page_88]

both strains of B. cinerea. Four leaves were removed on each plant, leaving 10 mm petiole [SOURCE: Page_88]

stubs on the stems to foster the establishment of the pathogen, and 10µL aliquots of spore [SOURCE: Page_88]

suspension were applied to each pruning wound. Wounded petioles of leaves 5 and 7 were [SOURCE: Page_88]

inoculated with strain BC$_1$ and those from leaves 6 and 8 with strain BC21. [SOURCE: Page_88]

Disease assessment. [SOURCE: Page_88]

All plants were incubated in a growth chamber with a 16h (162 μmole m-2s-1) [SOURCE: Page_88]

photoperiod at 21°C and relative humidity above 90%. During this period, plants were watered [SOURCE: Page_88]

once a day. The initiation and length of the resulting stem lesions was monitored daily for 7 [SOURCE: Page_88]

days after inoculation. The area under the disease progress curve (AUDPC) was computed as [SOURCE: Page_88]

described by Lecompte et al. (31) during the period from the third to the seventh day after [SOURCE: Page_88]

inoculation and used for statistical analyses. The pathogenicity assessment experiment was [SOURCE: Page_88]

repeated twice. [SOURCE: Page_88]

Data analysis. [SOURCE: Page_88]

Statistical analyses were carried out using Statistica software (Statsoft Inc., Tulsa, OK, [SOURCE: Page_88]

USA). The ANOVA / MANOVA module was used for analyses of variance and multiple [SOURCE: Page_88]

comparisons of means (tests of Neuman and Keuls). A "trial effect" was tested as all [SOURCE: Page_88]

experiments were carried out twice independently. No significant effect was detected and the [SOURCE: Page_88]

data were pooled for further statistical analyses. Data on spore production were log- [SOURCE: Page_88]

transformed prior to the analyses to stabilize the variance (57). Correlation analyses were [SOURCE: Page_88]

carried out with the Multiple Regression module. [SOURCE: Page_88]

87 [SOURCE: Page_88]

## RESULTS

Influence of plant nutrition on spore production by Botrytis cinerea on diseased leaves and stems. [SOURCE: Page_89]

Both strains of B. cinerea included in this study sporulated abundantly on the two types [SOURCE: Page_89]

of plant tissue tested (Fig. 1). Despite their known difference in aggressiveness on tomato (31), [SOURCE: Page_89]

their level of spore production (expressed as number of spores per gram of fresh tissue 10 days [SOURCE: Page_89]

after inoculation) did not differ significantly (P=0.36). Sporulation of both strains, was [SOURCE: Page_89]

significantly affected by the type of plant tissue colonized (P<0.001), being greater on leaf [SOURCE: Page_89]

disks than on stem segments. Spore production was also significantly affected (P<0.001) on [SOURCE: Page_89]

both plant tissues by the level of N fertilization provided to the tomato plants during their [SOURCE: Page_89]

growth prior to inoculation.       For both strains, spore production globally decreased with [SOURCE: Page_89]

increasing plant N fertilization levels up to 15-30 mM (Fig. 1). [SOURCE: Page_89]

Figure 1: Effect of N nutrition on sporulation of two strains of Botrytis cinerea on leaf disks (   ) and stem segments (       ).   The data represent the average number of spores produced in 10 days per gram fresh tissue. Bars indicate the standard error of the mean (two independent tests x 5 replicates per test). For a given strain and type of plant tissue, different letters indicate significant differences among fertilization levels (Test of Newman-Keuls, P<0.05). [SOURCE: Page_89]

88 [SOURCE: Page_89]

Influence of plant nutrition on the weight and composition of leaf and stem tissues. [SOURCE: Page_90]

The fresh and dry weight of both leaves and stems was significantly increased (P<0.001 [SOURCE: Page_90]

in both cases) by N fertilization up to 15 mM nitrate and then tended to decrease at higher N [SOURCE: Page_90]

rates (Table 1). Leaf content of N, C, P, and Mg was greater than that in stems but leaves were [SOURCE: Page_90]

lower in NO$_3$-, K, and the three sugars (Table 1). Nitrogen fertilization had a very significant [SOURCE: Page_90]

effect (P<0.001) on leaf tissue concentration of all measured components, while no significant [SOURCE: Page_90]

effect was observed for P, K, Mg and Ca in stem tissue. The total N and NO$_3$- content of stems [SOURCE: Page_90]

and leaves increased with increasing N fertilization. Carbon, P, K, glucose and fructose also [SOURCE: Page_90]

tended to increase in leaves but Mg and Ca tended to decrease (Table 1). [SOURCE: Page_90]

Many of the plant components measured were significantly correlated (see [SOURCE: Page_90]

supplementary Tables ST$_1$ and ST$_2$). The most highly correlated components in both plant [SOURCE: Page_90]

organs were glucose with fructose (R=0.97 in leaves and 0.83 in stems; P<0.0001), total N with [SOURCE: Page_90]

NO$_3$- (R=0.93 in leaves and 0.97 in stems; P<0.0001), and either of the two above sugars with [SOURCE: Page_90]

total N or NO$_3$- (R from 0.82 to 0.92 in leaves and from 0.47 to 0.82 in stems; P<0.0001). A [SOURCE: Page_90]

strong negative correlation was found between these four components and Ca in leaves (R from [SOURCE: Page_90]

-0.84 to -0.92; P<0.0001) but not in stems. A strong negative correlation was also found [SOURCE: Page_90]

between sucrose and total N and NO$_3$- (R=-0.76 and -0.73, respectively; P<0.0001) in stems but [SOURCE: Page_90]

not in leaves. [SOURCE: Page_90]

Relationship between tissue content and spore production. [SOURCE: Page_92]

Significant correlations (P<0.05) were observed between spore production by B. cinerea on detached organs of tomato plants and some of the tissue contents (Table 2). The only significant positive correlations were found between calcium content and sporulation on leaf disks (R=0.96) and between sucrose and sporulation on stem segments (R=0.94). Significant negative correlations (R values between -0.89 and -0.92) were found between sporulation on leaf disks and several mineral elements as well as glucose, fructose and total sugars. In contrast, fructose and total sugars were the only components having a significant negative correlation (R=-0.99 and -0.91, respectively) with sporulation on stem segments. [SOURCE: Page_92]

Development of stem cankers. [SOURCE: Page_93]

Canker development (AUDPC) was highly affected by the level of N fertilization [SOURCE: Page_93]

provided to plants that were used as substrates for the production of inoculum (Fig. 2). The [SOURCE: Page_93]

effect of N was more pronounced for the more aggressive strain BC$_1$ (P<0.001) than for the [SOURCE: Page_93]

less aggressive strain BC21 (P<0.05). Interestingly, stem cankers were almost always longer [SOURCE: Page_93]

for inoculum produced on PDA than for secondary inoculum produced on plant tissue (Fig. 2). [SOURCE: Page_93]

92 [SOURCE: Page_93]

on the development of stem cankers on tomato plants cv Monalbo.          The inoculum was [SOURCE: Page_94]

produced on stem segments (A, B) or leaf disks (C, D) from plants subjected to 4 levels of [SOURCE: Page_94]

93 [SOURCE: Page_94]

The severity of lesions (AUDPC) was also significantly affected by the type of plant organ [SOURCE: Page_95]

(leaf disk vs stem segment) used as substrate for spore production (P<0.05). To facilitate the [SOURCE: Page_95]

comparison of the effect of plant N fertilization on the pathogenicity of secondary inoculum for [SOURCE: Page_95]

both strains of B. cinerea from both types of plant substrates relative to inoculum produced on [SOURCE: Page_95]

PDA, an index of relative pathogenicity was computed as the ratio: [SOURCE: Page_95]

RP = 100 * (AUDPC plant substrate X / AUDPC PDA), [SOURCE: Page_95]

where AUDPC plant substrate X assessed the severity of symptoms caused by inoculum produced [SOURCE: Page_95]

on plant substrate X and AUDPC PDA the severity of symptoms caused by inoculum produced [SOURCE: Page_95]

on PDA medium.       The relative pathogenicity of strain BC$_1$ inoculum produced on stem [SOURCE: Page_95]

segments was generally greater than that of inoculum produced on leaf disks (Fig. 3). No clear [SOURCE: Page_95]

differences were observed for strain BC21. For both strains, the relative pathogenicity of [SOURCE: Page_95]

secondary inoculum was highest when produced on organs from plants that received very low [SOURCE: Page_95]

(0.5 mM nitrate) or very high (30 mM nitrate) levels of N fertilization (Fig. 3). Pathogenicity [SOURCE: Page_95]

of inoculum was lowest from plants with moderate levels of N. [SOURCE: Page_95]

(AUDPC) relative to inoculum produced on PDA. [SOURCE: Page_95]

## DISCUSSION

This study showed that the level of N fertilization of tomato plants had a highly [SOURCE: Page_97]

significant impact on the amount of spores produced by B. cinerea on diseased tissue as well as [SOURCE: Page_97]

on the pathogenicity of this secondary inoculum. Sporulation was also affected by the type of [SOURCE: Page_97]

plant tissue used as substrate. While substrate composition has been reported to affect spore [SOURCE: Page_97]

production by B. cinerea and other fungi (7,16,32,35,54), few studies have addressed the effect [SOURCE: Page_97]

of plant fertilization on the production of secondary inoculum on diseased tissue. Infection and [SOURCE: Page_97]

spore production of Blumeria (syn. Erysiphe) graminis (powdery mildew of barley ) was [SOURCE: Page_97]

enhanced with increasing N fertilization (3,25), as was spore production by Oidium [SOURCE: Page_97]

neolycopersici on tomato leaves (22) and uredospore production per leaf lesion by Puccinia [SOURCE: Page_97]

triticina on wheat (42). In contrast, our study of B. cinerea on tomato shows that spore [SOURCE: Page_97]

production was generally highest with low N fertilization (Fig. 1). Overall, this supports the [SOURCE: Page_97]

hypothesis that biotrophs (such as the rusts and powdery mildews) and necrotophs (such as B. [SOURCE: Page_97]

cinerea) may be differentially affected by N fertilization (22,48). However, the plant species [SOURCE: Page_97]

might also influence this since increasing N from 1.8 to 28.8 mM in the irrigation solution of [SOURCE: Page_97]

basil plants increased the incidence of sporulation by B. cinerea on stem segments (from nearly [SOURCE: Page_97]

zero to over 75%) three days after inoculation (55). Different strains of a pathogen might also [SOURCE: Page_97]

be differently affected, although this was not the case for the two strains of B. cinerea used in [SOURCE: Page_97]

our study. [SOURCE: Page_97]

Tissue analyses suggested that differences in N fertilization treatments resulted in [SOURCE: Page_97]

complex differences in the physiological status of the plants that significantly affected fungal [SOURCE: Page_97]

development. As reported in other studies (21,23,55), we also observed that, in addition to total [SOURCE: Page_97]

N and nitrate, the tissue content of soluble sugars and several minerals were significantly [SOURCE: Page_97]

affected by N fertilization. Earlier work on tomatoes reported a positive correlation between [SOURCE: Page_97]

the concentration of total soluble carbohydrates in leaves and the susceptibility of tomato plants [SOURCE: Page_97]

96 [SOURCE: Page_97]

to B. cinerea (21). Effects of plant tissue composition on spore production may be more [SOURCE: Page_98]

complex since the correlations observed for B. cinerea in our study varied depending both on [SOURCE: Page_98]

the type of plant tissue and on the type of sugar or mineral (Table 2). For example, total [SOURCE: Page_98]

soluble sugars (glucose + fructose + sucrose) in our study were negatively correlated with spore [SOURCE: Page_98]

production on both leaf disks and stem segments in contrast to plant susceptibility studied by [SOURCE: Page_98]

Hoffland et al. (21). Furthermore, sucrose concentration was positively correlated with spore [SOURCE: Page_98]

production on stem segments but negatively (not significant) correlated on leaf disks while [SOURCE: Page_98]

glucose was negatively correlated for both types of plant tissues. [SOURCE: Page_98]

Further studies are thus required to understand the mechanisms involved at the level of [SOURCE: Page_98]

gene expression. Expression of the sporulation-regulating brlA gene of Aspergillus nidulans [SOURCE: Page_98]

was reported to be induced by either glucose or N starvation, but with different kinetics and [SOURCE: Page_98]

with differing phenotypical outcomes (46). Similar studies with B. cinerea would be possible, [SOURCE: Page_98]

as orthologs of genes known to function upstream of blrA (including fadA, fluG, flbC and flbD) [SOURCE: Page_98]

have been reported in the genome of this fungus (1). [SOURCE: Page_98]

Trophic interactions related to primary metabolism are unlikely to be the only [SOURCE: Page_98]

mechanisms implicated. Other compounds related to plant defense mechanisms were not [SOURCE: Page_98]

examined in our study but would merit further attention as they could influence sporulation [SOURCE: Page_98]

indirectly through their effect on plant tissue colonization by the pathogen. N nutrition has a [SOURCE: Page_98]

large effect on the amino acid content (30) and constitutive phenolics (51) involved in various [SOURCE: Page_98]

stages of the infection process in tomatoes. In other plant models, peroxidase and chitinase [SOURCE: Page_98]

(two enzymes implicated in plant defense) were lower when N was limiting (11). Further work [SOURCE: Page_98]

could also include the effect of N (and resulting differences in plant tissue constituents) on the [SOURCE: Page_98]

expression of pathogenicity-related genes of B. cinerea.       Nitrogen-limiting conditions can [SOURCE: Page_98]

influence the expression of such genes for several plant pathogenic fungi (47,49) and possibly [SOURCE: Page_98]

for human pathogens such as Aspergillus fumigatus (37). [SOURCE: Page_98]

97 [SOURCE: Page_98]

Beyond an effect on sporulation, we also observed a highly significant effect of N [SOURCE: Page_99]

fertilization and type of plant tissue on the pathogenicity of secondary inoculum produced on [SOURCE: Page_99]

diseased tissue. Since the plants used for assessing pathogenicity were all produced under [SOURCE: Page_99]

identical conditions and were inoculated with the same number of spores free of plant debris or [SOURCE: Page_99]

maceration fluids from their initial production substrates, we hypothesize that the observed [SOURCE: Page_99]

effects arose from actual differences in the spores themselves. [SOURCE: Page_99]

The effect of the growth medium on the composition of spores is well documented. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Studies of the entomopathogenic fungi Beauveria bassiana, Metarhizium anisopliae and [SOURCE: Page_99]

Paecilomyees fumosoroseus showed that the composition of the growth medium influenced [SOURCE: Page_99]

endogenous reserves of total carbohydrates, glycogen and lipids (29), trehalose and mannitol [SOURCE: Page_99]

(39), erythritol (56) and proteins (6,56) in spores. These effects were sometimes accompanied [SOURCE: Page_99]

by differences in the behaviour of the spores, notably their ability to survive various stress [SOURCE: Page_99]

conditions and the kinetics of germination (6,20,40,41). It would be interesting to analyse [SOURCE: Page_99]

possible differences in various metabolites in B. cinerea spores affected by fertilization of the [SOURCE: Page_99]

host plant. However, it is not clear whether a putative difference in the endogenous reserves of [SOURCE: Page_99]

the secondary inoculum could account fully for the differences in pathogenicity observed in our [SOURCE: Page_99]

study. At the early stage of infection, when endogenous reserves of the spores would likely [SOURCE: Page_99]

have the most effect, only small and often non statistically significant differences were [SOURCE: Page_99]

observed in the time needed for initiation of stem cankers (Table 3). In contrast, the subsequent [SOURCE: Page_99]

phase of disease development and lesion expansion were strongly affected (Fig. 2), suggesting [SOURCE: Page_99]

that the effect on the fungus long outlasted the possible mobilization of different endogenous [SOURCE: Page_99]

reserves contained in the initial conidia. The implication of epigenetic phenomena reported for [SOURCE: Page_99]

several other organisms subjected to different nutritional environments would merit more [SOURCE: Page_99]

attention (5,14,59). [SOURCE: Page_99]

98 [SOURCE: Page_99]

In conclusion, the nutritional status of the host plant had a significant impact on the [SOURCE: Page_100]

abundance and pathogenicity of secondary inoculum produced by plant pathogenic B. cinerea. [SOURCE: Page_100]

This information could lead to exciting new studies to elucidate cellular mechanisms involved [SOURCE: Page_100]

and suggests that fertilization can influence the development of epidemics on a field scale. [SOURCE: Page_100]

Conversely, it opens the possibility of manipulating crop fertilization not only to reduce plant [SOURCE: Page_100]

susceptibility (31) but also to limit the impact of secondary inoculum and minimize the need [SOURCE: Page_100]

for pesticide use. [SOURCE: Page_100]

### 2. INRA, UR 1115 Plantes et systèmes de cultures horticoles, Domaine Saint Paul, CS 40509,

F-84914, Avignon cedex, France. [SOURCE: Page_107]

Corresponding author: [EMAIL_REDACTED] [SOURCE: Page_107]

Abstract: Gray mold, caused by Botrytis cinerea, constitutes a recurrent menace for [SOURCE: Page_107]

greenhouse production of tomatoes. Its management can be difficult despite many chemical [SOURCE: Page_107]

treatments and much effort is dedicated to developing alternative control methods and [SOURCE: Page_107]

enhancing their efficacy.    Although nitrogen (N) fertilization is known to affect plant [SOURCE: Page_107]

susceptibility to certain pathogens, little is known on its possible effect on the efficacy of [SOURCE: Page_107]

biological control. In the present study we examined the effect of five levels of N nutrition on [SOURCE: Page_107]

the efficacy of two biocontrol agents (Trichoderma atroviride and Microdochium dimerum) to [SOURCE: Page_107]

protect pruning wounds of tomato against B. cinerea. Plants were grown for two months in a [SOURCE: Page_107]

greenhouse with a soil-less drip-irrigation system. Differential N nutrition was applied for the [SOURCE: Page_107]

last four weeks prior to leaf pruning, treatment of wounds with the biocontrol agents and [SOURCE: Page_107]

inoculation with two strains of B. cinerea. They were then incubated in conditions conducive [SOURCE: Page_107]

to disease development. Plant fertilization strongly affected disease development for both [SOURCE: Page_107]

strains of the pathogen and it influenced the efficacy of both biocontrol agents. Disease [SOURCE: Page_107]

severity decreased with increasing N fertilization up to 10 mmol of nitrate per liter of nutrient [SOURCE: Page_107]

solution. High nitrogen fertilization also increased biocontrol efficacy by delaying symptom [SOURCE: Page_107]

development on the stems and slowing down lesion expansion. The extent of this effect [SOURCE: Page_107]

106 [SOURCE: Page_107]

depended on the biocontrol agent and on the strain of the pathogen. To our knowledge this is [SOURCE: Page_108]

the first report of an effect of N fertilization on the efficacy of biocontrol against an airborne [SOURCE: Page_108]

disease. It suggests that manipulating N fertilization could be a new useful tool for integrated [SOURCE: Page_108]

protection of greenhouse tomato, not only through reducing plant susceptibility to gray mold, [SOURCE: Page_108]

but also by enhancing the efficacy of biocontrol agents. [SOURCE: Page_108]

Key words: Biological control, Tomato, Trichoderma atroviride, Microdochium dimerum, [SOURCE: Page_108]

Botrytis cinerea, Nitrogen fertilization. [SOURCE: Page_108]
