## 1. INTRODUCTION

Reducing the reliance on pesticide use for crop protection is a generally acclaimed goal. Its [SOURCE: Page_109]

achievement, however, requires the availability of efficacious alternative methods for growers [SOURCE: Page_109]

to limit the risk of yield losses from pests and diseases. Much hope is placed on biological [SOURCE: Page_109]

control and commercial products are increasingly available against arthropod pests and more [SOURCE: Page_109]

recently against diseases (van Lenteren 2012; Nicot 2011). For disease control, a lower and [SOURCE: Page_109]

less regular level of efficacy, compared to pesticides, is often cited as one of the obstacles to [SOURCE: Page_109]

the commercial development of biological control agents (BCAs) and their wide adoption by [SOURCE: Page_109]

growers (Nicot 2011). Much research has been dedicated to enhancing the efficacy and [SOURCE: Page_109]

reliability of BCAs.    One approach has consisted in improving the performance of the [SOURCE: Page_109]

microbial BCA strains through mutagenesis or genetic manipulation (Clermont et al. 2011) or [SOURCE: Page_109]

through optimizing nutrient substrate composition and conditions applied during biomass [SOURCE: Page_109]

production (Jackson et al. 1991). Another approach may be to apply various compounds [SOURCE: Page_109]

(including calcium salts, chitosan, amino acids and carbohydrates) on the plants together with [SOURCE: Page_109]

the BCA (El-Ghaouth et al. 2002; Guetsky et al. 2002; Janisiewicz et al. 1992).              Soil [SOURCE: Page_109]

amendments with various organic compounds, including chitin, have also been shown to [SOURCE: Page_109]

improve biocontrol efficacy of soilborne diseases (Barakat 2008; Lopez-Mondejar et al. [SOURCE: Page_109]

2012). Surprisingly, little information is available on the possible role of mineral plant [SOURCE: Page_109]

fertilization on the efficacy of BCAs, although its effect on the susceptibility of the host plant [SOURCE: Page_109]

to pathogens is well documented for a variety of crops and diseases (Datnoff et al. 2007). [SOURCE: Page_109]

In a recent study, we showed that the susceptibility of tomato to Botrytis cinerea, a [SOURCE: Page_109]

fungus responsible for economically important diseases of a variety of crops, was [SOURCE: Page_109]

significantly influenced by nitrogen (N) fertilization (Lecompte et al. 2010). N fertilization [SOURCE: Page_109]

also had an impact on the abundance of spore production by B. cinerea on diseased plants and [SOURCE: Page_109]

on the pathogenicity of that secondary inoculum (Abro et al., 2012). For greenhouse tomato [SOURCE: Page_109]

108 [SOURCE: Page_109]

production, this fungus constitutes a recurrent menace. Its management can be difficult [SOURCE: Page_110]

despite many chemical treatments and much effort is dedicated to developing alternative [SOURCE: Page_110]

control methods and enhancing their efficacy. [SOURCE: Page_110]

The objectives of the present study were thus to investigate a possible effect of N [SOURCE: Page_110]

fertilization on the efficacy of two beneficial fungi shown in previous work to provide [SOURCE: Page_110]

protection of tomato pruning wounds against B. cinerea (Nicot et al. 2003; Bardin et al. [SOURCE: Page_110]

2008). For both BCAs, we observed enhanced biocontrol at high N fertilization levels, even [SOURCE: Page_110]

in the presence of high inoculum levels of the pathogen. [SOURCE: Page_110]

109 [SOURCE: Page_110]

## 2. MATERIALS AND METHODS

2.1 Plant production [SOURCE: Page_111]

Tomato plants were produced under five differential N nutrition regimes as described before [SOURCE: Page_111]

(Lecompte et al. 2010). In short, seeds of cv Swanson were sown in 1 cm^3 rockwool plugs in [SOURCE: Page_111]

a greenhouse. Ten days after sowing, the plugs were transferred onto rockwool blocks and [SOURCE: Page_111]

the plantlets (one per block) were fertigated twice a day with a standard commercial nutrient [SOURCE: Page_111]

solution (Duclos international Lunel, France). After one month, the plants (bearing 3-4 [SOURCE: Page_111]

leaves) were placed on the top of 2 liter pots filled with a mixture (1:1 V/V) of vermiculite [SOURCE: Page_111]

and pozzolana to start the nutrition treatments. Five levels of nitrate concentration were [SOURCE: Page_111]

tested in the fertilization solution: 0.5, 2, 5, 10 and 20 mmol.L-1 (abbreviated as mM in this [SOURCE: Page_111]

paper). The plants were fertigated with a drip irrigation system (one dripper per pot) at a [SOURCE: Page_111]

frequency adapted to the climatic demand.          Two batches of 200 plants (40 for each [SOURCE: Page_111]

fertilization regime) were produced independently in 2009 and 2010 to provide independent [SOURCE: Page_111]

repetitions of the whole study. [SOURCE: Page_111]

2.2 Plant tissue analysis [SOURCE: Page_111]

From each batch of 200 plants, five were randomly selected for each N nutrition regime just [SOURCE: Page_111]

before the inoculation assays. These samples were used to measure fresh and dry weight of [SOURCE: Page_111]

aerial parts and for analyses of mineral content of stem tissues as described before (Lecompte [SOURCE: Page_111]

et al., 2010). Seven tissue components were measured: total nitrogen (N), total carbon (C), [SOURCE: Page_111]

nitrate (NO$_3$-), phosphorous (P), potassium (K), magnesium (Mg) and calcium (Ca). In [SOURCE: Page_111]

addition, glucose, fructose and sucrose contents were determined with an enzymatic method, [SOURCE: Page_111]

as proposed by Gomez et al. (2007). [SOURCE: Page_111]

110 [SOURCE: Page_111]

2.3 Biocontrol agents [SOURCE: Page_112]

We used two fungal species known from previous work in our laboratory for their protective [SOURCE: Page_112]

effect against B. cinerea on tomato. Strain L13 of Microdochium dimerum, isolated from [SOURCE: Page_112]

pruning wounds on tomato (Nicot et al., 2002) was grown on yeast peptone glucose agar [SOURCE: Page_112]

(YPGA) containing per liter: 7g BactoTM yeast extract, 7g BBLTM GelysateTM peptone, 7g [SOURCE: Page_112]

glucose and 15g agar.     Strain T$_1$ of T. atroviride, kindly provided by Agrauxine, was [SOURCE: Page_112]

produced on DifcoTM potato dextrose agar (PDA). Both fungi were grown in a growth [SOURCE: Page_112]

chamber (21°C and 16-hour photoperiod; 162 μmole m-2s-1). Spores were collected in sterile [SOURCE: Page_112]

distilled water from the surface of 14-day old cultures and suspensions adjusted to 107 [SOURCE: Page_112]

spores.mL-1 were prepared for each strain. [SOURCE: Page_112]

2.3 Plant treatments with biocontrol agents [SOURCE: Page_112]

From each regime of N fertilization, two groups of 10 plants were treated with either [SOURCE: Page_112]

biocontrol agent and one group was used as control.        On each plant, four leaves were [SOURCE: Page_112]

removed, leaving 5-10 mm petiole stubs on the stems. The pruning wounds were either left [SOURCE: Page_112]

untreated (control plants) or treated with 10µL aliquots of a spore suspension of T. atroviride [SOURCE: Page_112]

or M. dimerum. Immediately after treatment, the plants were inoculated with B. cinerea. [SOURCE: Page_112]

2.4 Production of Botrytis cinerea inoculum [SOURCE: Page_112]

We used two strains of B. cinerea with contrasted aggressiveness on tomato. From previous [SOURCE: Page_112]

work of the laboratory, strain BC$_1$ was known to have a high level of aggressiveness on [SOURCE: Page_112]

tomato, while strain BC21 had a medium to low level of aggressiveness (Lecompte et al., [SOURCE: Page_112]

2010). Inoculum was produced on PDA in a growth chamber as described above. Spores [SOURCE: Page_112]

111 [SOURCE: Page_112]

were collected in sterile distilled water from the surface of 14-day old cultures and [SOURCE: Page_113]

suspensions were prepared and adjusted to 106 and 107 spores.mL-1 for each strain. As the [SOURCE: Page_113]

objective of the study was to investigate the possibility of manipulating N fertilization to [SOURCE: Page_113]

improve the efficiency of biocontrol, high inoculum concentrations of B. cinerea were used [SOURCE: Page_113]

purposefully to put the BCAs in a difficult situation. [SOURCE: Page_113]

2.5 Inoculations [SOURCE: Page_113]

For each treatment and N fertilization level, five plants were inoculated with strain BC$_1$ and [SOURCE: Page_113]

five with strain BC21. On a given plant, the pruning wounds of the third and fifth leaves [SOURCE: Page_113]

(numbered from the bottom up) were inoculated with 10 µL aliquots of suspension containing [SOURCE: Page_113]

107 spores.mL-1 and the fourth and sixth leaves with suspensions containing 106 spores.mL-1. [SOURCE: Page_113]

Following inoculation, the plants were incubated for 7 days in controlled conditions at 21°C, [SOURCE: Page_113]

90% relative humidity under 14 hours of photoperiod as described before (Lecompte et al., [SOURCE: Page_113]

2010). During this period, they were irrigated manually, using the same fertilization solutions [SOURCE: Page_113]

as those used before inoculation. [SOURCE: Page_113]

2.6 Disease assessment and data analysis [SOURCE: Page_113]

The plants were examined daily from the 3rd to the 7th day after inoculation and the infection [SOURCE: Page_113]

of petiole stubs, the initiation and length of resulting stem lesions were monitored. The [SOURCE: Page_113]

latency period was calculated as the number of days between inoculation and lesion [SOURCE: Page_113]

appearance on stems. If a lesion was visible on the third day after inoculation, the latency [SOURCE: Page_113]

period was a given a value of 3 days; if no lesion was visible on the last day of disease [SOURCE: Page_113]

assessment, it was a given a value of 8 days. On each inoculation site, the rate of lesion [SOURCE: Page_113]

expansion (mm per day) was calculated as the average daily increase in the length of the [SOURCE: Page_113]

112 [SOURCE: Page_113]

lesions between their appearance on the stem and the last day of measurement. It was thus [SOURCE: Page_114]

estimated based on 1 to 5 measurements of lesion size depending on the day of lesion [SOURCE: Page_114]

appearance. The area under the disease progress curve (AUDPC) was computed over the 5 [SOURCE: Page_114]

days of disease rating as described by Lecompte et al. (2010). The whole experiment was [SOURCE: Page_114]

conducted twice on independently produced batches of plants. Analysis of variance was used [SOURCE: Page_114]

to compare the two trials and to test for an effect of fertilization on disease development. [SOURCE: Page_114]

When appropriate, the means were compared with the test of Newman and Keuls.             To [SOURCE: Page_114]

compare the efficacy of the BCAs, a protection index was computed as 100 * (AUDPC untreated [SOURCE: Page_114]

– AUDPC biocontrol)/AUDPC untreated, where AUDPC biocontrol and AUDPC untreated were the [SOURCE: Page_114]

average AUDPC (for plants treated with a BCA or for control plants left untreated, [SOURCE: Page_114]

respectively) computed for a given BCA and N fertilization level combination.           The [SOURCE: Page_114]

correlation between disease severity (AUDPC) and tissue content in the tomato stems for [SOURCE: Page_114]

different minerals and sugars was investigated by using Pearson’s correlation coefficients. [SOURCE: Page_114]

The Statistica software package was used for all data analyses. [SOURCE: Page_114]

113 [SOURCE: Page_114]

## 3 RESULTS AND DISCUSSION

3.1 Effect of nitrogen fertilization on disease severity [SOURCE: Page_115]

On the untreated control plants, the pathogen colonized the petiole stubs and eventually [SOURCE: Page_115]

developed stem cankers on all inoculation sites in both replicate experiments. In contrast, [SOURCE: Page_115]

some of the wounds treated with either biocontrol agent were protected (despite the [SOURCE: Page_115]

purposefully high level of B. cinerea inoculum used in the study), resulting in no or reduced [SOURCE: Page_115]

disease development. As expected from previous work (Lecompte et al., 2010), disease [SOURCE: Page_115]

severity was significantly higher on plants inoculated with strain BC$_1$ than BC21 of B. [SOURCE: Page_115]

cinerea, regardless of inoculum concentration (Fig. 1). A global analysis of variance on [SOURCE: Page_115]

AUDPC (r^2=0.78, F=108, p<0.0001, 1199 DF), accounting for five classification variables (B. [SOURCE: Page_115]

cinerea strain, inoculum concentration, biocontrol, N fertilization level and experiment) and [SOURCE: Page_115]

their interactions, yielded significant effects for all variables and interactions, except the [SOURCE: Page_115]

“concentration x experiment” interaction (Table 1). Although significant differences between [SOURCE: Page_115]

experiments were observed, the contribution of this factor to the global model variance was [SOURCE: Page_115]

low as compared to other ones. Therefore, for the sake of conciseness and clarity, the major [SOURCE: Page_115]

effects described below will be presented with mixed data from the two experiments; however [SOURCE: Page_115]

differences between experiments will be pointed out when necessary       . [SOURCE: Page_115]

114 [SOURCE: Page_115]

115 [SOURCE: Page_116]

For both strains of B. cinerea and both inoculum concentrations, in the two [SOURCE: Page_117]

experiments, disease severity was significantly affected by the level of N fertilization [SOURCE: Page_117]

provided to the plants. On control plants with no BCA, severity decreased significantly (P [SOURCE: Page_117]

values < 0.0001 in all situations) with increasing N fertilization level up to 5 mM, and in [SOURCE: Page_117]

some cases at higher N rates (Fig. 1). These results are in agreement with those of earlier [SOURCE: Page_117]

work showing that high N fertilization reduced the severity of B. cinerea on tomato (Hoffland [SOURCE: Page_117]

et al., 1999; Lecompte et al., 2010). In the presence of either BCA (M. dimerum and T. [SOURCE: Page_117]

atroviride), the AUDPC also decreased significantly with increasing N fertilization (up to 5- [SOURCE: Page_117]

20 mM, Fig. 1). For plants treated with M. dimerum, differences between experiments were [SOURCE: Page_117]

observed. In the 2010 experiment, disease severity was already somewhat low at low N [SOURCE: Page_117]

levels and decreased only slightly with increasing N fertilization. In contrast, the decrease of [SOURCE: Page_117]

disease severity with increasing N levels was more pronounced in 2009. [SOURCE: Page_117]

116 [SOURCE: Page_117]

Fig. 1 Effect of nitrogen fertilization on the severity of disease caused by two strains [SOURCE: Page_118]

of B. cinerea (highly aggressive strain BC$_1$ and mildly aggressive strain BC21) on stems of [SOURCE: Page_118]

tomato plants. The plants were inoculated with the pathogen alone ( ) at two different spore [SOURCE: Page_118]

concentrations, or together with biocontrol agent Trichoderma atroviride ( ) or [SOURCE: Page_118]

Microdochium dimerum ( ). Disease severity (represented by the area under the disease [SOURCE: Page_118]

progress curve) was differentially reduced in the presence of the biocontrol agents. In all [SOURCE: Page_118]

cases, severity of disease decreased with increasing N fertilization levels. The bars represent [SOURCE: Page_118]

the standard error of the means. [SOURCE: Page_118]

117 [SOURCE: Page_118]

3.2 Effect of nitrogen fertilization on the efficacy of biocontrol [SOURCE: Page_119]

The efficacy of biocontrol globally increased with increasing N fertilization; however [SOURCE: Page_119]

variations depending on the strain, the concentration of the pathogen's inoculum and the [SOURCE: Page_119]

species of BCA were observed (Fig. 2). M. dimerum provided higher levels of protection than [SOURCE: Page_119]

T. atroviride and the efficacy of either BCA was higher against the mildly aggressive than [SOURCE: Page_119]

against the highly aggressive strain of B. cinerea.       In the presence of high B. cinerea [SOURCE: Page_119]

inoculum concentration, the efficacy of both BCA was low for plants grown at low N (Fig. [SOURCE: Page_119]

2A). The protection index of M. dimerum increased sharply with increasing N fertilization, to [SOURCE: Page_119]

reach nearly 80% and 100%, respectively, against the highly and the mildly aggressive strains [SOURCE: Page_119]

of B. cinerea, while that of T. atroviride increased only moderately. At the lower inoculum [SOURCE: Page_119]

concentration (Fig. 2B), M. dimerum achieved good disease control at low N levels, and [SOURCE: Page_119]

almost fully protected the plant against both strains of B. cinerea at high N. Increasing N also [SOURCE: Page_119]

improved the protective effect of T. atroviride against mildly aggressive strain BC21, but only [SOURCE: Page_119]

limited protection was achieved against strain BC$_1$, even at high N fertilization. [SOURCE: Page_119]

Few studies have examined the effect of soil fertilization on biocontrol. Khattabi et al. [SOURCE: Page_119]

(2004) reported that soil amendment with four types of N fertilizers resulted in decreased [SOURCE: Page_119]

survival of sclerotia of soilborne pathogen Sclerotium rolfsii and that the antagonistic activity [SOURCE: Page_119]

of biocontrol fungus T. harzianum against those sclerotia increased with increasing N doses. [SOURCE: Page_119]

N amendments in nutrient agar media have also been shown to increase in vitro biocontrol [SOURCE: Page_119]

activity of certain microorganisms. [SOURCE: Page_119]

118 [SOURCE: Page_119]

provided by two biocontrol agents against two strains of B. cinerea (BC$_1$ and BC21). The [SOURCE: Page_120]

plants were inoculated with the pathogen alone at two different spore concentrations (A: 107 [SOURCE: Page_120]

or B: 106 spores.mL-1), or together with biocontrol agent Trichoderma atroviride (Ta: ) or [SOURCE: Page_120]

Microdochium dimerum (Md:          ).   The efficacy of biocontrol was enhanced by high N [SOURCE: Page_120]

fertilization levels. The bars represent the standard error of the means. [SOURCE: Page_120]

119 [SOURCE: Page_120]

To gain a better understanding on the possible phenomena implicated in the similar effect [SOURCE: Page_121]

observed in our study, two key steps of pathogenesis were examined more closely, one (the [SOURCE: Page_121]

time needed for symptom development) reflecting the first interactions between the plant, the [SOURCE: Page_121]

pathogen and the BCA, and the second (the rate of stem lesion expansion) reflecting longer [SOURCE: Page_121]

interactions over several days between the three organisms. [SOURCE: Page_121]

3.3 Effect of nitrogen fertilization on the latency period [SOURCE: Page_121]

On untreated control plants the latency period increased significantly with increasing N [SOURCE: Page_121]

fertilization, slightly for BC$_1$ and more importantly for BC21 (Fig. 3, P values < 0.0001 in all [SOURCE: Page_121]

situations). This confirmed observations from our earlier work (Lecompte et al., 2010). [SOURCE: Page_121]

On plants treated with either BCA, the latent period was also significantly affected by [SOURCE: Page_121]

N fertilization (Fig. 3, P values < 0.002 in all situations). In the presence of M. dimerum, the [SOURCE: Page_121]

latency for both strains of B. cinerea was significantly higher than on control plants at N [SOURCE: Page_121]

levels of 5 mM and above when the inoculum concentration was high and at all N levels when [SOURCE: Page_121]

the inoculum concentration was low. In the presence of T. atroviride, the latency of B. [SOURCE: Page_121]

cinerea was similar to the control at 0.5 and 2 mM NO$_3$- in all situations, and significantly [SOURCE: Page_121]

higher at N levels of 5mM and above, except for highly aggressive strain BC$_1$ at high [SOURCE: Page_121]

inoculum concentration. [SOURCE: Page_121]

120 [SOURCE: Page_121]

Fig. 3 Effect of nitrogen fertilization on the latency period before symptom [SOURCE: Page_122]

appearance on stems of tomato plants, following inoculation by two strains of B. cinerea [SOURCE: Page_122]

(highly aggressive strain BC$_1$ and mildly aggressive strain BC21). The plants were inoculated [SOURCE: Page_122]

with the pathogen alone ( ) at two different spore concentrations, or together with biocontrol [SOURCE: Page_122]

agent Trichoderma atroviride ( ) or Microdochium dimerum ( ). The latency period was [SOURCE: Page_122]

differentially affected depending on the strain of the pathogen and the biocontrol agent. In all [SOURCE: Page_122]

cases the latency period was higher at high N fertilization levels. The bars represent the [SOURCE: Page_122]

standard error of the means . [SOURCE: Page_122]

121 [SOURCE: Page_122]

This differential effect of N fertilization on the latent period, dependant both on the strain of [SOURCE: Page_123]

B. cinerea and on the strain of BCA, reflects the outcome of early interactions between the [SOURCE: Page_123]

three organisms, leading to the emergence of stem symptoms. Overall, for both BCA, the [SOURCE: Page_123]

increase in the latency period with increasing N level paralleled that of overall efficacy of [SOURCE: Page_123]

biocontrol. This suggests that the early interactions between the BCA, the plant and the [SOURCE: Page_123]

pathogen were important for the outcome of wound protection. It is also compatible with the [SOURCE: Page_123]

observation by O'Neil et al. (1997), on tomato plants in absence of any protective treatment [SOURCE: Page_123]

that the susceptibility of stems to infection by B. cinerea declined sharply with wound age [SOURCE: Page_123]

within the first day after leaf removal. [SOURCE: Page_123]

3.4 Effect of nitrogen fertilization on lesion expansion [SOURCE: Page_123]

On untreated control plants inoculated with highly aggressive strain BC$_1$, the daily rate of [SOURCE: Page_123]

lesion expansion decreased significantly with increasing N fertilization regardless of [SOURCE: Page_123]

inoculum concentration (p<0.001 in both cases; no effect of inoculum concentration, p>0.58; [SOURCE: Page_123]

averaged data shown in Table 2).           Conversely, lesion expansion increased slightly with [SOURCE: Page_123]

increasing N levels above 2 mM for strain BC21 (Table 2), as observed in earlier work [SOURCE: Page_123]

contrasting highly and mildly aggressive strains of B. cinerea (Lecompte et al 2010). [SOURCE: Page_123]

On plants treated with either BCA, lesion expansion was also significantly affected by N [SOURCE: Page_123]

fertilization and the strain of B. cinerea, but not by the concentration of inoculum (P > 0.80). [SOURCE: Page_123]

123 [SOURCE: Page_124]

For plants inoculated with highly aggressive strain BC$_1$, significant differences between [SOURCE: Page_125]

control and BCA-treated plants were mostly observed at high N levels, with reductions in [SOURCE: Page_125]

lesion expansion amounting to 65-73 % for M. dimerum and nearly 50% for T. atroviride [SOURCE: Page_125]

(Table 1). Analyzing the influence of N fertilization on the reduction in lesion expansion by [SOURCE: Page_125]

strain BC21 was complicated by the fact that no stem lesions developed at high N levels in [SOURCE: Page_125]

the presence of M. dimerum and only in 2009 for T. atroviride. A significant reduction in [SOURCE: Page_125]

lesion expansion by BC21 was only observed in the presence of T. atroviride on plants grown [SOURCE: Page_125]

at the highest N level (Table 2). [SOURCE: Page_125]

Thus, even if the major effect of plant N nutrition on biocontrol efficacy mostly [SOURCE: Page_125]

occurred at the early infection phase, marked by a delay in symptom development, a reduction [SOURCE: Page_125]

in lesion expansion was able, in some cases, to contribute to increased biocontrol at high N [SOURCE: Page_125]

level when stems became infected. [SOURCE: Page_125]

3.5 Relationship between stem tissue content and the disease severity [SOURCE: Page_125]

A difference in N fertilization levels resulted in significant differences in fresh and dry weight [SOURCE: Page_125]

(g) as well as in tissue content of eight of the ten components quantified in the tomato stems [SOURCE: Page_125]

(Table 3). Dry and fresh weight, total N, and NO$_3$- increased with increasing nitrate level up [SOURCE: Page_125]

to 20 mM in the irrigation solution, while fructose increased significantly up to 5-10 mM, and [SOURCE: Page_125]

C was not significantly influenced, confirming some of the results of earlier work (Lecompte [SOURCE: Page_125]

et al., 2010). [SOURCE: Page_125]

Highly significant correlations (P<0.001) were observed between disease severity [SOURCE: Page_125]

(AUDPC) and the concentration in the stems (mg per gram dry matter) of some of the tissue [SOURCE: Page_125]

components: negative correlations with total N, NO$_3$-, and fructose, and positive with sucrose [SOURCE: Page_125]

(Table 4). However, these four tissue components were significantly auto correlated, with [SOURCE: Page_125]

124 [SOURCE: Page_125]

highly positive correlations between N, NO$_3$- and fructose content, and highly negative [SOURCE: Page_126]

correlations between sucrose and the other three components, making it difficult to point to [SOURCE: Page_126]

putative causal effects. Ccorrelations between susceptibility or resistance to diseases and [SOURCE: Page_126]

various indicators of nitrogen status of the host plant (amino acids, nucleic acids, phenols, [SOURCE: Page_126]

total N, protein content, C / N, etc) have been reported before in other plant-pathogen systems [SOURCE: Page_126]

(Huber and Thompson, 2007).         Also, a relationship between plant sugar content and [SOURCE: Page_126]

127 [SOURCE: Page_128]

susceptibility has been suggested by Bolton, (2009). Interestingly, no correlation was [SOURCE: Page_129]

observed between disease severity and total soluble sugars, contrarily to the results of [SOURCE: Page_129]

Hoffland et al. (1999) with total soluble carbohydrates, suggesting that other compounds than [SOURCE: Page_129]

those measured in our study (glucose, fructose, sucrose) may be implicated, or that only some [SOURCE: Page_129]

of the soluble sugars, fructose and sucrose, are implicated in the response to infection. The [SOURCE: Page_129]

recent description of a mutant of B. cinerea incapable of using fructose as a sole carbon [SOURCE: Page_129]

source (Aguayo et al., 2011) should provide a useful tool to investigate this subject further. [SOURCE: Page_129]

Competition for nutrients is a common mode of action of biocontrol agents on plant [SOURCE: Page_129]

surfaces. Although no information is available for the two BCAs included in our study, [SOURCE: Page_129]

competition for fructose and nitrate has been identified as a factor in the mode of action of [SOURCE: Page_129]

certain BCAs (Jacobsen, 2006). However, in our study, repeated analyses of covariance on [SOURCE: Page_129]

disease severity showed that there were no interactions between plant treatments (with either [SOURCE: Page_129]

BCA or untreated controls) and the various plant components identified, as the slopes relating [SOURCE: Page_129]

AUDPC to each of the individual plant components were similar for plants inoculated with B. [SOURCE: Page_129]

cinerea alone or with B. cinerea and a BCA (graphs not shown). This indicated that the [SOURCE: Page_129]

effects of these components were not different for plants inoculated with B. cinerea alone or [SOURCE: Page_129]

with B. cinerea plus a BCA, and conflicted with the hypothesis of a direct effect of the plant [SOURCE: Page_129]

components on biocontrol. Rather, the results suggest that the tissue content affected the [SOURCE: Page_129]

capacity of the pathogen to infect the plant, and that the outcome of the biocontrol was in turn [SOURCE: Page_129]

affected by this modified interaction between the host and the pathogen. This hypothesis is in [SOURCE: Page_129]

accordance with the observation that at each N regime, the efficacy of biocontrol was directly [SOURCE: Page_129]

related to the pressure exerted by the pathogen, in terms of inoculum concentration and strain [SOURCE: Page_129]

aggressiveness. Increasing the plant N content would either enhance plant defenses or reduce [SOURCE: Page_129]

the ability of the fungus to colonize the tissue, and by this direct reduction of fungal growth, [SOURCE: Page_129]

indirectly facilitate the action of the BCAs and hence their efficacy. Thus, the novel results [SOURCE: Page_129]

128 [SOURCE: Page_129]

presented here on the effect of N fertilization on the efficacy of two biocontrol agents against [SOURCE: Page_130]

B. cinerea should stimulate further work to better understand which mechanisms are involved [SOURCE: Page_130]

at the cellular and molecular level. [SOURCE: Page_130]

## 4 CONCLUSIONS:

The results of this study clearly indicated that the level of N fertilization provided to the plant [SOURCE: Page_130]

can influence not only the susceptibility of tomato stems to B. cinerea but also the efficacy of [SOURCE: Page_130]

biocontrol. High N fertilization enhanced the efficacy of both biocontrol agents tested against [SOURCE: Page_130]

B. cinerea. To our knowledge this is the first report of an effect of N fertilization on the [SOURCE: Page_130]

efficacy of biocontrol against an airborne disease. It suggests that the manipulation of N [SOURCE: Page_130]

fertilization could provide a new useful tool for integrated protection of greenhouse tomato. [SOURCE: Page_130]

To devise specific recommendations for growers, further work is needed to validate the [SOURCE: Page_130]

present results in conditions of commercial production and to evaluate possible side effects on [SOURCE: Page_130]

other diseases or pests in the tomato greenhouse. [SOURCE: Page_130]

participation in the experiments and Doriane Bancel, Emilie Rubio and Sylvie Serino for the [SOURCE: Page_130]

biochemical analyses. This work is part of the French 'Programme National de Recherche [SOURCE: Page_130]

PIClèg' and the SYSBIOTEL project. It received financial support from INRA and the [SOURCE: Page_130]

French 'Agence Nationale de la Recherche' (ANR). A grant for this study was also provided [SOURCE: Page_130]

by the Higher Education Commission of Pakistan. [SOURCE: Page_130]

### 2012. Cyto-histological evidences for the influence of nitrogen fertilization on the

efficacy of biological control against Botrytis cinerea in tomato. Biocontrol (To be [SOURCE: Page_134]

submitted) [SOURCE: Page_134]

133 [SOURCE: Page_134]

Cyto-histological evidences for the influence of nitrogen fertilization on the [SOURCE: Page_135]

efficacy of biological control against Botrytis cinerea in tomato [SOURCE: Page_135]

Manzoor Ali Abro(1), Philippe Nicot (1), Francois Lecompte (2), and Mohamed El Maataoui(3) [SOURCE: Page_135]

(1) INRA, UR407 Pathologie végétale, Domaine Saint Maurice, BP 94, F84143 Montfavet cedex, France. (2) INRA, UR 1115 Plantes et systèmes de cultures horticoles, Domaine Saint Paul, CS 40509, F-84914, Avignon cedex, France. (3) Laboratoire de Physiologie des Fruits et Légumes, Université d'Avignon, BP 21239, F- 84916 Avignon Cedex 9 France. [SOURCE: Page_135]

Corresponding author: [EMAIL_REDACTED] [SOURCE: Page_135]

Abstract. [SOURCE: Page_135]

Tomato stem cankers due to Botrytis cinerea is a serious problem causing plant death and [SOURCE: Page_135]

decreased yields in greenhouses. The disease control by fungicides encounters some failures [SOURCE: Page_135]

due to pathogen resistance. The use of biological control agents may be a good alternative to [SOURCE: Page_135]

overcome this problem but the success of this method is thought to depend on numerous [SOURCE: Page_135]

factors especially the nutritional status of the host plant. The present study was conducted to [SOURCE: Page_135]

assess at histological levels the interaction between nitrogen fertilization of tomato with [SOURCE: Page_135]

Botrytis infection and the efficacy of biological control with Trichoderma atroviride and [SOURCE: Page_135]

Microdochium dimerum. Tomato plants were fertigated with three N regimes (0.5, 5, and 20 [SOURCE: Page_135]

mM NO$_3$-) and inoculated with Botrytis alone or in combination with the biological control [SOURCE: Page_135]

agents. The performed cytological studies using light microscopy showed that at high N [SOURCE: Page_135]

fertilization the severity of disease decreased due to structural alteration of Botrytis mycelial [SOURCE: Page_135]

cells. Biological control with T. atroviride also decreased the disease severity compared to [SOURCE: Page_135]

control especially at high N fertilization. At cellular levels this was associated with profound [SOURCE: Page_135]

mycelial cell destructions in form of vacuolisation, glycogen deposition and mycelial cell [SOURCE: Page_135]

134 [SOURCE: Page_135]

death.   Similar cyto-histological alterations occurred with M. dimerum.         However, this [SOURCE: Page_136]

biological control agent proved to be more effective in comparison with T. atroviride. Taken [SOURCE: Page_136]

together the results suggested that not only high N levels interfere with mycelial development [SOURCE: Page_136]

of B. cinerea but also improve the effectiveness of biological control agents. [SOURCE: Page_136]

Key words: Botrytis cinerea, tomato, nitrogen fertilization, biological control, cytology, [SOURCE: Page_136]

mycelial structure [SOURCE: Page_136]

135 [SOURCE: Page_136]

Introduction [SOURCE: Page_137]

Grey mould caused by Botrytis cinerea is a major problem in greenhouse tomato (Solanum [SOURCE: Page_137]

lycopersicum) production causing serious economic losses (Jarvis 1989). It is able to invade [SOURCE: Page_137]

the plant tissues directly or through pruning wounds that results in stem cankers and rapid [SOURCE: Page_137]

plant death (Decognet et al. 2010). The disease in mainly controlled via chemical treatments [SOURCE: Page_137]

but many classes of synthetic fungicides have failed due to the genetic variability of the [SOURCE: Page_137]

pathogen (Williamson et al. 2007). Another alternative method to control B. cinerea on [SOURCE: Page_137]

tomato is by means of biological control agents (Eden et al. 1996; Elad et al. 1995; Nicot et [SOURCE: Page_137]

al. 2003b). Biological control agents significantly reduced the incidence of stem lesions on [SOURCE: Page_137]

tomatoes (Decognet et al. 1999a). For example, the efficacy of Microdochium dimerum has [SOURCE: Page_137]

been shown in earlier studies to provide high levels of protection on pruning wounds of [SOURCE: Page_137]

tomato in green house conditions (Nicot et al. 2003b). Trichoderma harzianum controls [SOURCE: Page_137]

various plant pathogens including B. cinerea in tomato (Eden et al. 1996; Elad et al. 1999b) [SOURCE: Page_137]

(Harman et al. 2004). [SOURCE: Page_137]

It is well known that the nutritional status of the host plants affects their susceptibility [SOURCE: Page_137]

to pathogens (Datnoff et al. 2007). However, the effect of nitrogen (abbreviated as N in this [SOURCE: Page_137]

paper) fertilization on susceptibility to Botrytis is controversial (Dik and Wubben 2004; [SOURCE: Page_137]

Yermiyahu et al. 2006). For example, elevated N concentrations in the fertilizer solution [SOURCE: Page_137]

increased disease severity of Botrytis on strawberry (Nam et al. 2006), in Begonia (Pitchay et [SOURCE: Page_137]

al. 2007), and in sweet basil (Yermiyahu et al. 2006). In contrast to high susceptibility at [SOURCE: Page_137]

higher N in tomato-B. cinerea pathosystem (Lecompte et al. 2010) found that high N [SOURCE: Page_137]

fertilization lowers the level of disease severity. Similarly, (Hoffland et al. 1999) revealed [SOURCE: Page_137]

that leaves of tomato plants grown at low N availability were about 2.5 times more [SOURCE: Page_137]

susceptible to primary lesion formation by B. cinerea compared to plant grown at high N [SOURCE: Page_137]

availability. In sweet pepper results indicate that the increasing nitrate in nutrient solution [SOURCE: Page_137]

136 [SOURCE: Page_137]

positively affected vegetative growth of the plants and also decreased the susceptibility [SOURCE: Page_138]

against B. cinerea (Yoon et al. 2010). [SOURCE: Page_138]

Several histological studies have been devoted to the interaction between B. cinerea [SOURCE: Page_138]

and different host plants such as faba bean leaves (Cole et al. 1998), wax flowers (Dinh et al. [SOURCE: Page_138]

2011), nectarines and plum (Fourie and Holz 1995), lily leaves (Hsieh et al. 2001), grapes [SOURCE: Page_138]

(Pezet and Pont 1992), rose flowers (Pie and Deleeuw 1991), tomato plants (Rijkenberg et al. [SOURCE: Page_138]

1980), gerbera flowers (Salinas and Verhoeff 1995), Arabidopsis (van Baarlen et al. 2007). [SOURCE: Page_138]

They all showed that the mycelium of B. cinerea developed intercellularly and it propagates [SOURCE: Page_138]

by degrading cell walls. In tomato Botrytis patho-system we previously demonstrated that the [SOURCE: Page_138]

conidia germinated on inoculated petioles giving raise to mycelium that attained rapidly stem [SOURCE: Page_138]

tissues 72 hours post inoculation (Ajouz et al. 2011). But histological data on the initial [SOURCE: Page_138]

interaction and colonisation of tomato stem grown with different levels of N fertilization after [SOURCE: Page_138]

inoculation with B. cinerea and on the efficacy of biological control is largely lacking. A [SOURCE: Page_138]

thorough knowledge of the histology of the interaction between tomato, N fertilization, B. [SOURCE: Page_138]

cinerea and antagonists can provide useful information for the efficacy of biological control [SOURCE: Page_138]

agents against this pathogen. [SOURCE: Page_138]

The objective of the present study was to examine the effects of N fertilization of the [SOURCE: Page_138]

tomato plants on: (1) B. cinera mycelim development (2) the efficacy of biological control by [SOURCE: Page_138]

using T. atroviride and M. dimerum through cyto-histological techniques by means of light [SOURCE: Page_138]

microscopy. [SOURCE: Page_138]

137 [SOURCE: Page_138]

Materials and methods [SOURCE: Page_139]

Production of plant material and nitrogen treatments [SOURCE: Page_139]

The used tomato (Solanum lycopersicum, cv Swanson) plants were produced as previously [SOURCE: Page_139]

described by (Lecompte et al. 2010) and maintained under greenhouse conditions until used [SOURCE: Page_139]

for experimentations. During this period, the plants were fertigated twice a day with a [SOURCE: Page_139]

standard commercial nutrient solution (Duclos International Lunel, France). After this period, [SOURCE: Page_139]

the plants (bearing 3-4 leaves) were transplanted in 2 L pots filled with a mixture (1:1, V/V) [SOURCE: Page_139]

of vermiculate and pozzolana (inert crushed volcanic rock) to start the nutrition treatments. [SOURCE: Page_139]

Three levels of N fertilization concentrations were tested in the fertilization solution: 0.5, 5, [SOURCE: Page_139]

and 20 mM. L-1 NO$_3$- using a drip irrigation system (one dripper per pot) up to 6 times a day [SOURCE: Page_139]

depending on the climatic demand, with one minute pulses. For each N level, sixty plants [SOURCE: Page_139]

were used. The pH of the nutrient solution was adjusted to 6 in each treatment by addition of [SOURCE: Page_139]

H2SO$_4$. To evaluate the climatic demand for irrigation adjustment three pots chosen at [SOURCE: Page_139]

random were weighted continuously to evaluate their loss of water, thus the climatic demand [SOURCE: Page_139]

in the green house. [SOURCE: Page_139]

Inoculum production [SOURCE: Page_139]

Strain BC$_1$ of B. cinerea was used in this study. From a previous work of our laboratory, [SOURCE: Page_139]

BC$_1$ was shown to have a high level of aggressiveness on tomato (Ajouz et al. 2010). The [SOURCE: Page_139]

strain was grown on PDA (Potato Dextrose Agar Medium) (Difco, Detroit, USA) in a growth [SOURCE: Page_139]

chamber (21°C, 14h photoperiod). For two BCAs T. atroviride strains T$_1$ and M. dimerum [SOURCE: Page_139]

L13 were tested in this study. They were selected for (1) their known protective effects of [SOURCE: Page_139]

leaf pruning wounds on tomatoes against B. cinerea and (2) their difference in their presumed [SOURCE: Page_139]

mode of action: nutrient competition for M. dimerum (Nicot et al. 2003a) and antibiosis for T. [SOURCE: Page_139]

atroviride (Dipietro 1995; Elad et al. 1999a). Both strains of BCAs were grown on PDA [SOURCE: Page_139]

138 [SOURCE: Page_139]

medium. For BCAs and B. cinerea spores were collected in sterile distilled water from the [SOURCE: Page_140]

surface of 14 day-old cultures. Each suspension was filtered through a 30 µm mesh sterile [SOURCE: Page_140]

filter to remove mycelium fragments and adjusted to the desired concentrations using a [SOURCE: Page_140]

haemocytometer. For inoculation of B. cinerea on tomato plants the inoculum as spore [SOURCE: Page_140]

suspension was adjusted to 106 spores. mL-1 and for the two BCAs tested the suspensions [SOURCE: Page_140]

were adjusted to 107 spores.mL-1. [SOURCE: Page_140]

Inoculations [SOURCE: Page_140]

In these experiments, set of 15 plants were randomly chosen from each N treatment and were [SOURCE: Page_140]

inoculated with a virulent strain of B. cinerea (BC 1). For each plant, four leaves were [SOURCE: Page_140]

excised conserving 5-10 mm petioles that were inoculated with 10 µL of spore suspension. [SOURCE: Page_140]

The BCAs were applied five minutes after the inoculation with B. cinerea.              On each [SOURCE: Page_140]

inoculated pruning wound, 10 µL of spore suspension of either M. dimerum or T. atroviride [SOURCE: Page_140]

were deposited. For each set of 15 inoculated plants per fertilization regime, 5 plants were [SOURCE: Page_140]

used as control (inoculated with sterile distilled water) and 10 plants were further inoculated [SOURCE: Page_140]

with BCAs (5 plants with M. dimerum and 5 with T. atroviride). [SOURCE: Page_140]

Incubation and disease assessment [SOURCE: Page_140]

Following inoculation, the plants were transferred to a growth chamber in conditions [SOURCE: Page_140]

conducive to disease development (21°C, RH above 85%) with a 14 h photoperiod. During [SOURCE: Page_140]

this period, the plants were irrigated manually using the same fertilization solutions as those [SOURCE: Page_140]

used before inoculation. Each inoculated wound was examined daily, between day 3 and day [SOURCE: Page_140]

7 after inoculation to evaluate infection level of the petiole stubs by B. cinerea and subsequent [SOURCE: Page_140]

development of stem lesions. The incidence of stem lesions and the length of developing [SOURCE: Page_140]

cankers (mm) were recorded daily for all treatments. The area under the disease progress [SOURCE: Page_140]

139 [SOURCE: Page_140]

curve (AUDPC) was computed as described by (Lecompte et al. 2010) during the period from [SOURCE: Page_141]

the third to the seventh day after inoculation. [SOURCE: Page_141]

Data analysis [SOURCE: Page_141]

Significant differences between treatments were assessed by analysis of variance. When [SOURCE: Page_141]

significantly different, results from different treatments were treated separately. The AUDPC [SOURCE: Page_141]

(computed between day 3 and 7) on day 7 after inoculation of B. cinerea was analysed. [SOURCE: Page_141]

ANOVA module of statistica software was used to analyse all the data. [SOURCE: Page_141]

Cyto-histology [SOURCE: Page_141]

Petiole samples were excised from five tomato plants from each treatment 48 h after [SOURCE: Page_141]

inoculation (HAI) and structurally compared to healthy petioles. We have previously showed [SOURCE: Page_141]

that 48 HAI Botrytis mycelium is well established toward petiole tissues (Ajouz et al. 2011). [SOURCE: Page_141]

The samples were immersed for 12 h at 4°C in a FAA fixative solution (formaldehyde, glacial [SOURCE: Page_141]

acetic acid, alcohol: 1/1/8: v/v/v). Fixed specimens were rinsed in distilled water, dehydrated [SOURCE: Page_141]

in a graded ethanol series (70–100%) and embedded in methacrylate resin (Technovit Kulzer [SOURCE: Page_141]

7100, Heraeus Kulzer, Wehrheim, Germany). [SOURCE: Page_141]

The specimens were orientated to obtain longitudinal sections.             After resin [SOURCE: Page_141]

polymerization at room temperature, the blocks were cut into 3 μm thick ribbons using an [SOURCE: Page_141]

automatic retraction microtome (Supercut 2065; Reichert-Jung, Leica Intruments, Nussloch, [SOURCE: Page_141]

Germany) equipped with disposable knives (Histoknife H; Heraeus-Kulzer). Sections were [SOURCE: Page_141]

mounted on microscope slides and stained using the periodic acid Schiff’s reagent (PAS) [SOURCE: Page_141]

procedure to visualize polysaccharides (pink) and naphthol blue black to visualize proteins [SOURCE: Page_141]

(dark blue) (El Maataoui and Pichot 1999). Observations were performed using a Leica DMR [SOURCE: Page_141]

140 [SOURCE: Page_141]

light microscope. Images were captured using a Leica DFC 300 FX digital camera and [SOURCE: Page_142]

analyzed using LAS software (Leica).         At least three samples were analyzed for each [SOURCE: Page_142]

treatment. Attention was paid to spore germination, mycelium structure and progression in [SOURCE: Page_142]

petiole tissues, and cyto-pathological effects. [SOURCE: Page_142]

141 [SOURCE: Page_142]

Results [SOURCE: Page_143]

Effect of N plant fertilization on disease severity [SOURCE: Page_143]

Disease severity assessed by AUDPC seven days after inoculation showed that N fertilization [SOURCE: Page_143]

induced noticeable alterations of Botrytis aggressiveness when inoculated alone on tomato [SOURCE: Page_143]

(Fig. 1). For example, at very low N level (0.5 mM) the obtained AUDPC was ∼123 whereas [SOURCE: Page_143]

at high N level (20mM) the AUDPC was ∼54, i.e. 50% reduction. Similar alterations have [SOURCE: Page_143]

been observed in the case of co-inoculations with BCAs. Both biological control agents [SOURCE: Page_143]

reduced the severity of the disease as compared to control (Botrytis alone) (Fig 1). However [SOURCE: Page_143]

this reduction was nitrogen and species dependent. Indeed, co-inoculation with T. atroviride [SOURCE: Page_143]

produced ∼104, 39 and 34 mm AUDPC at 0.5, 5 and 20 mM N respectively. The AUDPC [SOURCE: Page_143]

obtained with M. dimerum was ∼48, 19 and 5 mm at 0.5, 5 and 20 mM N respectively. At all [SOURCE: Page_143]

N levels ggenerally M. dimerum was more effective as compared to T. atroviride against B. [SOURCE: Page_143]

cinerea for the protection of the pruning wounds on tomato stems. [SOURCE: Page_143]

Fig. 1: Effects of N fertilization on disease severity and on efficacy of biological control as accessed via AUDPC seven days after inoculation with B. cinerea alone and when co inoculated with T. atroviride or M. dimerum. (See text for explanations) [SOURCE: Page_143]

142 [SOURCE: Page_143]

Cyto-histology [SOURCE: Page_144]

Sections from control samples (water treated) showed that the tissular organisation of tomato [SOURCE: Page_144]

petiole is composed (from exterior to interior) (Fig. 2A) of a protective epidermal layer, a [SOURCE: Page_144]

cortical parenchyma with chloroplasts, a collenchymas exhibiting thick-walled, empty cells [SOURCE: Page_144]

and vascular tissues embedded in a massive medullar parenchyma. At cyto-histological level, [SOURCE: Page_144]

minor alterations have been observed between the tested levels of N. For example, it has been [SOURCE: Page_144]

noted that at low concentrations, the parenchymatous cells showed important starch [SOURCE: Page_144]

accumulation as attested by the presence of numerous, PAS-positive inclusions (Fig. 2B). [SOURCE: Page_144]

Fig. 2: Tissular organization of tomato petiole (control). A, Longitudinal section showing the epidermis (E), the cortical parenchyma (CP), the collenchymas with thick walls (C), the medullar parenchyma (MP) and the vascular tissues (VT). B, Micrograph of the sectioned zone of petiole showing starch accumulation (red granules, arrows) 48 hours after water deposition. Bars = 50 µm, A and B. [SOURCE: Page_144]

143 [SOURCE: Page_144]

Effects of N fertilization on infection by B. cinerea [SOURCE: Page_145]

Sections from petioles inoculated with B. cinerea (BC$_1$) alone showed that spores [SOURCE: Page_145]

germination occurred in all N treatments and initiated a dense network of hyphae invading all [SOURCE: Page_145]

tissues with an intercellular progression due to middle lamella and cell wall lyses (Fig. 3A). [SOURCE: Page_145]

Parenchymatous tissues were more intensively affected as attested by the presence of [SOURCE: Page_145]

numerous cells exhibiting cytological traits characteristic of programmed cell death (Fig. 3B). [SOURCE: Page_145]

Thorough microscopic observations showed that the three tested N levels induced different [SOURCE: Page_145]

alterations of mycelium structure and development. Indeed, sections of petioles from 0.5 mM [SOURCE: Page_145]

and 5 mM N-treated tomato plants showed that the hyphal cells exhibited normally structured, [SOURCE: Page_145]

multinucleate, protein-rich cytoplasm with small vacuoles (Fig. 3B). In contrast, sections of [SOURCE: Page_145]

petioles taken from 20 mM treated plants showed profound alterations in the hyphal cell [SOURCE: Page_145]

structure. These consisted of the presence of large vacuoles and prominent PAS-positive [SOURCE: Page_145]

granules corresponding to glycogen accumulation (Fig. 3C). [SOURCE: Page_145]

144 [SOURCE: Page_145]

Fig. 3: Effects N fertilization on the colonization of tomato petiole tissues by B. cinerea [SOURCE: Page_146]

alone. A, Section of a petiole taken from 0.5 mM treated plants showing numerous hyphae [SOURCE: Page_146]

(arrows) that developed intercellularly and colonized all tissues particularly the medullar [SOURCE: Page_146]

parenchyma (MP) by degrading the middle lamella. B, High magnification showing well [SOURCE: Page_146]

structured mycelial cell compartments (arrows) and programmed cell death of host cells [SOURCE: Page_146]

(stars) in the presence of 5mM N. C, Section of a petiole taken from 20 mM treated plants [SOURCE: Page_146]

showing cytological alterations of mycelial cell compartments. Note the presence of [SOURCE: Page_146]

numerous vacuoles and glycogen accumulation (red granules). CW = cell wall, IS = [SOURCE: Page_146]

intercellular space. Bars = 50 A, B and C. [SOURCE: Page_146]

145 [SOURCE: Page_146]

Effects of N fertilization on the interaction between M. dimerum and B. cinerea [SOURCE: Page_147]

Petioles co-inoculated with B. cinerea and M. dimerum strain (L13) showed that [SOURCE: Page_147]

independently of nitrogen level both fungi (pathogen and BCA) developed mycelium [SOURCE: Page_147]

intercellularly within the tissues (Fig. 4A). The mycelium of both fungi is easily recognized: [SOURCE: Page_147]

multinucleated, large compartments for B. cinerea vs bi nucleated, thin compartments for M. [SOURCE: Page_147]

dimerum (not shown). However it was noted that Botrytis mycelium exhibited cytological [SOURCE: Page_147]

features indicating antagonistic interaction with L 13.     Indeed, in all observed samples [SOURCE: Page_147]

mycelial cells exhibited highly vacuolated cytoplasm with glycogen deposition. The extent of [SOURCE: Page_147]

these alterations appeared to be nitrogen dependent. For example they were moderated with [SOURCE: Page_147]

0.5 and 5 mM N (Fig. 4B) and intensive with 20 mM N leading to mycelium degeneration [SOURCE: Page_147]

(Fig. 4C). In this case, mycelial compartment (cells) loosed their organelles except the [SOURCE: Page_147]

cellulosic cell walls (Fig. 4D) that exhibited a loosened appearance and desegregation in [SOURCE: Page_147]

places. Additionally, it was observed that mycelial compartments were shorter than BC$_1$ [SOURCE: Page_147]

alone suggesting an initiation of chlamydospore formation (Fig. 4D). In some samples it was [SOURCE: Page_147]

also noted the formation of conidia typical of the genus Microdochium (Fig. 4D). [SOURCE: Page_147]

146 [SOURCE: Page_147]

Fig. 4: Cyto-histological aspects of the effects of N fertilization on the interaction between B. [SOURCE: Page_148]

cinerea and M. dimerum. A, Section of a petiole co-inoculated with spores of the two fungi [SOURCE: Page_148]

showing that mycelial development took place concomitantly: large compartments for B. [SOURCE: Page_148]

cinerea (arrows), uni-nucleated, thin compartments for M. dimerum (arrowheads). B, Section [SOURCE: Page_148]

from 5 mM N treated plant illustrating the degeneration of B. cinerea mycelium (arrows). [SOURCE: Page_148]

Note the presence of glycogen (red granules) and vacuoles in the mycelial compartments. [SOURCE: Page_148]

Arrowheads indicate the M. dimerum hyphae. C, In the presence of 20 mM N Botrytis [SOURCE: Page_148]

mycelium (arrows) exhibited intense vacuolization and cytoplasm condensation as a prelude [SOURCE: Page_148]

of death (inset). D, Micrograph showing abundant, crescents-like conidia of M. dimerum in [SOURCE: Page_148]

petiole tissues (arrows). Bars = 50 A, B, C and D. [SOURCE: Page_148]

147 [SOURCE: Page_148]

Effects of N fertilization on the interaction between T. atroviride and B. cinerea [SOURCE: Page_149]

Co-inoculation with T$_1$ strain of T. atroviride resulted in cytological disruption of Botrytis [SOURCE: Page_149]

hyphal cells the extent of which was nitrogen-dependant. Microscopic observations showed [SOURCE: Page_149]

that T$_1$ mycelium developed with the three levels of N fertilization inducing vacuolisation, [SOURCE: Page_149]

glycogen accumulation and apical growth arrest of the pathogen mycelium (Fig. 5A and B). [SOURCE: Page_149]

With all three N levels used it was observed that hyphae of Trichoderma establish close [SOURCE: Page_149]

contact with Botrytis hyphae suggesting a hyper parasitic behaviour (Fig. 5C). Here again the [SOURCE: Page_149]

intensity of antagonistic effect appeared to be modulated by nitrogen levels (compare Figs. [SOURCE: Page_149]

5A and B). Increasing the N concentration resulted in an increase of T$_1$ effectiveness. It was [SOURCE: Page_149]

also observed that Trichoderma sporulates in plant tissues particularly in xylem vessels (Fig. [SOURCE: Page_149]

## 5D).

148 [SOURCE: Page_149]

Fig. 5: Cyto-histological interaction between B. cinerea and T. atroviride T$_1$ according to N [SOURCE: Page_150]

fertilization. A, Section of a petiole from 0.5 mM N treatment showing the development of [SOURCE: Page_150]

both B. cinerea (arrows) and T. atroviride (arrowheads) mycelia. Note the vacuolated aspect [SOURCE: Page_150]

of Botrytis hyphae. B, Section from 5 mM N treated plant showing cytological alteration of B. [SOURCE: Page_150]

cinerea mycelium (arrows) in the presence of Trichoderma (arrowheads). Vacuolization and [SOURCE: Page_150]

hyphal cell destruction are evident and more pronounced with 20 mM N fertilization (B, [SOURCE: Page_150]

inset). C, Section showing Trichoderma hyphae in close contact with Botrytis mycelium [SOURCE: Page_150]

illustrating hyper parasitism (arrow). D, Micrograph showing sporulation Trichoderma in [SOURCE: Page_150]

xylem vessels (stars). Bars = 50 A, B, C and D. [SOURCE: Page_150]

149 [SOURCE: Page_150]

Discussion [SOURCE: Page_151]

The results of this study demonstrate that the over all severity of disease (AUDPC) was [SOURCE: Page_151]

consistently decreased with increasing N fertilization. Similarly, it also enhanced the efficacy [SOURCE: Page_151]

of the two BCAs tested (T. atroviride and M. dimerum) against B. cinerea in tomato. Our [SOURCE: Page_151]

results are in confirmation with the earlier results showing that high N fertilization reduced [SOURCE: Page_151]

the disease severity of tomato against B. cinerea in planta (Hoffland et al 1999, Lecompte et [SOURCE: Page_151]

al 2010). In addition we recently reported that (Lecompte et al. 2010) in-vitro conditions high [SOURCE: Page_151]

N fertilization decreases the mycelial growth of B. cinerea. Our cytological observations [SOURCE: Page_151]

clearly showed that when B. cinera is inoculated alone high N fertilization levels induced [SOURCE: Page_151]

profound hyphal cell destructions that provide evidence of the observed infection arrest. [SOURCE: Page_151]

Concerning effects of N fertilization on efficacy of biological control our results also [SOURCE: Page_151]

show that high N also enhances the efficacy of the two BCAs against B. cinerea. Mineral [SOURCE: Page_151]

nutrients are one possible factor that can be manipulated to enhance efficacy of biological [SOURCE: Page_151]

control. A wide range of supplements were used previously to enhance the efficacy of [SOURCE: Page_151]

biological control agents against various plant pathogens (Cabrefiga et al. 2011; Cao et al. [SOURCE: Page_151]

2008; Conway et al. 2004; Dawson-Andoh and Lovell 2000; Droby 2001).                 Increasing [SOURCE: Page_151]

concentrations of CaCl$_2$ resulted in decreased spore germination and germ-tube growth of B. [SOURCE: Page_151]

cinerea. The biological control activity of Candida oleophila against B. cinerea was also [SOURCE: Page_151]

enhanced with increasing CaCl$_2$ concentrations (Wisniewski et al. 1995). [SOURCE: Page_151]

The effectiveness of M. dimerum (strain L 13) to protect pruning wounds of tomato at [SOURCE: Page_151]

the crop level against B. cinerea has been demonstrated in earlier studies (Bardin et al. 2008; [SOURCE: Page_151]

Decognet et al. 1999b). But the involved mode of action has not been well documented. [SOURCE: Page_151]

Since no hyper parasitism vis-à-vis B. cinerea and no toxin production have been observed it [SOURCE: Page_151]

was hypothesized that nutrient competition and pH modification could be involved. Our [SOURCE: Page_151]

150 [SOURCE: Page_151]

results indicate that the extant of M. dimerum effectiveness against B. cinerea is modulated by [SOURCE: Page_152]

N fertilization of tomato. As possible mechanisms to explain this phenomenon we could [SOURCE: Page_152]

hypothesize that high N fertilization has: (1) a direct effect on the pathogen development by [SOURCE: Page_152]

reducing the growth and aggressiveness. (2) a direct effect on antagonistic activity of BCAs [SOURCE: Page_152]

by increasing its growth and development (3) indirect effect on the defense of the plant. [SOURCE: Page_152]

Concerning nutrient competition, B. cinerea needs an exogenous carbohydrate source [SOURCE: Page_152]

(such as glucose and fructose) for spore germinations (Blakeman, 1975). This nutritional [SOURCE: Page_152]

dependence suggests that microorganisms that use nutrients efficiently may represent good [SOURCE: Page_152]

competitors against this pathogen (Elad and Stewart 2004).         The cytohistological study [SOURCE: Page_152]

performed here clearly shows that in the presence of M. dimerum, the mycelium of B. cinerea [SOURCE: Page_152]

exhibited cytological alterations and developmental failures (cell wall desegregation and [SOURCE: Page_152]

rupture). These antagonistic effects could be attributed to both nutrient deprivation and pH [SOURCE: Page_152]

alkalinization or both. These observations suggest that the antagonistic effect of L 13 is more [SOURCE: Page_152]

efficient with high N fertilization levels probably due to the negative effects of high N [SOURCE: Page_152]

fertilization on the mycelium development of B. cinerea. [SOURCE: Page_152]

In the case of B. cinerea-tomato pathosystem we have already reported that high N [SOURCE: Page_152]

fertilization reduced the severity of disease (Lecompte et al. 2010). Here we additionally [SOURCE: Page_152]

show that the high N plant nutrition also enhance the effectiveness of BCAs. Here we [SOURCE: Page_152]

hypothesized that L13 hinders the mycelial development of B.cinerea probably by nutrient [SOURCE: Page_152]

competition. M. dimerum seems to be capable of metabolizing the nutrient compounds faster [SOURCE: Page_152]

than pathogen thus interfering with mycelial development of the pathogen. Another putative [SOURCE: Page_152]

hypothesis is that the alkalinization of the medium which is a consequence of the metabolism [SOURCE: Page_152]

of M. dimerum could affect the pathogenicity of B. cinerea and thus ensure better protection [SOURCE: Page_152]

of pruning wounds. Indeed the activity of enzymes responsible for pathogenicity of B. [SOURCE: Page_152]

cinerea, such as endopolygalacturonases that degrade pectin in cell walls is even lower than [SOURCE: Page_152]

151 [SOURCE: Page_152]

the pH tends to be basic (Prins et al. 2000). The increasing of pH in the presence of M. [SOURCE: Page_153]

dimerum could therefore hinder the progress of B. cinerea in tissues of the host plant and also [SOURCE: Page_153]

increase the efficacy of M. dimerum against B. cinerea. [SOURCE: Page_153]

Our results also indicate that T. atroviride exhibited an inhibitory effect against B. [SOURCE: Page_153]

cinerea at high N fertilization in planta. The interaction between the antagonistic activity of [SOURCE: Page_153]

Trichoderma spp. against plant pathogens and mineral nutrition has been studied previously [SOURCE: Page_153]

in in vitro conditions. For example (Khattabi et al. 2004) reported that the antagonistic [SOURCE: Page_153]

activity of T .harzianum against Sclerotium rolfsii was enhanced by increasing nitrate levels. [SOURCE: Page_153]

Similarly (Duzniewska 2008) showed that the increasing levels of mineral nutrients, including [SOURCE: Page_153]

nitrogen, resulted in significant increase of the growth rate of T. harzianum mycelium and [SOURCE: Page_153]

favorably affects the antagonism of Trichoderma against B. cinerea. This suggests that high [SOURCE: Page_153]

N levels increase the ability of the T. atroviride to inhibit the development of the pathogen. It [SOURCE: Page_153]

is generally thought that Trichoderma interfere with phytopathogenic fungi via an antibiosis [SOURCE: Page_153]

mechanism (Dipietro et al. 1993; Schirmbock et al. 1994). Some studies have shown that [SOURCE: Page_153]

Trichoderma spp. gave promised results as biological control agents against B. cinerea for the [SOURCE: Page_153]

protection of pruning wounds of tomato (Elad et al. 2004). The microscopic observations of [SOURCE: Page_153]

the present study suggest that this effect may be due to cytological alterations of the mycelial [SOURCE: Page_153]

cells. These alterations could be attributed to the lytic effect of antibiotic substances secreted [SOURCE: Page_153]

by T. atroviride (Schirmbock et al. 1994) leading to the arrest of mycelium development. The [SOURCE: Page_153]

fact that high N fertilization increases the effectiveness of T. atroviride may reflect the direct [SOURCE: Page_153]

effect of N on B. cinerea mycelium. [SOURCE: Page_153]

In conclusion this study showed that at cyto-histological levels high N fertilization [SOURCE: Page_153]

greatly influences B. cinerea mycelial development in tomato tissues. It is also enhanced the [SOURCE: Page_153]

efficacy of fungal biological control agents. Thus the manipulation of N fertilization makes it [SOURCE: Page_153]

possible not only to reduce the disease severity but also improve the efficacy of biological [SOURCE: Page_153]

152 [SOURCE: Page_153]

control against B. cinerea in green house tomato. To our knowledge, this is the first in planta [SOURCE: Page_154]

observation of the interaction between N fertilization and biological control agents with B. [SOURCE: Page_154]

cinerea. [SOURCE: Page_154]
