159 [SOURCE: Page_160]

General discussion [SOURCE: Page_161]

Mineral nutrients are routinely used to increase crop yield and improve overall plant [SOURCE: Page_161]

health and quality. For sustainable eco-system and improved production efficiency their [SOURCE: Page_161]

judicious use is very critical. Nutrients are important for growth and development of plants [SOURCE: Page_161]

and also for microorganisms, and they are important factors in disease control. All the [SOURCE: Page_161]

nutrients can affect disease severity (Huber 1999). However, there is no general rule, as a [SOURCE: Page_161]

particular nutrient can decrease the severity of one disease but at the same time can also [SOURCE: Page_161]

increase the severity of other disease or have a completely opposite effect in a different [SOURCE: Page_161]

environment (Huber 1980). The nutritional status of a plant has a major impact on disease [SOURCE: Page_161]

susceptibility, and this has been exploited for suppressing a variety of diseases (Engelhard, [SOURCE: Page_161]

1989). [SOURCE: Page_161]

In all mineral elements nitrogen is by far the most extensively reported element [SOURCE: Page_161]

affecting plant disease. Several results at field and green house experiments level suggest that [SOURCE: Page_161]

high N fertilization can either reduce or increase disease development depending upon the [SOURCE: Page_161]

host crop and the pathogen involved. Some times for the same pathogen effect of N is [SOURCE: Page_161]

differentially affected by different host crop. For example, Pitchay et al (2007) have reported [SOURCE: Page_161]

a much higher incidence of B. cinerea lesions in the begonia at high fertilization. But in the [SOURCE: Page_161]

tomato, it has been consistently reported a negative relationship between high N nutrition and [SOURCE: Page_161]

susceptibility to B. cinerea (Lecompte et al 2010). [SOURCE: Page_161]

During the 1st part of this study we have tested the effect of different levels of N [SOURCE: Page_161]

fertilization on the susceptibility of tomato to six strains of B. cinerea widely different in their [SOURCE: Page_161]

aggressiveness (three are mildly aggressive and three are highly aggressive). The results of [SOURCE: Page_161]

this study show that over all high N fertilization between 10-15 mM reduced the susceptibility [SOURCE: Page_161]

of tomato to B. cinerea, for all six strains high N decreased the severity of disease. However, [SOURCE: Page_161]

160 [SOURCE: Page_161]

we have found that the contrasted effects between the six strains of B. cinerea tested. For [SOURCE: Page_162]

more aggressive strains high N fertilization reduced the lesion expansion on the tomato stems, [SOURCE: Page_162]

in contrast for mildly aggressive strains high N fertilization slightly increase the daily lesion [SOURCE: Page_162]

expansion. [SOURCE: Page_162]

Nutritional status of the host plant can affect the response of the pathogen. Contrasted [SOURCE: Page_162]

intra-specific pathogen responses to plant nutritional status has already been reported in other [SOURCE: Page_162]

pathosystems. However, the correlation between nutritional status of the host plants and plant [SOURCE: Page_162]

susceptibility for the various strains tested, always either positive or negative, but not opposite [SOURCE: Page_162]

as we have observed here. For example, when different isolates of Blumeria (Erysiphe [SOURCE: Page_162]

graminis) was tested on barley the infection efficiency of the pathogen was mostly dependent [SOURCE: Page_162]

on the isolates used, it was 2.5 to 7.8 times higher at 240 mg N per plant compared to 30 mg [SOURCE: Page_162]

N per plant (Jensen and Munk, 1997). In other study the inhibitory effect of calcium on B. [SOURCE: Page_162]

cinerea has also been shown to depend on the isolate used, but high calcium levels always [SOURCE: Page_162]

decreased the fungal growth of a pathogen (Chardonnet et al., 2000). [SOURCE: Page_162]

It has been suggested that high N fertilization increases host susceptibility to [SOURCE: Page_162]

biotrophic fungi and decreases that of necrotrophic fungi. To explain this phenomenon one [SOURCE: Page_162]

explanation could be that biotrophic pathogens rely mostly on the nutrient content of the [SOURCE: Page_162]

apoplast, or on nutrient transfer from the host cells by a haustorium, while necrotrophic fungi [SOURCE: Page_162]

that kill cells have a better access to the host nutrients (Divon and Fluhr, 2007; Solomon et al., [SOURCE: Page_162]

2003). In our studies we have also shown that in vitro conditions the strains responded [SOURCE: Page_162]

positively, and similarly, to the N content of the medium used, at least up to a concentration [SOURCE: Page_162]

of 2g.L-1 NH4NO$_3$. In this study total N content increased with increasing N in the nutrient [SOURCE: Page_162]

solution, so it could be hypothesized that any repression of fungal growth at the higher host N [SOURCE: Page_162]

content was the result of a better ability of the plant to defend itself against the pathogen. As [SOURCE: Page_162]

earlier studies shows that N play a vital role in the development of plant diseases, because in [SOURCE: Page_162]

161 [SOURCE: Page_162]

all nutrient elements N is required by the plant in the greatest quantity also it is involved in [SOURCE: Page_163]

metabolic processes, and is a component of proteins and metabolic compounds in disease [SOURCE: Page_163]

defence. It is also consistent with reports that a high N nutrition can lead to production by the [SOURCE: Page_163]

host of higher levels of some constitutive and induced defence molecules (Dietrich et al., [SOURCE: Page_163]

2004; Stout et al., 1998). The different responses of our strains to the host N status could be [SOURCE: Page_163]

explained by the fact that infection strategies of B. cinerea (types and levels of secreted fungal [SOURCE: Page_163]

toxins, cell wall degrading enzymes or oxalic acid) are strain-dependant (Choquer et al., 2007; [SOURCE: Page_163]

Siewers et al., 2005). So here we thus conclude that balanced N fertilization can provide [SOURCE: Page_163]

opportunities to fertilize the crops not only to maximise the over all yield and quality of the [SOURCE: Page_163]

produce but also N could be use as a cultural control of many plant diseases in an indirect [SOURCE: Page_163]

way. In case of tomato-Botrytis pathosystem high N fertilization reduced the over all disease [SOURCE: Page_163]

severity for all isolates. [SOURCE: Page_163]

In second part of this study we have studies the effect of five different levels of N [SOURCE: Page_163]

fertilization of the host plant influences production and aggressiveness of B. cinerea [SOURCE: Page_163]

secondary inoculum. We already know that N fertilization can influence the susceptibility of [SOURCE: Page_163]

plants to B. cinerea but there is lack of studies on the possible effects on sporulation of the [SOURCE: Page_163]

pathogen and on the aggressiveness of resulting secondary inoculum.               Sporulation is [SOURCE: Page_163]

important for plant pathologists since spores are the major vehicle for the dissemination of [SOURCE: Page_163]

fungal diseases (Dahlberg K R 1982). Abundance of fungal sporulation plays a key role in [SOURCE: Page_163]

the development of epidemics, because long-distance dispersal in the air is an important [SOURCE: Page_163]

survival strategy for many fungi, enabling them to colonize new territory rapidly or to migrate [SOURCE: Page_163]

between summer and winter habitats. It is especially relevant for fungi pathogenic on crop [SOURCE: Page_163]

plants, because wind dispersal of their spores for hundreds or thousands of kilometres has [SOURCE: Page_163]

caused the spread of several important diseases on a continental or global scale and allows the [SOURCE: Page_163]

regular reestablishment of diseases in regions where the climate is seasonally unfavourable. [SOURCE: Page_163]

162 [SOURCE: Page_163]

Keeping in view the importance of the sporulation of B. cinerea, this study showed [SOURCE: Page_164]

that level of N fertilization of tomato plants had a highly significant impact on the amount of [SOURCE: Page_164]

spores produced by B. cinerea on diseased tissue as well as on the pathogenicity of this [SOURCE: Page_164]

secondary inoculum.      Sporulation was also affected by the type of plant tissue used as [SOURCE: Page_164]

substrate.   Overall sporulation by two strains of B. cinerea decreased significantly with [SOURCE: Page_164]

increasing nitrate up to 15-30 mM. Aggressiveness of the spores was significantly influenced [SOURCE: Page_164]

by the nutritional status of their production substrate. Disease severity was highest with [SOURCE: Page_164]

spores produced on plants with very low or very high N fertilization (0.5 or 30 mM nitrate). [SOURCE: Page_164]

It was lowest for inoculum from plants with moderate levels of N fertilization. [SOURCE: Page_164]

So our results are in confirmation with the earlier studies which showed that [SOURCE: Page_164]

composition of substrate can affect the sporulation of B. cinerea and other fungi (Gao and [SOURCE: Page_164]

Liu, 2010; Nicot et al., 1996), few studies have addressed the effect of plant fertilization on [SOURCE: Page_164]

the production of secondary inoculum on diseased tissue for example, the disease severity and [SOURCE: Page_164]

spore production of Blumeria (syn. Erysiphe) graminis (powdery mildew of barley ) were [SOURCE: Page_164]

increased with increase with increasing N fertilization (Bainbridge, 1974; Jensen and Munk, [SOURCE: Page_164]

1997), similarly in other study spore production by Oidium neolycopersici on tomato leaves [SOURCE: Page_164]

(Hoffland et al., 2000b) and uredospore production per leaf lesion by Puccinia triticina on [SOURCE: Page_164]

wheat was also increased with increasing N fertilization (Robert et al., 2004). In contrast, our [SOURCE: Page_164]

study of B. cinerea on tomato shows that spore production was generally lowest with high N [SOURCE: Page_164]

fertilization.   Overall, this supports the hypothesis that biotrophs (such as the rusts and [SOURCE: Page_164]

powdery mildews) and necrotophs (such as B. cinerea) may be differentially affected by N [SOURCE: Page_164]

fertilization (Hoffland et al., 2000b; Solomon et al., 2003). However, the plant species might [SOURCE: Page_164]

also influence this since increasing N from 1.8 to 28.8 mM in the irrigation solution of basil [SOURCE: Page_164]

plants increased the incidence of sporulation by B. cinerea on stem segments (from nearly [SOURCE: Page_164]

zero to over 75%) three days after inoculation (Yermiyahu et al., 2006). [SOURCE: Page_164]

163 [SOURCE: Page_164]

In summary, the apparent contradictory effects of N fertilization on the sporulation of [SOURCE: Page_165]

different fungi we can hypothesized that the N fertilization affects each fungus differentially [SOURCE: Page_165]

because every fungus has different resource requirements for growth and development. [SOURCE: Page_165]

However effect of each plant species can not be ruled out as in some studies the same [SOURCE: Page_165]

pathogen act differentially according to host plant attacked. We conclude that the effect of N [SOURCE: Page_165]

fertilization on fungal sporulation is highly pathogen-host specific and is probably dependent [SOURCE: Page_165]

on differences in resource requirements of the pathogen or the sensitivity of the pathogen to [SOURCE: Page_165]

plant resistance reactions or on both these factors. [SOURCE: Page_165]

In the 3rd part of the present study we examined the effect of five levels of N nutrition on the [SOURCE: Page_165]

efficacy of two biocontrol agents (Trichoderma atroviride and Microdochium dimerum) to [SOURCE: Page_165]

protect pruning wounds of tomato against B. cinerea. Although N fertilization is known to [SOURCE: Page_165]

affect plant susceptibility to certain pathogens including B. cinerea in tomato, little is known [SOURCE: Page_165]

on its possible effect on the efficacy of biological control. Our results showed that tomato [SOURCE: Page_165]

plant N fertilization strongly affected disease development for both strains (BC$_1$ & BC21) of [SOURCE: Page_165]

the B. cinerea and it influenced the efficacy of both biocontrol agents. In control plants [SOURCE: Page_165]

disease severity decreased with increasing N fertilization up to 10 mmol of nitrate per liter of [SOURCE: Page_165]

nutrient solution. In the presence of either BCA (M. dimerum and T. atroviride), the AUDPC [SOURCE: Page_165]

also decreased significantly with increasing N fertilization (up to 5-20 mM). So our results [SOURCE: Page_165]

are in confermation with the earlier esults showing that high N nutrition decreased the [SOURCE: Page_165]

susceptibility of tomato plants to B. cinerea (Lecompte et al., 2010, Hoffland et al., 1999). [SOURCE: Page_165]

In addition high nitrogen fertilization also increased biocontrol efficacy by delaying [SOURCE: Page_165]

symptom development on the stems and slowing down lesion expansion. The efficacy of [SOURCE: Page_165]

biocontrol globally increased with increasing N fertilization; however variations depending on [SOURCE: Page_165]

the strain, the concentration of the pathogen's inoculum and the species of BCA were [SOURCE: Page_165]

observed. M. dimerum provided higher levels of protection than T. atroviride and the efficacy [SOURCE: Page_165]

164 [SOURCE: Page_165]

of either BCA was higher against the mildly aggressive than against the highly aggressive [SOURCE: Page_166]

strain of B. cinerea. The protection index of M. dimerum increased sharply with increasing N [SOURCE: Page_166]

fertilization, to reach nearly 80% and 100%, respectively, against the highly and the mildly [SOURCE: Page_166]

aggressive strains of B. cinerea, while that of T. atroviride increased only moderately. [SOURCE: Page_166]

Few studies have examined the effect of soil fertilization on biocontrol. (Khattabi et [SOURCE: Page_166]

al., 2004) reported that soil amendment with four types of N fertilizers resulted in decreased [SOURCE: Page_166]

survival of sclerotia of soilborne pathogen Sclerotium rolfsii and that the antagonistic activity [SOURCE: Page_166]

of biocontrol fungus T. harzianum against those sclerotia increased with increasing N doses. [SOURCE: Page_166]

N amendments in nutrient agar media have also been shown to increase in vitro biocontrol [SOURCE: Page_166]

activity of certain microorganisms. Thus, even if the major effect of plant N nutrition on [SOURCE: Page_166]

biocontrol efficacy mostly occurred at the early infection phase, marked by a delay in [SOURCE: Page_166]

symptom development, a reduction in lesion expansion was able, in some cases, to contribute [SOURCE: Page_166]

to increased biocontrol at high N level when stems became infected. [SOURCE: Page_166]

Highly significant correlations (P<0.001) were observed between disease severity [SOURCE: Page_166]

(AUDPC) and the concentration in the stems (mg per gram dry matter) of some of the tissue [SOURCE: Page_166]

components: negative correlations with total N, NO$_3$-, and fructose, and positive with sucrose [SOURCE: Page_166]

(Table 3).    Ccorrelations between susceptibility or resistance to diseases and various [SOURCE: Page_166]

indicators of nitrogen status of the host plant (amino acids, nucleic acids, phenols, total N, [SOURCE: Page_166]

protein content, C / N, etc) have been reported before in other plant-pathogen systems (Huber [SOURCE: Page_166]

and Thompson, 2007). Also, a relationship between plant sugar content and susceptibility has [SOURCE: Page_166]

been suggested by Bolton, (2009). Interestingly, no correlation was observed between disease [SOURCE: Page_166]

severity and total soluble sugars, contrarily to the results of Hoffland et al. (1999) with total [SOURCE: Page_166]

soluble carbohydrates, suggesting that other compounds than those measured in our study [SOURCE: Page_166]

(glucose, fructose, sucrose) may be implicated, or that only some of the soluble sugars, [SOURCE: Page_166]

fructose and sucrose, are implicated in the response to infection. The recent description of a [SOURCE: Page_166]

165 [SOURCE: Page_166]

mutant of B. cinerea incapable of using fructose as a sole carbon source (Aguayo et al., 2011) [SOURCE: Page_167]

should provide a useful tool to investigate this subject further. [SOURCE: Page_167]

Competition for nutrients is a common mode of action of biocontrol agents on plant [SOURCE: Page_167]

surfaces. Although no information is available for the two BCAs included in our study, [SOURCE: Page_167]

competition for fructose and nitrate has been identified as a factor in the mode of action of [SOURCE: Page_167]

certain BCAs (Jacobsen, 2006). This indicated that the effects of these components were not [SOURCE: Page_167]

different for plants inoculated with B. cinerea alone or with B. cinerea plus a BCA, and [SOURCE: Page_167]

conflicted with the hypothesis of a direct effect of the plant components on biocontrol. [SOURCE: Page_167]

Rather, the results suggest that the tissue content affected the capacity of the pathogen to [SOURCE: Page_167]

infect the plant, and that the outcome of the biocontrol was in turn affected by this modified [SOURCE: Page_167]

interaction between the host and the pathogen. This hypothesis is in accordance with the [SOURCE: Page_167]

observation that at each N regime, the efficacy of biocontrol was directly related to the [SOURCE: Page_167]

pressure exerted by the pathogen, in terms of inoculum concentration and strain [SOURCE: Page_167]

aggressiveness. Increasing the plant N content would either enhance plant defenses or reduce [SOURCE: Page_167]

the ability of the fungus to colonize the tissue, and by this direct reduction of fungal growth, [SOURCE: Page_167]

indirectly facilitate the action of the BCAs and hence their efficacy. [SOURCE: Page_167]

166 [SOURCE: Page_167]

Conclusions and Perspectives [SOURCE: Page_168]

1: In conclusion, we have shown here that the the tomato plants grown with different regimes [SOURCE: Page_168]

of N fertilization (0.5 to 20 mM), overall disease severity was lower for all isolates on plants [SOURCE: Page_168]

with higher nitrogen inputs, but the effect of host N on Botrytis development depends on the [SOURCE: Page_168]

strain and the concentration of inoculum leading to primary infection. One explanation for [SOURCE: Page_168]

this could be that the fungal development depend both on trophic and defence processes, and [SOURCE: Page_168]

that the availability of nutrients for the pathogen ultimately depends of its ability to explore [SOURCE: Page_168]

new sources of unexplored plant tissues. Upon N scarcity, low substrate availability would [SOURCE: Page_168]

impair growth of moderately aggressive isolates, those against which plant defences are more [SOURCE: Page_168]

efficient, while resources available to aggressive strains would not be limiting, whatever the [SOURCE: Page_168]

nutrition status of the host. More research is needed in order to find how N fertilization [SOURCE: Page_168]

increase or decrese disease tolerance or resistance to different host-pathogen systems, what [SOURCE: Page_168]

the changes are in plant metabolism and how this can be used to control plant disease. In [SOURCE: Page_168]

addition, further research is required to use different isolates of the pathogen on different [SOURCE: Page_168]

patho systems. [SOURCE: Page_168]

2: The nutritional status of the host plant had a significant impact on the abundance and [SOURCE: Page_168]

pathogenicity of secondary inoculum produced by plant pathogenic B. cinerea. This [SOURCE: Page_168]

information could lead to exciting new studies to elucidate cellular mechanisms involved and [SOURCE: Page_168]

suggests that fertilization can influence the development of epidemics on a field scale. [SOURCE: Page_168]

Conversely, it opens the possibility of manipulating crop fertilization not only to reduce plant [SOURCE: Page_168]

susceptibility but also to limit the impact of secondary inoculum and minimize the need for [SOURCE: Page_168]

pesticide use. [SOURCE: Page_168]

3: The results of this study clearly indicated that the level of N fertilization provided to the [SOURCE: Page_168]

plant can influence not only the susceptibility of tomato stems to B. cinerea but also the [SOURCE: Page_168]

167 [SOURCE: Page_168]

efficacy of biocontrol. High N fertilization enhanced the efficacy of both biocontrol agents [SOURCE: Page_169]

tested against B. cinerea.    To our knowledge this is the first report of an effect of N [SOURCE: Page_169]

fertilization on the efficacy of biocontrol against an airborne disease. It suggests that the [SOURCE: Page_169]

manipulation of N fertilization could provide a new useful tool for integrated protection of [SOURCE: Page_169]

greenhouse tomato. To devise specific recommendations for growers, further work is needed [SOURCE: Page_169]

to validate the present results in conditions of commercial production and to evaluate possible [SOURCE: Page_169]

side effects on other diseases or pests in the tomato greenhouse. Thus, the novel results [SOURCE: Page_169]

presented here on the effect of N fertilization on the efficacy of two biocontrol agents against [SOURCE: Page_169]

B. cinerea should stimulate further work to better understand which mechanisms are involved [SOURCE: Page_169]

at the cellular and molecular level. [SOURCE: Page_169]

4: This study showed that at cyto-histological levels high N fertilization greatly influences B. [SOURCE: Page_169]

cinerea mycelial development in tomato tissues. It is also enhanced the efficacy of fungal [SOURCE: Page_169]

biological control agents. Thus the manipulation of N fertilization makes it possible not only [SOURCE: Page_169]

to reduce the disease severity but also improve the efficacy of biological control against B. [SOURCE: Page_169]

cinerea in green house tomato. To our knowledge, this is the first in planta observation of the [SOURCE: Page_169]

interaction between N fertilization and biological control agents with B. cinerea. [SOURCE: Page_169]

5: The main conclusion of our work is that, at least for one of the two necrotrophs in this [SOURCE: Page_169]

study, the effect of N fertilization on susceptibility appeared statistically mediated by the [SOURCE: Page_169]

metabolism of sugars. As sugar accumulation is specific in each plant species, and for a given [SOURCE: Page_169]

species, in its different organs, it might explain why the variation of plant susceptibility to [SOURCE: Page_169]

necrotrophs in response to N fertilization is not unequivocal. Genotypes with contrasted [SOURCE: Page_169]

sugar accumulation should be useful to test a general relationship between specific sugars and [SOURCE: Page_169]

resistance to necrotrophs in lettuce and other species. Clearly more work is needed in this [SOURCE: Page_169]

area and it is, of course, likely those different fungi will respond in different ways to the [SOURCE: Page_169]

application of N fertilization. It is important that future studies combine measurements of [SOURCE: Page_169]

168 [SOURCE: Page_169]

fungal growth and symptom production with plant biomass and in plant nutrient levels. [SOURCE: Page_170]

Further studies are thus required to understand the mechanisms involved at the level of gene [SOURCE: Page_170]

expression. Trophic interactions related to primary metabolism are unlikely to be the only [SOURCE: Page_170]

mechanisms implicated. Other compounds related to plant defense mechanisms were not [SOURCE: Page_170]

examined in our study but would merit further attention as they could influence fungi [SOURCE: Page_170]

indirectly through their effect on plant tissue colonization by the pathogen. [SOURCE: Page_170]

In the end we conclude that in most of the studies reported here clearly shown that optimum [SOURCE: Page_170]

N fertilization of the host plant has decreased the susceptibility of tomato plants to B. cinerea, [SOURCE: Page_170]

their sporulation and aggressiveness and also high N fertilization enhanced the efficacy of [SOURCE: Page_170]

biocontrol agents against this pathogen for the protection of pruning wounds of tomato. This [SOURCE: Page_170]

is probably because N nutrient is involved in the tolerance or resistance mechanism of the [SOURCE: Page_170]

host plant. [SOURCE: Page_170]

In sustainable agriculture balanced nutrition is an essential component of any integrative crop [SOURCE: Page_170]

protection programme because in most cases it is more cost-effective and also [SOURCE: Page_170]

environmentally friendly to control plant disease with the adequate amount of nutrients with [SOURCE: Page_170]

no pesticides. Also, balanced nutrient fertilization can reduce disease to an acceptable level, [SOURCE: Page_170]

or at least to a level at which further control by other cultural practices or conventional [SOURCE: Page_170]

biocides are more successful and less expensive. [SOURCE: Page_170]

169 [SOURCE: Page_170]

181 [SOURCE: Page_182]

Abstract: Nitrogen (N) fertilization is known to influence the susceptibility of many plants to a variety of diseases. In the case of diseases caused by Botrytis cinerea, the role of N fertilization appears to be variable, with high levels either fostering or reducing severity depending on the studies. To test whether this variability could be due to possible differences in the host plants, inoculum pressure or in the behavior of different strains of the pathogen, studies were carried out to investigate the effect of different N fertilization regimes on the susceptibility of tomato and lettuce to six isolates of B. cinerea. Possible epidemiological effects of N fertilization through the sporulation of the pathogen and on the pathogenicity of resulting secondary inoculum were also investigated on tomato. Plants were grown in a soil-less drip- irrigation system. Differential N nutrition ranging from 0.5 to 30 mM NO$_3$- was applied for the last four weeks prior to inoculation on the leaves (lettuce) or on leaf pruning wounds (tomato) and incubation of the plants in conditions conducive to disease development. On the tomato stems, disease onset was delayed and overall severity was lower for all isolates on plants with higher N inputs, regardless of inoculum concentration.         However, the rate of stem lesion expansion was differentially affected depending on the strains, decreasing with increasing N fertilization levels for the more aggressive isolates, while increasing for the less aggressive isolates. In contrast with tomato, high N fertilization increased disease severity on lettuce for all isolates tested. On tomato plant tissue, sporulation of B. cinerea decreased significantly with increasing N fertilization up to 15-30 mM NO$_3$- and the pathogenicity of the spores was significantly influenced by the nutritional status of their production substrate. It was highest for spores produced on plants with very low or very high N fertilization (0.5 or 30 mM NO$_3$-) and lowest for those from plants with moderate levels of N fertilization. Plant fertilization also strongly affected the efficacy of two biocontrol agents (Trichoderma atroviride and Microdochium dimerum) to protect pruning wounds of tomato against B. cinerea. The highest levels of protection were obtained with high N fertilization and related to a delay in symptom development on the stems, sometimes associated with a slowdown in lesion expansion. Histological studies showed that the decrease in disease severity at high N fertilization was associated to structural alteration of Botrytis mycelial cells. In the presence of a biocontrol agent, the effect on the pathogen was further associated to vacuolisation, glycogen deposition and mycelial cell death.      Hypotheses to explain these results are discussed in light of the possible physiological effects of nitrogen fertilization on nutrient availability for the pathogen in the host tissue and of possible production of defense metabolites by the plant. These results also open new possibilities for including the manipulation of N fertilization as a tool for the integrated protection of vegetable crops. [SOURCE: Page_183]
